PART 14: APPEALS
INFORMATION NOTE
What this Part is about: Part 14 [Appeals] governs appeals to the Court
of Appeal.
NOTE
Following the appellate civil Rules we reproduce the criminal appellate
Rules, with all their commentary. (These Rules are enacted by the Judges,
not by any provincial government body. )
Words and phrases used in this Part that have defined meanings in
the Appendix [Definitions] or Rule 14.1 [Definitions] include: appeal;
appellant; applications judge; case management officer; Civil Appeal
Hearing List; commencement document; costs award; court appealed
from; court clerk; decision; defendant; enactment; fast track appeal; file;
judge; judgment; judicial centre; lawyer; litigation representative; Minister;
official court reporter; order; panel; party; plaintiff; pleading; procedural
order; property; record; Registrar; remedy; respondent; rules; standard
appeal; Unscheduled Civil Appeals List.

14-5
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.1(1)
DIVISION 1
THE RIGHT TO APPEAL¹
Subdivision 1
Interpretation and Application
Deﬁnitions
14.1(1) In this Part,
(a) “appeal” means an appeal to the Court of Appeal governed by this
Part;
(b) “appellant” means a person who under an enactment or these rules
(i) ﬁles an application for permission to appeal to the Court of
Appeal, or
(ii) ﬁles a notice of appeal;
(c) “case management ofﬁcer” means a person appointed as a case
management ofﬁcer under the Court of Appeal Act;
(d) “Civil Appeal Hearing List” means the list referred to in rule 14.33(1)
[Scheduling standard appeals] that identiﬁes selected appeals that
have been scheduled for oral argument and the date on which each
oral argument is to be heard;
(e) “court appealed from” means the court, person or tribunal from
which an appeal has been brought;
(e.1) “Court of Appeal Management System” means the system
established by the Court of Appeal for purposes including creating,
issuing, ﬁling, accessing, transmitting, storing, recording, providing
or submitting documents or information by electronic means in
respect of matters under this Part;
(f) “decision” means the whole or any part of the decision of the court,
person or tribunal from which an appeal lies and includes a judgment,
order, decision, verdict, direction, determination or award and, where
the context requires, includes the verdict or ﬁnding of a jury;
(g) “fast track appeal” means an appeal that must be managed in
accordance with rule 14.14 [Fast track appeals];
(h) “ﬁle” means to submit the correct document through the Court of
Appeal Management System or otherwise as permitted in accordance
with the Court of Appeal of Alberta Practice Direction — Electronic

Footnote
1 All the Parts of the present Rules of Court except the appellate and criminal judicial review Rules were
initially drafted by the Alberta Law Reform Institute and its draftsman. Part 14 was not so drafted. For some
years after 2010, the old Civil Court of Appeal Rules remained in force. A committee of the Court of Appeal
did a draft of Part 14, to which the Court of Appeal as a whole made very few changes. The Rules of Court
Committee approved this revised draft, and the Alberta government’s legislative draftsman made a number
of small changes in style. The revised draft of Part 14 was then approved by an Order in Council which was
ﬁled as Alta Reg 41/2014.

R.14.1(2) PART 14: APPEALS

(i) “judge”, when used in reference to the Court of Appeal, includes the
Chief Justice of Alberta, the justices of appeal and the supernumerary
judges of the Court of Appeal;
(j) “panel” means three or more judges of the Court of Appeal unless
the Chief Justice, in case of emergency, declares that two judges
may form a panel;
(k) “party” means a party to an appeal or an application under this Part
and includes an intervenor where the context requires;
(l) “Registrar” means a person appointed as a Registrar of the Court

Registrar of the Court of Appeal and any person designated by a
Registrar or the Chief Justice of Alberta to act for a Registrar;

person who, under an enactment or these rules,
(i) is named as a respondent to an application for permission to

Speaking to the Civil List is abolished, effective April 30, 2023.
(2) Where a respondent ﬁles a notice of cross appeal, unless this Part otherwise
provides, the rules relating to appeals apply to the cross appeal and, in particular,
(a) references to an appeal include a cross appeal;
(b) references to an appellant include a respondent who ﬁles a notice of
cross appeal;
(c) references to a respondent include an appellant who is named as a
respondent in a notice of cross appeal;
(d) references to parties to an appeal include parties to a cross appeal.
[Alta. Reg.41/14;61/23]

14-7
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.2(1)
Though the Rules do not provide for conditional cross-appeals, if the cross-appellant says that
he will not pursue his appeal in certain events, then if those occur the court need not consider
that cross-appeal.¹
Semble municipal taxation legislation as interpreted allows both the taxpayer, and the
municipality to appeal.²
Because paragraph (1) (m) of the Rule deﬁnes “respondent” in terms of who is named in the
notice of appeal, no adversity of interest is necessary to be a respondent.³
A company called itself an “interested non-party” and the notice of appeal said it was “not
party to appeal”. On appeal it ﬁled a factum seeking relief. That switch of status should not have
been attempted, but the order under appeal did affect the legal interests of the company, and had
there been an application to add the company as a true party to the appeal, it would likely have
succeeded.⁴
The word “decision” must be read in context, and so in R.14.48 it must be by a judge. The
deﬁnition in R.14.1 is broad, but another Rule like 14.48 can select a conclusion by only one type
of ofﬁcial.⁵
Application of general rules
14.2(1) Subject to this Part, to any enactment, and to any direction by an appeal
judge, if this Part does not deal with a matter, other Parts of these rules apply to
appeals, with any appropriate modiﬁcations.
(2) Where a rule in this Part provides that a speciﬁc rule in another Part applies
to appeals, the speciﬁc rule applies with any appropriate modiﬁcations.
(3) Rule 13.5(1) [Variation of time periods] does not apply to a time period or
deadline referred to in this Part.
(4) Subject to any appropriate modiﬁcations or any direction by an appeal judge,
when applying the provisions of any other Part to an appeal under this Part,
(a) a reference to the court clerk or a court ofﬁcer is to be read as a
reference to the Registrar;
(b) a reference to a plaintiff is to be read as a reference to an appellant;
(c) a reference to a defendant is to be read as a reference to a respondent;
(d) a reference to the court or a judge are to be read as a reference to an
appeal judge;

Where an Act says when an appeal to the Court of Appeal must be ﬁled and served within a
certain time, the Court cannot extend or waive that time, and the Rules of Court cannot be used
to do that.²
When these rules apply
14.3 If a person has a right to appeal to the Court of Appeal under an enactment
or these rules or is granted permission to appeal to the Court of Appeal, the
appeal must be made and managed in accordance with this Part.

General Appellate Practice
There are textbooks on appeals.³
The English history of appeals is described.⁴
An appeal is from, and turns on the correctness of, the formal order, not the reasons given for
it.⁵
See further R.14.75(2) n. A. That is why lack of reasons does not bar an appeal.⁶
And so the
Court of Appeal need not express any opinion on a topic in the reasons of the court appealed,

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.4(1)

And if the purported appeal is only from certain fact ﬁndings, the
appeal may be quashed.²
The Consolidated Practice Directions of the Alberta Court of Appeal supplement these Rules.³
It does not take a larger panel than three justices to reverse a previous Court of Appeal
precedent. But it does require advance leave from another three-justice panel (though sometimes
the Court of Appeal orders a bigger panel). That leave should be sought before the time to ﬁle
factums. See further, R.14.72 and the note on Precedent near the beginning of this book.

must perform all services as would a competent lawyer, so he or she must become familiar with
the relevant Rules.⁶
If the trial court holds separate hearings on different days about substance and about costs, a
notice of appeal about the former does not cover costs, and an amendment or a late new appeal
would be needed for costs.⁷
A motion for fresh evidence is not the way to challenge a later decision; a formal appeal is
necessary.⁸
Where an argument was not made to an Applications Judge hearing an ex parte
motion, someone dissatisﬁed with the order should move before the Applications Judge to set it
aside, not appeal to a judge.⁹
Subdivision 2
Appeals as of Right
Right to appeal
14.4(1) Except as otherwise provided, an appeal lies to the Court of Appeal from
the whole or any part of a decision of a Court of King's Bench judge sitting in
court or chambers, or the verdict or ﬁnding of a jury.
This subrule applies only to appeals from King's Bench judges. Appeals from one Court of
Appeal judge to a panel of the Court of Appeal are governed by R.14.5.¹⁰

R.14.4(2) PART 14: APPEALS
(2) No appeal is allowed to the Court of Appeal from the dismissal by a Court of
King's Bench judge of an application made without notice.
(3) Where an application has been made to the Court of King's Bench without
notice and has been dismissed, the applicant may reapply
(a) on notice, if the dismissal was for lack of notice, or
(b) by renewal of the application if the dismissal was for reasons other
than the lack of notice.
(4) No appeal is allowed directly to the Court of Appeal from a decision of an
applications judge. [Alta. Reg.41/14;136/22;218/22]
A. Right to Appeal¹
1. Rights Come Only from Legislation
There is no constitutional right to appeal.²
Nor is there any common-law right to appeal; it can
only be given by an express provision in a statute or the Rules of Court.³
Agreement or contract
cannot create a right to appeal.⁴
A court of appeal has no general powers beyond what legislation gives it.⁵
There is a discussion
of how absolute rights of appeal and need for leave to appeal other topics, ﬁt together.⁶
A statutory right of appeal cannot be cut down by a Regulation.⁷
The usual statutory power of
the Court of Appeal to hear appeals cannot be cut down or removed by a court order in a class
action, if that legislation does not cut down that jurisdiction.⁸
In topics given to Parliament under s.91 of the Constitution (B. N. A. ) Act, Parliament can
legislate that court decisions are ﬁnal and not subject to appeal.⁹
Where court decisions on a
certain topic are said by the Act to be “ﬁnal”, that overrides a general statutory right of appeal.¹⁰

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.4(4)
And of course it is even clearer if legislation says there cannot be a (further) appeal.¹
A statutory
bar to appeals (on arbitration’s availability and stays) was construed broadly, as it is a policy
decision not to slow down arbitration with appeals.²
A right to appeal cannot accrue or be accruing until all legislative conditions precedent are
satisﬁed.³
Rule 14.4 gives the right to appeal most often invoked. It is broad, but it is limited to civil
matters,⁴
and the Criminal Code contains a general prohibition on criminal appeals not speciﬁcally
provided for in the Code.⁵
Seeking a declaration or prerogative relief or Charter relief does not
automatically convert a criminal matter into an appealable civil one.⁶
Criminal Appeal Rules cannot
create a right of appeal not given by the Code.⁷
Rule 14.4 excepts situations where legislation otherwise provides. So sometimes an appeal
from a King’s Bench judge needs leave to appeal, especially as statutes prevail over Rules by
R.1.9. So s.48 of the Arbitration Act requires leave to appeal from an arbitration decision.⁸
However, statutes giving a judge or ofﬁcer a power to make an order or direction are presumed
to give him or her that power as a judge or ofﬁcer of the court, and so to be subject to any general
statutory right to appeal from his or her court.⁹
Appeals to the Court of Appeal from a Court of
King's Bench judge acting as persona designata under a provincial statute lie under s.29 of the
Judicature Act, but it requires leave to appeal.¹⁰
On appeal from the Alberta Court of Justice in a civil case, the only appeal lies to Court of
King's Bench, and no further appeal is possible, even if the appellant once tried to transfer the
action to King's Bench.¹¹
The Court of Appeal has no jurisdiction.¹²
There is no direct appeal from

R.14.4(4) PART 14: APPEALS
an arbitrator,¹
nor from an Applications Judge.²
One cannot appeal directly to the Court of Appeal
from an assessment ofﬁcer of the Court of King's Bench; the appeal lies to a King's Bench justice.³
A judge reviewed the narrowness of a number of situations in the Bankruptcy and
Insolvency Act where an appeal to the Court of Appeal as of right is given.⁴
2. Who Can Appeal
(a) Parties
A named party to a lawsuit can appeal to the Court of Appeal from a Court of King's Bench
decision adverse to and affecting him or her, even if he or she took no active part in the Court
of King's Bench, relying upon another party to oppose actively (and which that other party did
oppose).⁵
(b) Non-Parties
A non-party to the proceedings in the tribunal or court below cannot appeal or seek leave to
appeal, even if the point of law in question could well affect him or her.⁶
A mere intervener with
no personal interest cannot appeal.⁷
But where the question is the complainant in a professional
discipline case, the law is not clear, and standing should not be decided summarily.⁸
A party is proper and necessary to an appeal where the issue is an injunction and the proposed
injunction would name and affect that person directly. Or where that person is a party to the
lawsuit appealed from (given the deﬁnition of “respondent” in British Columbia’s Court of Appeal
Act). Or where the injunction was to cover all lawyers in a law ﬁrm, and the person concerned is
one of those lawyers. Who is a party to a class action is not a good analogy.⁹

Gulf Oil Can. (CA Jun 7) [1974] AUD 633. A motion under it to review an arbitration was dismissed on the
merits, in Re Jasper (CA) [1979] 6 WWR 65, 17 AR 32. But then Mijon Hldg. v. Edmonton (City) (CA 1980)
23 AR 215, 12 Alta LR(2d) 88, held that by statute the jurisdiction over arbitrators lay in the King's Bench,
not the appellate court. So old R.507 had nothing to apply to, and the Lieutenant-Governor in Council then
repealed it. Now see the Arbitration Act, RSA 2000, c. A-43: the appeal is to King's Bench (s.44) and then
to the Court of Appeal with leave (s.48). King's Bench had inherent jurisdiction at common law to quash an
arbitration decision? U. N. A. v. Didsbury (1994) 150 AR 81. If a King's Bench judge refuses leave to appeal
from an arbitrator to the King's Bench, that refusal cannot be appealed to the Court of Appeal, with or without
leave of an appellate judge: Sherwin-Williams Co. v. Walls Alive (Edm. ) 2003 ABCA 191, 327 AR 386 (one

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.4(4)
The Class Proceedings Act s.36(1) gives a right to “parties” to appeal to the Court of Appeal.
Someone not a class member is not a party. But they can seek permission to appeal under
subsec. (3) on an issue named in subsec. (1), where the representative plaintiff has no ongoing
appeal. Rule 14.4 gives a right to appeal, but does not say who has the right, and s.36 of the
Class Proceedings Act prevails over the Rule. Generally, only a party can appeal.¹
(c) Standing
Usually people can appeal only when they themselves have standing, a topic best decided
conclusively early.²
Private interest standing requires a direct personal interest, or being directly personally
affected by the order or judgment in question.³
Nor can a non-party appeal over a decision about whether the government must act (tax), even
if the non-party is the one who would be taxed.⁴
Public interest standing requires a real stake or
genuine interest in the case, and a judge of a court who has ruled on a matter has no such stake
or interest. Nor is his appeal a reasonable and effective way to bring the matter before the courts.
Litigation on a complete record is a far better way. All others concerned have ways to get to court,
if anything further occurs. Nor would an early appeal by the Justice of the Alberta Court of Justice
bind those later persons.⁵
An appeal is from the formal order or judgment, not the reasons, so
correcting an error in the reasons is not a ground for a non-party to join an appeal or intervene. He
or she should be directly affected or have some good addition to help the court.⁶
(d) No One Affected
Sometimes in constitutional litigation admittedly no one at present is directly affected, but
hypothetical situations suggest that in future certain other people in certain circumstances may
be affected. The courts sometimes look at that, but it raises a host of difﬁculties.⁷
If no one with an interest is left to litigate, the Information and Privacy Commissioner is
interested, but is not a party to the original dispute, and the Commissioner has no standing to
appeal, being the tribunal being reviewed.⁸
Unless its jurisdiction is in question, a statutory tribunal
cannot appeal an order quashing the tribunal’s decision. It is not a person aggrieved.⁹

14-14
R.14.4(4) PART 14: APPEALS
(e) Formal Order
Generally, one can appeal only if the formal order against one is adverse, and one cannot
appeal from mere reasons for decision, especially if they do not decide the issue.¹
However,
where a judge in the course of a (criminal) case gives declarations of constitutional invalidity of
certain parts of legislation, the Crown can appeal to the Court of Appeal, even though in the ﬁnal
result of the prosecution the Crown wins.²
There is a special power to appeal to the Supreme
Court of Canada on a constitutional issue where there would not otherwise be the right to appeal
to it, e. g. , where the appeal is from a lower court and not a Court of Appeal, or where one lost the
Charter issue but won the suit. But there is no need to invoke that right where one already has an
ordinary channel of appeal to the Supreme Court of Canada.³
A statute giving a right to appeal, but silent as to who can appeal, is no authority on who can
be the appellant.⁴
A vexatious litigant order does not bar an appeal (as of right) from that very order.⁵
3. Appeal from Nullity
If a statute gives an appeal from a class of tribunal, it is no bar to an appeal that the tribunal
was never properly created and does not in law exist; the appeal should be allowed for that
reason.⁶
An appeal is not precluded just because the court appealed from held (correctly) that it
had no jurisdiction.⁷
But the case cannot then be referred back to the tribunal, even if the statute
so directs.⁸
4. Advisory Opinions by Court of Appeal
When a Court of Appeal acts in an advisory role, it gives advice, not a ﬁnal judgment on appeal
from a legal hearing.⁹
The Court of Appeal declined to give an advisory opinion on the procedural
effects of its previous decision.¹⁰
A decision by a Court of Appeal on removal of a judge from ofﬁce,
pursuant to a statutory procedure, which is a condition precedent to removal, is appealable to the
Supreme Court of Canada.¹¹

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.4(4)
Section 53 of the Supreme Court Act allows the Federal Cabinet to refer to the Supreme Court
of Canada questions of law. That is constitutional, and authorized by s.101 of the B. N. A. Act 1867.
It is consistent with the words “general Court of Appeal”.¹
One cannot appeal from directions in reasons for judgment to send a copy of the reasons to
the Law Society.²
An appeal can only be brought from a decision, not a mere suggestion by a
judge with no decision.³
5. Appeal from Preliminary Ruling
In Alberta, most interlocutory civil orders, such as those about service, discovery, or security
for costs, do not need permission to appeal them.⁴
There is no statutory bar in Alberta to appeals in civil matters from preliminary rulings⁵
before
ﬁnal judgment,⁶
such as excluding evidence during a trial. In rare instances, there might even be
reason to appeal them. But in the vast majority of cases, the Court of Appeal refuses to hear such
appeals.⁷
That applies equally to appeals from interim rulings by a tribunal.⁸
If the appellant wins
the trial later, the appeal is unnecessary. If he or she loses, and the ruling contributed to that, he or
she can appeal the whole judgment and include the ruling as one ground.⁹
If it did not contribute,

R.14.4(4) PART 14: APPEALS
an appeal would be academic. Rule 14.71 prevents the failure to appeal the interim ruling from
doing any harm. Rule 14.71 exists to remove the tension between that Rule and short deadlines
to appeal. Usually the ﬁnal decision is considered to incorporate essential earlier preliminary
decisions, e. g. on a constitutional issue.¹
And in case of real doubt, the preliminary ruling could be appealed, but that appeal could then
be held back and then run along with a later appeal from the judgment.²
Where a preemptive
cross-motion to bar the applicant on a previous motion from making certain arguments was
severed and an appeal was brought, the appeal was adjourned until the substantive motion could
be decided, for it might render the appeal moot.³
But sometimes the Court of Appeal will hear such
an appeal, though often it requires leave to appeal.⁴
In those cases where by Rules or an Act, permission to appeal is needed, it will usually be
denied if the decision appealed is interlocutory.⁵
And it is rare that a judge will stay proceedings
pending an appeal partway through.⁶
Though the Court of Appeal is reluctant to hear appeals
in instalments, leave was given for an interlocutory appeal from a tribunal, as it will be done to
prevent harm later irreparable, especially where the proceedings are moving very slowly.⁷
Rule 14.5(1) (c) requiring leave to appeal from a ruling during trial refers to a real trial, not a
matter brought by Originating Notice.⁸
It does not apply to a ruling before the second issue is tried,
where there was an agreement (or order) to split trial of issues, and one was tried (and here where
only the Court of Appeal made trial of the second issue necessary). The need for permission is
narrow and is designed only to avoid interrupting trials.⁹
A fortiori one cannot appeal about a question not yet decided in the lower court.¹⁰
The courts often criticize appealing interim corollary relief awards in divorce suits, especially if
a deﬁnitive trial could be held about as quickly.¹¹
Sometimes they decline to hear the interlocutory
appeal.¹²

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.4(4)
The Court of Appeal discourages litigation by instalments, or hearing separately very similar
appeals by separate (but similar) parties.¹
A proposed costs appeal seemed to be interlocutory or premature, and usually the Court
of Appeal will not hear those. The real aim of the application for leave seems to have been
interpretation, a declaration of no practical use. The document in question is yet to be interpreted
or ruled on by the courts; that was still to come in this suit.²
In disputes over the nature of an appeal (to see if it will lie, or needs leave), one must look at
the substance of the matter in question.³
6. Appeal from Application Without Notice
The Court of Appeal ordinarily does not hear an appeal from an ex parte order; the better
relief is to move in King's Bench to set it aside under R.9.15,⁴
particularly in a child custody or
access matter.⁵
If that has been tried and failed on the merits, that weighs against giving leave to
appeal (if such leave is needed).⁶
The reasons for that policy are discussed.⁷
Even if legislation does not call for service of a court proceeding on the opponent, nor on the
government in question, if in fact they were served, the issue of whether R.14.4(2) bars an appeal
to the Court of Appeal does not arise.⁸
B. Pointless Appeals
1. Generally
One ground to deny leave to appeal was that soon a scheduled hearing in King's Bench would
decide afresh the issues proposed to be appealed, and decide on a better record.⁹
2. Moot Appeals
(a) What is Moot
If the subject of an appeal is moot¹⁰
or academic, or later becomes so, the Court of Appeal
ordinarily will not hear the appeal, and just dismiss or quash it.¹¹
That is so where deciding the

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.4(4)
Mootness is not merits, and whether the proposed topic is arguable (e. g. in law) is not the test.¹
An appeal which originally was about a live matter, may become moot by a latter occurrence
with the result that the decision no longer would have any practical effect on the parties’ rights,
or where the practical relief sought is no longer available. That change can be in the facts or the
legal circumstances, such as repeal of the legislation in question. That must be assessed in the
particular circumstances of the case, not abstractly and hypothetically.²
A case can become moot
even though the plaintiff seeks a declaration, as a declaration may not be granted where the
dispute has become academic or will have no practical effect in resolving any remaining issues.³
If the previous governing legislation was repealed, the case does not cease to be moot because
new legislation substantially the same is enacted, especially where some other ofﬁcial act is
involved. The court has no evidence or information about how the new legislation would apply.
Whether the old legislation was constitutional would be of little help in seeing if the new legislation
is constitutional. (The two pieces of legislation here were not of the same type or authority. )⁴
But remission or stay of the penalty does not render moot the question of contempt,⁵
nor does
compliance with the order under appeal.⁶
Nor is an appeal from grant of a permit made moot by
the fact that the oil well in question has been drilled, especially if its ongoing operation has safety
issues.⁷
Nor does the fact that two private parties have settled make the matter moot where
validity of legislation is in issue, and the Attorney-General has intervened and appealed.⁸
New
legislation does not make an appeal moot, if acts done under the old legislation are also in issue.⁹
Only exceptionally should a court hold that its own act renders an appeal moot.¹⁰
If a decided
appeal has since become moot, the court should not reopen or rehear it.¹¹
If the strike in question
has been settled before the appeal is heard, the appeal may be moot.¹²
Where after the proceeding commenced the mother and the children moved back to a city
where she had had them before, that did not end the live controversy about where to live nor

make the proceeding moot. Abandoning her own cross-application does not end her opponent’s

he could at once commence a new identical one.¹
A section in the election provisions of the Municipal Government Act had a large rate in there
being no mootness and so letting a declarataory suit continue even after a new election.²
(b) Policy and Justice
There are a host of policy and justice factors underlying that rule against hearing moot
appeals.³
That may include a situation where the facts have changed a lot since the hearing
appealed from,⁴
and there is a power in the lower court to amend its judgment for changed facts.⁵
Someone in almost every lawsuit would like more certain law, and what future amendments to
legislation will occur are difﬁcult to know, without proper evidence.⁶
(c) Exceptions
There is an exception which permits hearing a moot appeal, where the appellant shows⁷
that
matter is important to decide, and either a decision will have some practical effect on the parties’
rights, or no other case could suitably or bring it before the court in time.⁸
And even then, there

a profession sued its regulatory body, to attack its new regulatory requirement. The issues were not moot
then, but her noncompliance was not acted on, so the suit arguably became moot. If it became operative
later, there might not be time to test it in court, so the merits were heard: Health Sci. Assn. v. Alta. College
of Paramedics 2016 ABQB 723, 50 Alta LR(6th) 163 (¶’s 37-55). See the C. P. E. , Chapter 33, Part S.5 and
Chapter 74, Part H.4. It is material that the appellant let his appeal languish during the years the dispute was
adversarial and relevant, reviving the appeal only after it became moot: Cimmer v. Niessner, supra (¶ 47).

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.4(4)
should be an existing adversarial relationship.¹
The two parties should have a clear motive to put
forward their best arguments, and access to sufﬁcient information or evidence to do so.²
And the
court's function is basically adjudicative, not legislative.³
The tests are reviewed in the J. H. case.⁴
The question is discretionary, and no single factor dominates.⁵
Though there are exceptions to the general rule against hearing moot cases, court resources
are very scarce at present and that was compelling. Hearing argument (already) on the merits is
not a fourth exception allowing a moot appeal.⁶
Constitutional questions should not be decided without a factual matrix, nor should efﬁcacy
of judicial intervention. And it should not be assumed that more consultation with an aboriginal
group would cure constitutional problems. So such a question should not get leave to appeal, if it
has become moot.⁷
Some cases continued despite mootness, without laying down a precise exception.⁸
(d) Procedure
Whether an appeal is moot is to be decided by the panel on appeal, not earlier by a single
justice of appeal:⁹
The rule about hearing moot appeals is not the same as the Court of Appeal’s refusing to
decide and comment on every issue in the appeal, especially issues which do not affect the
result.¹⁰
The court would not hold that litigation was moot because that would be inconsistent with a
decision already made in parallel litigation in the same court. The order that it should be run by
that procedure had not been appealed.¹¹
Costs may be denied or reversed for delay in raising mootness.¹²
Where an arguable
interlocutory appeal was made moot by the decision in another appeal, each party was ordered
to bear its own costs.¹³

An appeal from an injunction is not necessarily nugatory if it would only be heard after the
injunction expired, if the injunction was supported by an undertaking as to damages given by

R.14.4(5) PART 14: APPEALS
the respondent, and most of the potential claim under the undertaking would be for what would
have been earned from the current employer. So the undertaking as to damages would prevent
irreparable harm.¹
An appeal may be quashed if the material ﬁled reveals no reasonable possibility that it could
succeed. The test is whether it is manifestly devoid of merit or substance.²
A party can lose its right to appeal by waiving it, even unintentionally. Express waivers are
rare,³
except as part of a settlement. But taking steps to enforce and afﬁrm the judgment being
appealed may bar one’s right to appeal it, if the two rights are inconsistent.⁴
For example, a party
against whom a contract had been declared valid could not take beneﬁts under the contract,
and then appeal using arguments which would make the contract void or terminated. But a party
awarded damages or support can probably collect the payments and appeal asking for bigger
damages or support.⁵
If the respondent does not argue that the ﬁrst appeal is barred by the
appellant’s acquiescing in the trial judgment, he cannot use the same acquiescence to bar a
further appeal.⁶
Obeying an order does not bar one from appealing it.⁷
Where the judgment given is satisfactory to a party, there is no point to his appealing, even if
he is dissatisﬁed with some of the reasons given, especially if the decision is interlocutory, as they
then create no res judicata; the appeal is pointless and hopeless.⁸
(5) No appeal is allowed
(a) from a judgment granting a divorce, on or after the date on which the
divorce takes effect, or
(b) unless an appeal judge extends the time, from an order made in a
divorce proceeding, more than 30 days after the date on which the
order was made. [Alta. Reg.41/14;136/22;218/22]

The time to appeal under the Divorce Act is 30 days, whereas for most appeals, it is one
month.¹
Months can be shorter or longer than 30 days, so this special Rule for appeals under the
Divorce Act is necessary. The statute would govern, but the Rules should not mislead counsel.
Note also R.12.59.
C. Setting Aside Judgments for Fraud etc.
If fraud is an exception to functus officio, so that a King's Bench judge can set aside an
entered King's Bench judgment, the mere fact that the plaintiff’s ﬁnancial evidence was one-sided
is not enough.²
An action to set aside a judgment as got through fraud and perjury was dismissed
as an abuse of process, because there was very little new evidence, it was far from conclusive,
and much of it had been obtainable long before, some easily.³
Where a previous judgment is set aside for perjury or the like, the court should substitute the
judgment that should have been given in the ﬁrst place. The jurisdiction to set aside judgments for
fraud appears largely equitable.⁴
Subdivision 3
Appeals with Permission
Appeals only with permission
14.5(1) Except as provided in this rule, no appeal is allowed to the Court of
Appeal from the following types of decisions unless permission to appeal has
been obtained:
(a) a decision of a single appeal judge;
(b) any pre-trial decision respecting adjournments, time periods or time
limits;
(c) any ruling during trial, where the appeal is brought before the trial is
concluded;
(d) a decision made on the consent of the parties;
(e) a decision as to costs award only, but an appeal or cross appeal is
not “as to costs award only” if a related substantive decision is also
being appealed; [Alta. Reg.126/23]
(f) any decision where permission to appeal is required by an enactment;
(g) any decision in a matter where the controversy in the appeal can
be estimated in money and does not exceed the sum of $25 000
exclusive of costs;

A. Leave to Appeal Under Subrule (1)
1. Introduction
The law requires permission (leave) of the court to appeal in any of the traditional
circumstances named in R.14.5(1). This is often still called “leave to appeal”. Rule 14.5(1) also
requires permission appeal from decisions on some newer topics: respecting security for costs,¹
respecting adjournments or time periods²
or limits, from rulings during trial, or an order setting a
trial date.³
Rule 14.5(1) is an actual prohibition on appeals from costs or consent orders, and so a free-
standing right to appeal in another Rule does not remove the need for leave.⁴
Of course one cannot get permission to appeal if an appeal would not lie, or if there is no right
of appeal at all, or if the Court of Appeal would not hear the appeal. Such grounds are discussed
above under R.14.4.⁵
Nor is permission possible if there is no real prospect of success, or no
generally-important or legal question.⁶
So one cannot get permission on a constitutional question,
if the tribunal to be appealed from has no jurisdiction to decide constitutional questions.⁷
One
ground to deny permission to appeal was that the issue was moot. Another was that soon a
scheduled hearing in King's Bench would decide afresh the issues on a better record.⁸
Permission to appeal from a certain type of order (such as setting time limits), is needed only if
that part of the order is being appealed, not if the appeal is from other parts of the order not doing
things described in R.14.5(1).⁹
The fact that in appeals from one appeal judge, ordinarily the judge hearing the permission
application will be the judge sought to be appealed, does not create impermissible institutional
bias.¹⁰

14-25
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(1)
The process is bifurcated and prior leave is needed, not a combined hearing for leave and to
allow the appeal.¹
2. Appeal from Consent Order
One circumstance requiring leave is an appeal from an order made by consent of the parties.²
Consent to the King's Bench order (which bars appeal to the Court of Appeal) must be real,
not apparent or mere form.³
That means an actual bargain, not mere acquiescence or failure to
protest, nor a concession that the remedy would be right if the liability existed.⁴
So one cannot ordinarily appeal terms which were put in an order at one’s request, which
terms were never in dispute.
Where it was not clear whether a costs order after trial was the court’s decision, or the contract
of the parties to compromise, the matter was sent back by the Court of Appeal to the trial judge.⁵
To get leave to appeal a consent order, one needs to show reasonable prospect of success,
important question of law or precedent, and no prejudice from delay if the appeal is heard. (If
children are involved, their best interests can override all else, and resources of parents and the
court become relevant. )⁶
3. Appeal as to Costs Award Only
Another circumstance requiring leave under R.14.5(1) is an appeal as to a costs award only.⁷
What is such an appeal depends on substance, not form.⁸
It does not depend on whether costs
will raise a new issue. And the Rule expressly says that leave is not needed if a related substantive
decision is also appealed. The two appeals need not be heard at the same time, nor ﬁled at the
same time, to escape the need for leave.⁹
But very important is what the formal order says, not
whether other relief was sought at the same time.¹⁰
One relevant question is if the two decisions
could have been heard in one appeal, if the Court of King's Bench had followed the usual timing.
So if the partiers delayed addressing costs until other applications on other topics were decided,
leave was needed to decide costs.¹¹

14-26
R.14.5(1) PART 14: APPEALS
If merits of the suit and its costs are both appealed, but then merits are discontinued, the
appeal becomes one of costs alone and needs leave.¹
The obstacle to ﬁling one notice of appeal
(for substance and costs) was not the Rules, but expiry of the time to appeal, necessitating an
earlier appeal as to merits alone before the decision on costs was pronounced. The problem arose
only from delay in entering one judgment. The second notice did not make the appeal one as to
“costs only”.²
If a party cross-appeals several things and some cannot go forward, and only costs
remain, that does not make it an appeal as to costs alone, needing leave. All the issues on both
sides would have been appealed together had King's Bench decisions followed the usual timing.³
But one does not need leave for a bona fide appeal about something other than costs, even if
costs are also appealed, and even if ultimately the only thing which the appeal court varies is the
costs.⁴
If there is a full appeal on all issues, a cross-appeal on costs only, needs no leave.⁵
An appeal about implications or enforcement of a costs order may not be about costs alone,
but if it is about time or adjournments, it still needs leave to appeal it.⁶
In Alberta, one needs permission to appeal even if the costs order impugned is wrong in
principle.⁷
It might be otherwise if the judge appealed from lacked jurisdiction?⁸
Did an appeal about the size of one’s lawyer’s fees require leave?⁹
Though that used to be
unclear, the 2023 amendment to the Rules (Alta. Reg.126/2023) now makes the answer clear.
Now the phrase is “a costs award”, which plainly is about payment by one party to another. It does
not include a dispute over the size of a debt from a client to the client’s own lawyer, nor a contract
about that topic. (furthermore, R.14.5(1) (i) now has references to Rr.10.26 and 10.44, which
reinforce that clarity). See also the new 2023 definition of “costs award”. It is now clearly different
from “lawyer’s charges”. But who is liable for the legal fees is not discretionary, is not a question
as to costs only, and needs no leave to appeal.¹⁰

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(1)
Leave to appeal costs alone should rarely be granted.¹
The tests for leave to appeal an order
as to costs alone are whether the topic is arguable,²
important to settle law or principle,³
practically
useful,⁴
and would entail no unreasonable delay.⁵
All these criteria must be met.⁶
In one case, the
question was moot and not of general importance to the public or profession and it was hard for
a Court of Appeal to lay down general principles.⁷
Such leave is needed because costs are highly
discretionary and such appeals are deferential.⁸
There is no need for permission to appeal costs alone, if the appeal is from an Applications
Judge to a King's Bench judge.⁹
If the higher court ﬁnds that no appeal lies to it without leave, it can stay proceedings pending
an application to the lower court for permission to appeal.¹⁰
4. Appeal Worth Under $25, 000
See Part I below.
5. Criteria for Permission to Appeal
See also subpart 3 above, and Part I below, and the notes after R.14.5(3).
Legislated need for leave to appeal is a gate-keeping function,¹¹
and lack of opposition to
leave from the respondent is not enough ground for the court to give leave. Technical procedural
objections may not be important enough to justify leave.¹²
Some of the criteria for permission to appeal under the Rules overlap with some of the criteria
under some statutes, so some of the cases noted under Part B below may be of use under the
Rules.

14-28
R.14.5(1) PART 14: APPEALS
The tests for leave to appeal in certain interlocutory (procedural) matters come in two sets. First
are general tests: arguable case,¹
question of law,²
importance,³
practical effect here, no undue
prejudice, no bar to appeal, expense is worth it,⁴
and standard of review.⁵
But these categories
also represent litigation by instalments and a serious conﬂict between appeal and promptness,
even dilemmas. So there is a second test: leave should be rare, and arguability and importance
are not enough.⁶
That is especially true of appeals from orders for security for costs, which seldom
succeed.⁷
Leave may be refused if the chances of success are poor because the standard of review is
tough.⁸
Or hopeless because of some clear legal rule.⁹
On a motion for permission to appeal, an
important question of law is irrelevant if any appeal would likely not reach the merits of that topic,
e. g. legislation bars suits on the topics, or limitation periods have expired.¹⁰
Leave may be denied if the issues proposed have no general interest to the Bar at large,
and there had been here a prior appeal where any issues of principle should have been raised;
litigating in instalments is not condoned. Large amounts involved may justify a second level of
appeal, but not always; that is not a general rule.¹¹
On the amount in issue, see Part I below.
An important aspect of whether the question is of sufﬁcient importance, is how many people
would be affected by deciding the question.¹²

14-29
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(1)
Permission to appeal should be denied if the respondent makes out a good case for security
for costs, but the applicant admits that he cannot post costs.¹
It is hard to get permission to appeal
a security for costs topic, still harder if it was made by a case management judge familiar with that
lawsuit; there must be unreasonableness, or a clear error.²
One justice had refused to hear a lay litigant on behalf of an estate, as he and it had no lawyer.
He did not get permission to appeal, as no error of law or important fact, unreasonable exercise
of discretion, or question of general importance was shown.³
Knocking out on appeal one reason by a regulatory tribunal would be academic if the other
reason stood, where either reason would justify the decision. So if leave to appeal one ground is
denied, there is no point considering the other reason.⁴
6. Preliminary Rulings
Permission to appeal is not required from a decision which procedurally took place in the
preliminary stages of a lawsuit or trial, but which was not really an interlocutory ruling in that, as
it was in effect an injunction against a non-party.⁵
Though normally the Court of Appeal will not hear an appeal from an interlocutory ruling
during a hearing, it can do so in some circumstances, and the test seems like manifest injustice
or error, or arbitrariness.⁶
Usually the processes of the administrative tribunal should be exhausted before one tries to
appeal one of its interlocutory decisions, so leave to appeal such a decision was adjourned.⁷
A decision that evidence is inadmissible is not a “judgment” and so no permission to appeal
is possible.
7. Appeal from Order About Time
Case management is important, and the timeline of the lawsuit is at its heart, and includes
the order in which motions will be heard, and schedules for motions and trial preparation. An
appeal from a scheduling decision is often pointless or detrimental, and appellate interference
there is highly unusual.⁸
That is why R.14.5(1) (b) requires permission to appeal decisions about
adjournments, time periods, or time limits.⁹
Leave requires an arguable point of some signiﬁcance to the practice or to the particular
lawsuit. Unduly slowing the lawsuit, or undue excess of prejudice over beneﬁt, weigh against
leave.¹⁰

R.14.5(1) PART 14: APPEALS
The “time periods” category needing leave applies only to times which are an important part
of the order, or the main issue in the appeal, not to something like an injunction which expires
at trial.¹
Permission was needed to appeal extension of time to ﬁle an expert report.²
But no
permission was needed to appeal an order about who can examine an expert and when, and how
to rebut his evidence.³
It takes permission to appeal an adjournment or refusal of an adjournment,
even adjournment of a trial,⁴
or an order whose main effect is to adjourn a hearing.⁵
The Rule requires leave to appeal decisions respecting time periods, even if the effect of the
decision was to determine the substantive right to contest proceeding with the merits. That the
effect is to determine substantive rights is not enough to exclude this Rule. This Rule is worded
differently from its predecessor, Part J of the Consolidated Practice Directions. The new Rule
applies to all appeals, and the effect of the order appealed is not a criterion. The scope of the
present new Rule is broad. Whether the order was ﬁnal or interlocutory is not the issue in Alberta.⁶
8. Appeal from One Justice of Appeal
See Part J.1 below.
B. Tests for Leave to Appeal Under Other Legislation⁷
1. Supreme Court of Canada
Since the 1970’s, the Supreme Court of Canada has rarely given reasons for granting or
denying leave to appeal to it. But one leave panel did give brief reasons denying leave, saying “the
applicant has not raised a question of law of public importance which is the test we are bound to
apply under the Supreme Court Act. . . ”.⁸
Another decision denies leave for want of general interest
or importance, but contains dicta that the Supreme Court of Canada could give leave to appeal
even if leave to appeal to the Court of Appeal had been denied by that Court.⁹
Applications for
leave to appeal are only reheard in exceedingly rare circumstances.¹⁰
The rest of this section B is about leave to appeal to the Alberta Court of Appeal.

from a pre-trial decision about adjournment or time periods, but the test is not whether part of the decision or
order deals with time periods; the question is which ruling is being appealed. Here it was a scheduling ruling.
That tends to be litigation by instalments, and the delay from appealing will be disproportionate. The appeal
will become moot, or there will be a stay so the appellant wins de facto at once: Ozark v. TERIC, infra (¶’s
28, 45). Setting something down for hearing on a particular date is a scheduling order needing leave to

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(1)
2. Criteria for Leave to Appeal to the Court of Appeal
(a) Various Statutes
There are many statutes which also require leave of someone (often an appellate judge) in
order to appeal (e. g. to appeal from the Alberta Utilities Regulator,¹
or from an arbitrator²
). Often
the legislation conﬁnes any appeal to a point of law or jurisdiction alone.³
Special criteria apply to leave to appeal from a municipal subdivision and development appeal
board,⁴
an assessment review board,⁵
under insolvency legislation;⁶

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(1)
(b) Recurring Principles
Determining when something may be appealed or if there is a right to seek leave to appeal is
dependent upon the substance of the matter in question.¹
There is an extended discussion of the principled basis for need for leave to appeal, including
various rôles of a Court of Appeal.²
Where leave to appeal is needed and a question of law or not is important, at the leave stage
a deﬁnite question of pure law need not be certain; it may be enough that an extricable question
of law might emerge from one of the questions of mixed law and fact.³
But there must be some
relevant evidence on the record so the judge can see whether there is a relevant question of law.⁴
What are the tests for giving leave to appeal?⁵
Generally speaking, there must be an arguable
error in the order to be appealed,⁶
and it must affect the result.⁷
Leave to appeal should not be
granted even if important legal questions of general public interest are involved, and large sums
are in issue, if the decision sought to be appealed is plainly right,⁸
or incontestable because of

R.14.5(1) PART 14: APPEALS
res judicata.¹
An important question of law does not assist if it likely would not be reached in this
appeal, e. g. because of other statutory bars to suit.²
The tests for leave in bankruptcy cases are
set out.³
Whether the proposed appeal is arguable is heavily inﬂuenced by the standard of review
applicable to it.⁴
Not all errors in law by a statutory tribunal are subject to view on a standard of
correctness, but it may be useful for the Court of Appeal to clarify what is the standard of review,
and so leave to appeal was given here.⁵
Often it is material whether the decision would be a precedent in other cases.⁶
A question of
law includes how to interprete a statute, scope of a tribunal’s jurisdiction, and compliance with
procedural fairness and natural justice.⁷
A question of law is ordinarily not enough:⁸
it must be
a general question of law of general importance to many litigants.⁹
If the question of law is well

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(1)
settled, it has no general public importance.¹
Or if the parties are agreed on the substantive
legal test?²
The more important the question, the easier it is to get leave.³
Not every question in
administrative law is a question of jurisdiction for appellate purposes.⁴
If more evidence would be
needed to decide the substantive submissions, that may bar leave.⁵
If the statute allows permission to appeal only on a question of law or jurisdiction, then an
appeal about assessing conﬂicting evidence, or procedure, is impossible.⁶
A court should be slow
to give leave to appeal from a tribunal on a question of its adjournments or procedure.⁷
It is not
necessary for the Court of Appeal to be the ﬁnal arbiter of which documents should be ordered to
be produced.⁸
A question of fact with no question of law or principle involved, will not get leave.⁹
A Subdivision and Development Appeal Board can use any evidence it wants, including outside
evidence or maps, to interpret a development permit, which use makes the decision a ﬁnding of
mixed law and fact.¹⁰
Though criminal precedents are usually not of much help in civil motions for leave to appeal,
one decision discusses policy issues and suggests some relevant considerations.¹¹
That there
is a legal question of genuine importance with no Canadian appellate authority on point, was
important in one case.¹²
On the test for leave to appeal a provincial offence, see the Day Chief
case.¹³
(c) Miscellaneous Aspects of Leave to Appeal to Court of Appeal
What needs leave to appeal depends on each separate portion (paragraph) of the impugned
King’s Bench order.¹⁴
Leave to appeal from one Justice of Appeal’s decision to a panel of the Court of Appeal, is
rarely given, and requires some compelling reason. A decision simply on procedure, such as
extending timel, is a poor candidate. And actual harm or loss should be a clear result, not mere
possibility of future loss.¹⁵

14-36
R.14.5(1) PART 14: APPEALS
Usually the Court of Appeal denies permission to appeal interim decisions of the Alberta
Utilities Commission to the Court of Appeal; the party should wait for the Commission’s ﬁnal
decision and see if he wins.¹
Permission may be refused if the matter is likely to be moot by the time that the appeal could
be heard.²
Leave may be denied if it would create undue prejudice to the respondent,³
or if there
would be a bar to the appeal, such as irreversibility of the order sought to be appealed.⁴
Permission to appeal may be refused if the appeal is out of time, and no ground to extend the
time is shown.⁵
The Director is not exempted from need for leave to appeal under the Family Law Act, even after
a permanent guardianship order. That another statute was also involved and the two proceedings
were together, is not an exception.⁶
Courts encourage settlement, so the most compelling circumstances are needed to appeal
an arbitration award.⁷
Where the Arbitration Act calls for importance to the parties, some element of public interest
is necessary too.⁸
It is relevant whether the public would have some interest in the appeal, but it is
not mandatory, as the Legislature does not mention that factor.⁹
There is a complicated discussion
of what is an application for leave to appeal to the Court of Appeal under s.44 vs. s.48 of the
Arbitration Act.¹⁰
To get leave under s.45 of the Arbitration Act, the judge must decide whether
issue is reasonably arguable, likely to affect the result, likely of interest to others or later suits,
and whether there is any independent reason to refuse the leave or restrict the appeal proposed,
though none of those four tests is conclusive.¹¹
It is not a bar to leave to appeal, that the question of law was not argued in the trial court?¹²
If part of the order is in a category needing permission to appeal and another distinct part is
not, only the former part needs permission.¹³
But the fact that there will be an appeal as of right as
to part is not a ground to give permission on the part which needs leave.¹⁴
On the interactions of
two possible grounds of appeal, see also subpart A.5 (end), supra.

14-37
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(1)
Whether an order was made “under” a certain Act, and so needs permission to appeal depends
on the procedure used to get the order, not on whether the process is ﬁnished. It is not enough
that another Act also gave the court jurisdiction, nor to look for which elements of the order come
under which Act.¹
Discussion of when an order in a Divorce or Family Law suit is “made pursuant
to this Act” and so bring with it a statutory right to appeal, when the order was about procedure,
such as compelling discovery answers.²
Whether that statutory appeal right dispensed with any
need to get leave to appeal imposed by provincial legislation, has conﬂicting answers in the case
law of various provinces.³
Comments about the appeal’s merits given by another court, or by itself when giving permission
to appeal, do not bind the Court of Appeal panel later hearing the appeal.⁴
3. Evidence
The Rules allow an afﬁdavit in support of a motion for permission to appeal, but if it is from a
Subdivision and Development Appeal Board, the Municipal Government Act s.689 bars evidence
which was not before the Board, so only limited types of new evidence can be put into such an
afﬁdavit.⁵
The applicant may ﬁle an afﬁdavit if breach of the rules of natural justice is alleged, or in
order to show that the legal question argued is important to the applicant or to other segments of
the community, e. g. that many other people are similarly situated.⁶
On whether needed records should come from a party (by afﬁdavit) or from the tribunal
sought to be appealed (usually preferable), see the Globexx case.⁷
Despite this theory, it is very
common, proper, and desirable, to seek permission to appeal with an afﬁdavit identifying the key
documents and attaching correct copies, as online or law report versions often have editing or
typos.⁸
Such basic documents or basic procedural history (or importance to the applicant?) are
not new evidence requiring permission to adduce.⁹
However, afﬁdavits ﬁled to get leave are not part of the record on appeal, and do not and cannot
be put before the panel hearing the appeal.¹⁰
So statutory bars to new evidence are irrelevant, and
no objections to such afﬁdavits on the permission for appeal application should be decided by the
one judge who hears that.¹¹

R.14.5(1) PART 14: APPEALS
Other material should not be in afﬁdavits to support an application for permission to appeal,¹
especially argument,²
or law or policy matters which the court itself will know.³
On a motion for
leave to appeal from a summary conviction an “afﬁdavit of merit” by a staff lawyer that the errors
were signiﬁcant, and the grounds of appeal highly arguable, and that there was a strong likelihood
that the result would change on appeal, is inappropriate and inadmissible. Viable grounds are
shown by the record, the reasons, and the legal authorities, not by evidence. Usually evidence for
such an application should not be hearsay.⁴
C. Whether Appeal is Restricted to Set Questions
If nothing more is said, after permission to appeal is given, the appellant can raise any ground
on appeal, not merely the one argued to get permission.
However, a general statutory power to hear appeals with permission permits a judge to give
permission to appeal limited to one or more speciﬁc questions of law. If the judge does that, the
appeal is so conﬁned to that speciﬁc question.⁵
In Alberta, usually the order giving permission
restricts the appeal to a ground or grounds stated by the judge giving permission.⁶
Restricting
permission to set questions bars any other questions, except a suggestion that the Court of
Appeal lacks jurisdiction to hear the appeal.⁷
The respondent is usually similarly limited in a civil
case, but not so limited in a criminal one. By statute the question must be restricted in some
summary conviction offences, e. g. provincial ones.⁸
If legislation says that an appeal must be on a question of law, it would be unusual to give
permission in general without identifying the question of law. If leave (permission) is given and no
question is stated though the appeal must be on a question of law, the judge can and should later
state the question of law.⁹

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(1)
In Alberta cases where ﬁxed questions are customary, it is important for counsel to frame the
suggested questions in the written application seeking permission.¹
But the court has a discretion to permit other issues to be argued on the application for
permission (leave), as long as there is no prejudice.²
D. Effect of Lack or Refusal of Leave
If permission is necessary, unless and until it is granted, no appeal exists. If permission to
appeal is needed but not got, any appeal lacks jurisdiction and will be quashed (if not yet set for
hearing),³
or dismissed (if it comes on for hearing).⁴
Because the appeal does not exist, there
cannot be a stay pending appeal⁵
or other incidental relief.⁶
And ﬂaws in its procedure may not
be cured.⁷
The Registrar may refuse to accept appeals ﬁled without getting the necessary permission to
appeal.⁸
Lack of necessary permission to appeal may be ground to refuse to restore an appeal struck
from the list for some smaller default.⁹
Including two appeals in one notice of appeal, one of the two not needing leave, does not
remove the need for leave for the other one if it is a type needing leave.¹⁰
If one judge refuses permission to appeal and no appeal lies without permission, no appeal
from the one judge's refusal of permission is possible in Alberta¹¹
(unless maybe the judge wrongly
declined jurisdiction and refused to hear the application).¹²
Cases from other provinces are not

R.14.5(1) PART 14: APPEALS
good law in Alberta.¹
The Supreme Court of Canada will not give permission to appeal to it on an
issue on which the intermediate Court of Appeal denied permission to appeal to it, even if it gave
permission on other issues.²
Nor will the Alberta Court of Appeal hear an appeal from refusal of a
King's Bench judge to give permission to appeal to the Court of King's Bench; the Court of Appeal
cannot hear it.³
If permission to appeal by the Court of Appeal is necessary, but not sought, on opening of the
appeal the court may not give permission retroactively, even if the respondent does not object.⁴
A topic for which leave to appeal has been neither sought nor granted cannot be raised on
appeal in an appeal where leave is necessary.⁵
Applications for permission to appeal are only reheard in exceedingly rare circumstances.⁶
After a single justice of appeal has refused an application, and after leave to appeal has been
refused, that does not remove the single judge's power to reopen argument on the application
if no formal judgment has been ﬁled; but such reopening will be extremely rare, and the test is
extremely strict.⁷
E. Time to Apply for Leave
1. References
On coordinating the application for permission to appeal, and the notice of appeal, see Part H
below. On extending time to seek permission, see also R.14.8n.
2. Implied Deadline
If there is no express time to seek permission, the statute implies that permission cannot be
given after the time to appeal expires.⁸
The rule against amending a pleading to add a new cause of action after expiry of the limitation
period applies also to amending an Originating Application for judicial review, especially to turn it
into an application for permission to appeal from the impugned award.⁹

14-41
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(1)
3. Special Types of Case
Appeals should be prompt, especially if from a Subdivision and Development Appeal Board,
and especially if there is a stay pending appeal. So a stay was conditional on the return date of the
application for permission to appeal being moved forward to a much earlier date.¹
Planning-law leave applications are urgent, and an application for permission to appeal is
effectively a stay, so judges were critical of a lawyer who seemed to be stalling the hearing of the
application.²
The need for permission to appeal on certain topics like children or land development, is to
avoid leaving a person or land in limbo for a long time, so the court should be slow to extend time
for permission to appeal.³
4. Computation
If legislation says that an application for permission to appeal must be returnable within 30
days, that means an actual attendance on a judge within that time.⁴
However, Alberta legislation about subdivision and development appeal boards no longer says
that.⁵
Sec.688(2) of the Municipal Government Act now sets time limits for appealing from a
Subdivision and Development Appeal Board, running from "issue" of the decision. That word is
not deﬁned in any relevant legislation. It was highly likely that this referred to making the decision,
not to giving or receiving notice of it, especially as there cannot be different dates for different
parties.⁶
But now time to seek permission to appeal from them runs from when written notice is received
of the decision,⁷
even if an oral decision has been given previously? There is no power to extend
the statutory 30 days for ﬁling and service, and the court cannot and should not give permission
to appeal if those deadlines are not met.⁸
If statute requires a written decision by the tribunal, time
to apply for permission to appeal it runs from when the written decision would be received in the
ordinary course of mail, not from an oral announcement of decision.⁹
The 30-day limit for seeking leave is met if an application for leave to appeal is ﬁled and served
within it. There is no (retroactive) breach of that time limit by a later order striking out part of that
application (and indeed it is hard to strike out parts of such an application).¹⁰

R.14.5(1) PART 14: APPEALS
In an appeal from a Subdivision and Development Appeal Board, both the municipality and
the Board must be served with a written application for permission to appeal. So they have to be
served within the statutory 30 days, and service within time limits set in the Rules of Court is not
sufﬁcient if it occurs later.¹
There is an extended discussion of how to interpret service and notice provisions in legislation
allowing appeals with permission, in the N. Sunrise case.²
On getting leave only during hearing of the appeal on the merits, see Part D above.
5. Inadequate or Awkward Times and Time Extensions
If there is not time to give proper notice before hearing the application, the judge can adjourn
and give directions as to who is to be given notice and so forth.³
If time ends on a Sunday, it is
automatically extended to the next business day.
Many statutes give a time limit to seek permission, and that time cannot be extended unless
the statute says so.⁴
If the statute expressly lets the court extend the time to apply for permission
to appeal, it can be extended after it expires, so long as the statute is silent on that point.⁵
However,
ﬁling a timely notice of appeal (rather than an application for permission) may sufﬁce to preserve
time.⁶
There are criteria for granting or withholding an extension of time.⁷
After some delay, prejudice
to the respondent is likely.⁸
The tests for extending time are genuine timely intent to seek leave or
appeal, an explanation for the delay, no serious prejudice to the respondent, no fruits taken from
the judgment, and a reasonable chance of success.⁹
The Mattson decision discusses the tight statutory time limits for leave to appeal from a
Subdivision and Development Appeal Board, and what can be done to ameliorate them, including
permission to ﬁle the Application on time, with the supporting afﬁdavit evidence and memorandum
of argument coming a little later.¹⁰
Often a party to a decision by an administrative tribunal used to ﬁle an application for permission
to appeal a decision of that tribunal within time, but adjourn it indeﬁnitely¹¹
if the overall proceeding
before the tribunal was not complete. How much one can include in one application for permission

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(1)
is not clear. Nor is whether one should appeal the original interlocutory decision or a later decision
which conﬁrms it.¹
If the application for permission would be late, the application to extend time to seek leave
should be ﬁled along with the application for permission, so the court can see the merits, and lack
of merit may be grounds to deny a time extension.²
An application for permission that is not heard
within 6 months is deemed abandoned.³
Permission to appeal was granted after factums were ﬁled and close to the hearing date.⁴
The
issues in the factums were close enough to what the justice of appeal giving permission would
have picked. But counsel were warned to be ready to address the standard of review.
There is no point in extending time to apply for leave in a case where the court hearing that
would have no power to give permission (and the other court has already refused it. )⁵
Such an
extension of time to appeal afresh is not the best way to cure improper dismissal of an appeal for
procedural defaults.⁶
Any application to quash an appeal should be prompt, on pain of costs.⁷
The court may refuse
an application for permission if it is late.⁸
Where the legislation sets a time limit to grant permission
to appeal, “or within such extended time” as the court allows, the time need not be continuous,
and can be extended after it expires. The time extension Rule applies generally.⁹
F. Procedural Conditions of Giving Permission
On permission limited to certain questions, see Part C, supra.
Permission to appeal may be given on condition of the appellants’
submitting to pay the respondent’s costs in any event,¹⁰
or paying thrown-away

be little chance of getting that, so it would be pointless to extend time: APL Ent. v. Richard Dudelzak Pro.
Corp.2005 ABCA 359 (one JA). A good case to extend time is academic if legislation requires permission
to appeal, and the requisite facts for such permission are absent: Alta. Treas. Br. v. Conserve Oil 1st Corp.

the appellant is often involved in such litigation and needs clarity or a change in the law, but the respondent
never litigates such topics.

R.14.5(1) PART 14: APPEALS
costs,¹
and ensuring speedy proceedings.²
The Rules and all court procedures aim for dealing
with all appeals as diligently as possible.
Permission to appeal is always granted on the implicit condition that the appellant will prepare
as expeditiously as circumstances permit.³
The practice often is for the judge not to say much when giving permission to appeal, so as not
to create difﬁculties for the Justices hearing the actual appeal later.⁴
G. Interveners and Standing⁵
In an appeal from a Subdivision and Development Appeal Board, only the Board and the
municipality are mandatory parties under s.688 of the Municipal Government Act, but a judge will
often add others (e. g. the developer and the neighbors) as parties.⁶
Some lawyers think that no
one else not mentioned in the Act need be a respondent, and others think that the statute forbids
naming anyone else, e. g. a neighbor. None of those beliefs is accurate. The common law, the rules
of natural justice, and the Rules of Court still require notice to all persons signiﬁcantly affected.
The statute sets a minimum for respondents, but does not remove ordinary principles of notice.
Failure to do that may be curable, but wastes everyone’s time and money. If a decision were made
without the proper parties and evidence, the result could be even worse.⁷
Meritorious legal issues are not a ground for permission to appeal if raised only by a person
whose affairs were not in issue in the impugned (tribunal) order.⁸
Only exceptionally will permission be given to intervene before permission to appeal.⁹
And
so neither will an intervener ordinarily be allowed to take part in the application for permission to
appeal?¹⁰
But only the Municipal Government Act says that the persons affected must get notice
of an application for leave to appeal from a Subdivision and Development Appeal Board, and so
a neighbor affected may ask to be added as a party before leave; no one else can represent her
interests.¹¹
Rarely can some person or group get permission to intervene to raise issues raised by
none of the existing parties, especially if that person or group has no special right or duty affected.¹²

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(1)
Even if it is a statutory party to the appeal, no longer can the tribunal being appealed from
argue the merits, if both parties are represented and a sufﬁcient adversarial context already
exists; the tribunal cannot even argue standard of review, and true jurisdictional questions rarely
arise.¹
A statutory tribunal can take part in (someone else’s) appeal from its decision only to defend
its jurisdiction or to explain the record.²
The dilemma where no party wants to oppose the appeal,
and the tribunal is willing to appear by counsel and argue jurisdiction and explain its decision, is
discussed.³
A Subdivision and Development Appeal Board should not make submissions on the
merits during a motion for leave to appeal from it to the Court of Appeal, even if the municipality
does not appear.⁴
A tribunal appealed from appeared on the motion for leave, but merely explained
established practices and procedures well-known. Impartiality was not compromised. Nor did the
tribunal thereby augment its reasons for decision to resist the appeal. A tribunal should not be
forced to repeat in its reasons matters implicitly or of common practice, in every decision, lest one
decision be later appealed.⁵
An intervener below with very little interest in the decision, which could not show that any of its
own affairs which would be affected, should not get permission to appeal, especially if the appeal
would have little chance of success, and the intervener had a chance to make its arguments
in the court which it sought to appeal from.⁶
But where the respondents did not wish to contest
permission, an intervener who showed that only her interests were really at stake, was allowed to
ﬁle written argument against permission to appeal.⁷
Legislation said that for purposes of appeals, where the Attorney General had appeared,
he or she was a party. So the Supreme Court of Canada Rules requiring notice of a motion for
permission to “all parties in the courts below” applied, and the Attorney General got an order
directing service on himself.⁸
Usually interveners or would-be interveners neither pay nor receive costs, but the judges have
a discretion, especially where the proposal is that the intervener pay costs (not receive them).⁹
Whether it raised public or private issues is relevant. Failing to get intervener status when raising
a purely private interest, is a proper time to order payment of costs. Especially when the would-be
intervener is not a public interest litigant, but rather a representative of members of one industry,
and even more so when the proposed intervention was very close to taking a position on the
merits.

R.14.5(1) PART 14: APPEALS
H. Miscellaneous Procedure
1. Particulars and Form
An application for permission to appeal to the Court of Appeal should clearly state the question
or questions which it is proposed that the Court of Appeal answer.¹
Where permission to appeal is needed, it is fairly common for the appellant to ﬁle (or tender)
the written application for permission and the notice of appeal at the same time.²
However, the
notice of appeal does not have to be ﬁled before permission is given.³
And the Registry’s practice
may be to refuse a notice of appeal of a kind needing leave, until leave is granted, maybe even
until the formal order granting permission is entered.⁴
Failure to have a separate preliminary decision on permission to appeal, and hearing it at the
same time as the grant of more time, is an arguable error of law. No Rule or Practice Direction
calls for or allows such a combined hearing, and some decisions recently hear the two separately.
In the two with a combined hearing, it is not explained how or why.⁵
2. Who Decides
The Supreme Court of Canada used to hear leave motions in panels of three judges, and
apparently still does where an oral hearing is held. For many years, all nine Supreme Court judges
have had some input into whether to give leave. Now it is believed that all nine judges vote on the
decision as to leave, though the ﬁnal decision on leave is by three judges.
Can one Justice of Appeal alone decide whether permission to appeal is needed?⁶
One justice of appeal has no jurisdiction to rule on permission to appeal from a Board where
the Act calls for permission of the “Court of Appeal”; then permission takes a panel.⁷
On rehearing a motion for permission, see the Mymryk,⁸
Hinse⁹
and Haljan¹⁰
cases. The fact
that permission was granted does not bar the court’s later quashing the appeal.¹¹

award must ﬁrst obtain permission to appeal. The statute mandates a bifurcated process. It is an error of
law to direct that the application for permission to appeal be heard contemporaneously with the appeal:

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(1)
It is unprecedented to give permission to appeal from an order giving permission to appeal
(even if the ﬁrst was by a King's Bench judge and the second by a justice of appeal). And if
it is ordered that the two appeals be argued together, then the second permission decision is
pointless and moot. Naturally the merits of the ﬁrst appeal would be decided ﬁrst, making the
second appeal unnecessary to consider.¹
See also subrule (3) and notes after subrule (4).
3. Evidence
See also Part B.4 above.
On an application for permission to appeal, the applicant may adduce factual evidence,²
but not opinion evidence.³
The applicant may give evidence (even late) to show standing.⁴
The
Supreme Court of Canada usually resists receiving afﬁdavits about whether a legal topic is of
general importance, on a motion for permission to appeal, though there are exceptions.⁵
If the appeal or leave must be on a point of law alone, and the suggested ground requires
knowing things not on the record (such as communications between trial counsel and his then
client the appellant), if no such evidence is given, ﬁnding a relevant question of law is impossible.⁶
There is a duty of full and frank disclosure when seeking ex parte permission to appeal. If later
legislation makes the question of law on which permission was granted academic, permission
may be withdrawn.⁷
The Privy Council on arguing the appeal were not bound by earlier ex parte
permission by themselves to appeal to themselves.⁸
One cannot appeal on the basis of facts which one knew but did not tell the court appealed
from about.⁹
An unsuccessful applicant may have to pay much higher costs for having tried to stall hearing
of her own leave application.¹⁰

14-48
R.14.5(1) PART 14: APPEALS
4. Costs of Leave Application
Costs of an application for permission to appeal may be increased somewhat where the losing
party took unnecessary steps because of acrimony.¹
Even if the appeal itself would involve large
sums, costs of the permission application should be on a lower column?²
Where permission to
appeal is granted despite active opposition, immediate costs to the appellant of the permission
application may be appropriate. Many of the tests for permission have nothing to do with the
merits, and will not come up again. The respondent is not obliged to oppose permission.³
Permission to appeal from a Subdivision and Development Appeal Board was not warranted,
but the respondent had to pay solicitor-client costs because it had ignored the statutory stay and
proceeded with the development despite the stay.⁴
On interveners, see Part G above.
I. Small Amounts
One needs permission from an appellate judge to appeal if the amount involved in the appeal
can be estimated in money and is $25, 000 or less.⁵
Usually economics prevent such appeals
anyway.⁶
(There are many picky cases saying that no amount of money at all is involved in various
types of appeal.⁷
They are largely irrelevant to this subrule, for if right, they mean that no amount
is involved and so this subrule does not apply. )
Where the right to appeal without permission depends on the amount or value of the matter
in controversy, that means the loss which granting or refusing of the right would entail.⁸
But the
loss does not have to be a loss limited to loss to the estate of the bankrupt; different extent of
recovery for the other creditors should be taken into account.⁹
Even if the main claim is small, no
permission is necessary, if the counterclaim is well over $25 thousand, and matters other than
money are also involved.¹⁰
One cannot escape this subrule by adding together amounts awarded to different parties, or
for different purposes such as interest or costs.¹¹
