14-49
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(1)
If there is no permission to appeal, such a small appeal may well be struck out summarily.¹
Permission to appeal should not be given where only a small sum is in issue, the question is
how to interpret a particular contract, and no other cases would necessarily be affected by the
decision.²
But permission may be given on how to interpret part of an important public statute,
despite other factual issues and likely ensuing delay.³
Leave to appeal a matter under $25
thousand was granted. There was reasonable prospect of success, and a legal question important
to the public.⁴
Poor chances of success and delay are grounds to deny permission to appeal.⁵
If the appeal
is not arguable, permission to appeal a small amount will not be given.⁶
Permission to appeal was
refused, where the prospects of success were very low; that the chambers judge under appeal
expressed some doubt is not sufﬁcient.⁷
Nor will it be given respecting a postulated future debt
or its set-off.⁸
A 1985 amendment to the Builders’ Lien Act limits such appeals to cases of $5, 000 or over,⁹
and prevails over R.14.5(1) (g).¹⁰
If neither party raises the ﬂoor amount as an issue, the case management ofﬁcer may.¹¹
If permission is given because the legal point is important to one party, often that party will
have to pay the other side’s costs in any event.¹²
A winning appellant may get no costs for his
appeal if the amount he recovers is very small.¹³
If the proposed appeal is about costs or legal fees under $25, 000.00 and a King's Bench judge
refuses permission to appeal under R.14.5(1) (d), (e), one appellate judge cannot give permission
under R.14.5(1) (g).¹⁴

R.14.5(2) PART 14: APPEALS
One judge refused to extend time to appeal, as it appeared that the amount involved was
below $25, 000, so that it would take permission to appeal. Since there would be little chance of
getting that, it would be pointless to extend time.¹
Permission to appeal a costs order well under $25, 000 was given as the Court of Appeal
had not yet ruled on the principle in question, and a party was being made to pay costs because
the appeal had become moot through matters in no way the fault of or connected with the party
paying.²
Rule 14.5(1) (g) applies to a small monetary penalty under election legislation where that was
the only result, and there was no disqualiﬁcation or inability to run again, and the election in
question was long past. Important issues are a topic for the application to appeal, and do not
remove the need for leave.³
A statute increasing the ﬂoor amount for appeals does not apply to cases where the judgment
to be appealed is given before the Act comes into force, so the right to appeal was vested.⁴
An
appellant may have to pay costs of a failed application to quash an appeal as being under the ﬂoor
amount, if the amount in issue only appeared from the conﬂicting afﬁdavits for the application, not
before.⁵
Where the court’s jurisdiction depends on the amount in issue, the prayer for relief in the
statement of claim is not enough to establish that.⁶
The “value of the rights in issue” refers to the
whole life of the contract, not just the amount accrued to date.⁷
(2) Permission to appeal decisions of single appeal judges under subrule (1)
(a) must be sought from the same judge who made the decision that is to be
appealed.
(3) No appeal is allowed under subrule (1) (a) from a decision of a single appeal
judge granting or denying permission to appeal.
(4) No appeal is allowed under subrule (1) (j) from an order denying the vexatious
litigant permission to institute or continue proceedings.
[Alta. Reg.41/14;85/16;218/22]

14-51
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.5(4)
J. Appeals to a Panel from One Justice
1. Generally
These subrules do not permit an appeal from refusal of permission to appeal, even with
permission of the judge who denied permission.¹
An appeal was dismissed partly for failure to get permission under these subrules.²
The tests for permission to appeal under subrule (1) (a)³
include whether there is an issue of
law or principle or jurisdiction or question of general importance,⁴
whether discretion is involved,
whether there is an arguable ground of appeal,⁵
and whether the appeal would be of practical
utility.⁶
Therefore the only new factor, beyond the ones common to all permission applications,
is showing there is no abuse of process.⁷
Therefore one applying for permission should do more
than reargue the points already argued before the one justice of appeal.⁸
To get permission there
should either be a serious question of general importance, or possible error of law, or discretion
unreasonably exercised, or misapprehension of important facts. If none is present, permission
should be denied.⁹
The mere fact that there are ongoing enforcement efforts by the respondent
is not enough.¹⁰
A deferential standard of review on appeal militates against permission.¹¹

14-52
R.14.5(4) PART 14: APPEALS
permission should be refused if the result of the proposed appeal would have no practical effect,
e. g. bias of a judge no longer involved and who denied nothing permanent.¹
Permission to appeal should not be given if no discrete arguable error is shown, and an appeal
would be merely to have a panel of three justices decide the original application afresh on its
merits.²
Leave to appeal from one appellate justice to three is extraordinary.³
One reason to be
slow to give leave to appeal from one justice of appeal, is that likely that new appeal would be
made moot by deciding the earlier-ﬁled substantive appeal, unless the earlier appeal were to be
stayed to be heard along with the new one.⁴
Such a judge should not give leave to appeal her own
decision unless it is shown that the decision was unreasonable, or based on misapprehension
of important facts.⁵
Often leave to appeal from one justice of appeal should be given only if there
is some topic of law, jurisdiction, or policy important to the public, to the courts, or to those who
may argue other appeals later.⁶
Discretionary decisions or ones about time or logistics are rarely
suitable for permission to appeal.⁷
Rehearing must be in the public interest.⁸
Rarely will one justice of appeal give leave to appeal that decision, because such one-judge
decisions are usually not so important as matters appealable as of right. The point of leaving a
matter to one judge is to conserve judicial resources. The chance of success on such an appeal
is very low. There would have to be some compelling reason to reargue the matter,⁹
i. e. general
importance to the community or to the parties. And value to the community requires precedential
value in this area of law, or a further decision. Usually single justices of appeal apply settled law.¹⁰
Enough importance to the parties requires misunderstanding of the law or misapprehension of
the evidence, which substantially contributed to the impugned decision.¹¹
For leave to appeal, a
reasonable chance of success is needed, which means over a 40% chance of winning.¹²
Are these subrules constitutional?¹³
Amendments to an Appeal Record and factums were denied, and permission to appeal this to
a panel was denied, as no particular error was alleged, and no question of general importance or
question of law was suggested. What was sought would in effect overrule a panel’s earlier refusal
of an adjournment of the appeal.¹⁴
It is especially hard to get permission to appeal decisions about timing or logistics.¹⁵

14-53
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.6(1)
2. Miscellaneous
Rule 14.5(2) is very clear, and removes any argument, or common-law principle, that the same
judge should not hear an application for leave to appeal from his or her own decision.¹
Bias of a case management judge in the Court of King's Bench had become moot, as that
judge could never be the trial judge, and he had declined to hear any future summary judgment
applications, and changing the case management judge would not move the suit forward, so
permission to appeal leaving an appeal about that struck off, was denied.²
Subrule (4) was applied and leave to appeal denied, in the Thompson case.³
Subrule (4) does not bar an appeal from the declaration that someone is a vexatious litigant,
though permission for such an appeal is needed.⁴
The tests for giving such orders are settled and
are not legal questions of general importance.⁵
On such a motion, the court can take notice of the
court record of other proceedings by the litigant in question.⁶
The questions for an existing appeal should not be expanded after two years' delay, especially
if the panel could decide the existing appeal without the extra questions.⁷
Subdivision 4
Cross Appeals
Cross appeals
14.6(1) A respondent to an appeal may cross appeal any decision on which it
could have commenced an appeal, by ﬁling a notice of cross appeal under rule
14.11 [How to start a cross appeal].
(2) Subject to subrule (3), where an appeal has been commenced as of right
or with permission, the respondent does not need permission to ﬁle a cross
appeal with respect to any decision described in rule 14.5 [Appeals only with
permission] if the cross appeal is only intended to vary the decision already
under appeal.
(3) Where an enactment provides that an appeal may be commenced in the
Court of Appeal with permission, a respondent who wishes to cross appeal must
apply for permission to cross appeal.⁸
[Alta. Reg.41/14]

R.14.6(3) PART 14: APPEALS

Related Provisions
14.5 (permission to appeal) ; 14.11 (how to cross appeal) ; 14.12(3) (format of cross appeal) ;

14.24(2) (cross appeal in fast track appeal)
An appeal is from the formal judgment, not the reasons.¹
So a respondent who is content
with the formal judgment need do nothing except give argument. If he or she wants the formal
judgment varied, then a cross-appeal is necessary. A cross-appeal is only appropriate where
the respondent asks for a different disposition of the litigation, viewed in real-life practical
consequences for that party. Dissatisfaction with the reasons for decision does not sufﬁce. A
respondent can give additional arguments to support the trial decision without cross-appealing.²
A cross-appeal does not institute a new proceeding, and merely gives notice to prevent
surprise. So if it would sufﬁce but is given late, and if there is no prejudice, again likely the court
would cure the delay.³
The tests for extending time are the same as for a late main appeal.⁴
A company called itself an “interested non-party” and the notice of appeal said it was “not party
to appeal”. On appeal it ﬁled a factum seeking relief. That switch of status should not have been
attempted, but the order under appeal did affect the legal interests of the company, and had there
been an application to one justice of appeal to add the company as a true party to the appeal, it
would likely have succeeded.⁵
Though the Rules do not provide for conditional cross-appeals, the court will give affect
to a cross-appellant’s statement that his appeal will not be pursued in certain events, if they
materialize.⁶
Rule 14.6 does not apply to an appeal to a judge from a taxing ofﬁcer.⁷
Giving the respondent any relief under one head would have required changing the formal
judgment, but there was no cross appeal. Almost four years had gone by, so it was far too late to
cross appeal or to extend time to do so. Title of the land had been held up, and prejudice should
be presumed.⁸
See also R.14.1 n.

DIVISION 2
THE APPEAL PROCESS
Subdivision 1
Starting an Appeal or Cross Appeal
How to start an appeal
14.7 Appeals must be started
(a) where an enactment or these rules give a right of appeal, by ﬁling a
notice of appeal under rule 14.8 [Filing a notice of appeal], or
(b) where permission to appeal must be obtained, by applying for
permission under rule 14.44 [Application for permission to appeal],
and if permission is granted, by then ﬁling a notice of appeal under
rule 14.8 [Filing a notice of appeal].
[Alta. Reg.41/14]
Information Note
The general right to appeal is found in rule 14.4 [Right to appeal], specific rights to appeal
are found in various statutes. Rule 14.5 [Appeals only with permission] and some statutes
require permission to appeal in some cases.

14.10 (filing with King's Bench)

Someone never a party to the civil proceedings involved, cannot ﬁle a notice of appeal; if he
does it is a nullity and he has no status. And if the person ﬁling for someone else is not a lawyer,
then that is bad too.¹

14.8(1) In this rule, “date of decision” means the later of
(a) the date that the judgment, order or other decision being appealed is
made, or
(b) if reasons are given after a judgment, order or other decision being
appealed is made, the date the reasons are issued.
A. Computing Time
1. References and History
On time limits to seek permission to appeal, see R.14.5(1) n. E.

1 As the case law and legislation in Alberta make clear. However, if the person named has legal counsel by

14-56
R.14.8(1) PART 14: APPEALS
In Alberta, time to ﬁle and serve an appeal¹
from the Court of King's Bench used to run from
when the order or judgment appealed from was formally entered and served²
(unless service
was not required). Not any more; now the time runs from pronouncement, or when the decision is
made.³
Entry of the formal order in the trial court, or lack of entry, no longer is relevant to compute
the time.⁴
2. When Time Starts to Run
A decision that someone is guilty of contempt of court, before any decision on penalty, is a
“decision” under R.14.8 and so can be appealed, and time to appeal guilt starts running. Separate
appeals from the guilt and the penalty are possible; that a single appeal is also possible, does not
stop time running from the ﬁnding of guilt. Contempt proceedings are often bifurcated between
the two.⁵
However, for appeals from many tribunals, time may run from notice of the decision, which
starts when the notice reaches the appellant’s address or counsel, even if the appellant never
personally got it.⁶
Federal statutes also often make time to appeal run from pronouncement of the decision or
reasons, without waiting for entry.⁷
Pronouncement does not usually occur until the party wishing
to appeal has got notice of the judgment or order,⁸
i. e. notice of what the decision is, whether the
reasons are oral, written, or non-existent.
There are many decided cases on computing the time.⁹
Formerly sometimes it was said
not desirable to ﬁle an appeal before the formal order is entered, but now doing that is usually
necessary, as time runs from pronouncement, and will usually expire before entry.¹⁰

14-57
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.8(1)
Rule 14.8(2) (a) (ii) sets a 10-day time limit for ﬁling a notice of appeal after getting permission
to appeal, which limit probably starts running once the result (permission) is announced, even if
reasons are to follow.¹
If there is no express statutory time period for an appeal (here to a subdivision and development
appeal board), nevertheless time does run. The statutory time starts to run (expire) when the
interested party knew, or should have known, that the event to be appealed had occurred, such
as a letter taking a clear position on the time. Merely seeing construction going on may not be
enough to know; it depends on the context.²
Rule 14.8(1) (b) implies that when time for appeal runs from reasons, those must be reasons
for the decision appealed, not reasons for some other decision in the case. That has in mind a
decision with reasons, “to follow”. But reasons later for costs or refusing to vary costs, are not the
same as reasons for the substantive decision.³
Asking the judge to reconsider does not extend the time to appeal, nor start it running afresh,
whether or not the trial judge was functus and could change his or her decision.⁴
If there is both a preliminary ruling and a later substantive decision (both in the Court of King's
Bench or the same tribunal) and the procedure in the Court of King's Bench is applicable, an
appeal can be ﬁled from the later substantive decision (within the time for that) and can complain
of the preliminary ruling (if it affected the ﬁnal substantive decision).⁵
3. When Time Stops Running
Time before there is an effective appeal continues to run even longer: until the notice of appeal
is served. Lawyers often forget that it is not enough to ﬁle an appeal in time; one must also
serve the notice of appeal within the time for appealing. But it may be easier to get service time
extended than to extend the time to ﬁle the appeal.⁶
However, if the time is set by statute, which
says nothing about extending time, then late service means there is no appeal.⁷
The presumptive appeal period under R.14.8(2) (a) (iii) is “one month” from pronouncement.⁸
But R.14.4(5) speaks of “30 days” to make the Rules consistent with s.21(3) of the Divorce Act.
The party alleging that an appeal is a nullity because it was not ﬁled properly (the respondent
to the appeal) has the onus of proof, because of the presumption of regularity.⁹
4. Effect of Late Appeal
The right to appeal and the procedure needed to appeal are different topics. A right to appeal
(even in a statute) does not bar a requirement (even in a Rule of Court) that that be done within
a certain time limit.¹⁰

R.14.8(1) PART 14: APPEALS
If an appeal is late and time is not extended, the appeal will be struck out.¹
Especially if it was
ﬁled well after the time limit for appealing, and no reason to extend the time is given, and indeed
no motion to extend it has been made.²
Occasionally, where to ﬁle the appeal is a problem.³
If there were two hearings and two orders, one substantive, and one on costs, and only the
former was appealed, to amend the notice of appeal later after time to appeal has expired, to
include the second order on costs, an application to extend time would be needed also.⁴
B. Extending Time
This Rule is only about time to appeal, not about other time limits once an appeal is properly
begun. For example, on time to ﬁle the record, see R.14.18.
1. Power to Extend
If the time to appeal or serve the notice is set by a statute, then no one can extend it.⁵
Not
even by one day.⁶
Unless of course the statute expressly gives a power to extend that time. Or if
another statute gives power to extend the time.⁷
Time under the Bankruptcy and Insolvency Act
can also be extended.⁸

other enactment or by any rule or decision of the Tribunal. But the same Act also said that there is no right of
appeal if proper documents were not ﬁled within the speciﬁed time limit. The latter provision had a footnote
stating that the former provision allowed a time extension. The two statutory provisions should be read to
make them consistent and allow a time extension. The provision allowing time extensions began “When a
matter before the Appeal Tribunal. . . ” There “before” has a broad meaning, and just means with reference to,
or sight or notice: Noskey v. Métis Settlements App. Tribunal 2023 ABCA 195, Edm 2103 0237 AC (Jun
19) (¶’s 31-44).
8 Though it is sometimes said that special circumstances are needed to extend the time to appeal in bankruptcy,
the tests there are much like the usual tests for extending time: Kubota Can. v. Case Cr. (Re DCD Ind. ) 2004
ABCA 41, 346 AR 166. One case was only late by one or two days, the appeal was arguable, and an initial
receiving order had not been appealed, but could have been appealed as of right. It was irrelevant that this
was an appeal from a refusal to review it, not from the original receiving order: Re Koska (Koska v. Alta.
Treas. Br. ) 2002 ABCA 138, 303 AR 230, leave den (SCC 18 Sep ’03).

14-59
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.8(1)
Nor can anyone shorten a statutory time to appeal.¹
Unless of course the statute itself gives
that power to abridge, which the Divorce Act does, for example.²
One cannot appeal refusal to extend time to appeal in a criminal case, as the Criminal Code
does not provide for that. But the Code does allow a renewed application to one judge to extend
the time, if new conditions or new evidence has emerged.³
The extra evidence here was far from
new, and no error emerged. Second request to extend time was denied.
However, if the time to ﬁle and serve an appeal is set by the Rules of Court, then 1968 R.548
let the court extend the time, before or after it expired. Now the applicable Rules are Rr.1.4(2) (h)
and 13.5(2).
If there is no statutory right to bring an appeal of this type, then the court or a judge cannot
extend the time for ﬁling the appeal.⁴
The court will not extend time to appeal without an actual application for that relief.⁵
2. Tests to Extend Time to Appeal
(a) General Approach
It takes weighty reasons to extend the time to appeal.⁶
Time to appeal should not be extended
lightly.⁷
Time extensions should be very rare.⁸
Especially if the delay is some years.⁹
Expiry of time
to appeal is important, for it lets the winner in the ﬁrst court plan his or her affairs.¹⁰
The public
also has an interest in promptness, so as not to waste judicial resources on non-compliance with
straight forward time limits.¹¹
Promptness is very important to society because timely decision is.¹²
Traditionally, the appellant who did not ﬁle an appeal in time had to meet three tests, often
called the Cairns tests:¹³

Timely intent to appeal, indeed giving notice of that intent, is of importance in a motion for
extension of time to appeal.¹
But timely instruction to one's counsel is not material?²
These tests have been applied to criminal appeals also.³
(b) Evidence and Explanations
The appellant needs direct evidence for a vital element like timely intent, not mere assertion.⁴
Many cases also require a reasonable explanation for the failure to act sooner.⁵
A good
explanation for the delay is needed.⁶
An extension of time to appeal after the respondent
applies to quash removes the lateness of the appeal as a ground to quash, but the
respondent who applied to quash is entitled to the costs of the application.⁷
Especially if a
court ofﬁcial had warned of the tardiness.⁸
Error in calculating the time is not enough?⁹

to understand the applicable procedures is not a reasonable excuse, especially if due diligence

especially as preparing and ﬁling and serving the papers to appeal to
the Court of Appeal does not take much energy, nor long concentration.⁷
Great delay in seeking the extension may also be a bar.⁸
Delay in applying to extend time,
after learning the problem, can also weigh against the appellant.⁹
Lack of any question of general
principle may be relevant too.¹⁰

R.14.8(1) PART 14: APPEALS
Those “exceptional cases” tend to be cases where the merits of the appeal are hard to weigh,
but the delay is short.¹
If the appeal is ﬁled only a few days late, and there is no prejudice, very
often the time is extended.²
Even if there was clear error in reading the Rules.³
In civil cases, a
brief delay in serving a notice of appeal which has been ﬁled in time, is often excused.⁴
The topic
is extensively discussed, and the authorities (mostly criminal) are reviewed, with discussion of
the respondent’s strategic silence.⁵
One decision where the Cairns tests were not met, extended
time because one issue of general law was unclear and important, but the appeal was conﬁned
to that issue.⁶
Delay caused by reliance on assurances or waivers by the opponent may sufﬁce.⁷
Acquiescence
by the respondent may be such a special circumstance.⁸
And indeed, some recent cases say that the traditional Cairns criteria themselves are not
hard-and-fast rules.⁹
But one case reviews exhaustively the precedents from the Court of Appeal,
including one often not cited, and concludes that the six tests in Cairns are rigid, though either
lack of an element, or satisfaction of all of them, can be bypassed by the court or a judge in truly
exceptional circumstances.¹⁰
(d) Miscellaneous
A party attended the Registry on the last day to ﬁle an appeal, but technical problems prevented
him ﬁling the appeal until the next day, one day too late. A judge extended time to appeal, as the
party genuinely intended to appeal in time and explained the delay.¹¹
An appeal from one justice of appeal refusing a time extension needs leave, and must meet
the tougher tests for that, such as a question of general importance.¹²
A chambers judge’s decision
whether to extend time to appeal is discretionary, and so reviewed on appeal on a standard of
reasonableness and can only be upset on the usual tests.¹³

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.8(1)
Prejudice is relevant only if it comes from the delay and would not have occurred from any
extension.¹
On proof of prejudice or no prejudice, see the Tole case.²
Many steps relying on the
order now sought to be belatedly appealed, may bar extension.³
The applicant must account for
the delay.⁴
Many cases on breaches of the Rules in effect divorce a client from his or her lawyer, so that
the client does not suffer from the lawyer’s faults, but a recent decision rejects that approach here,
especially after months of inaction.⁵
The usual tests for extending time to appeal also apply to late cross-appeals, especially if the
trial judge varies the trial judgment before entry, or the main appeal is ﬁled before judgment is
entered.⁶
3. Divorce Cases
The Divorce Act s.21(4) requires “special grounds” to extend time to appeal. “Very special
circumstances” can be satisﬁed by extensive medical conditions.⁷
In a divorce case, even one day too late may be fatal and not cured, if the appeal has no merit
at all.⁸
Ignorance of the statutory time limit is not enough excuse after several months’ delay, if there
are no other special grounds (especially under the Divorce Act), and even more so if the respondent
is likely to have arranged her affairs around the order appealed from.⁹
Mere ignorance of that law
by the losing party’s solicitor, and intent in time to appeal, is not enough to extend time to appeal
after several months’ delay. Especially if evidence is not offered to meet the other traditional tests
for time extension. The Divorce Act expressly calls for “special grounds” to extend time.¹⁰
Budgeting
on the strength of the King's Bench maintenance order is likely after some months, and so would
likely be prejudice to the respondent.¹¹
One could not combine the 30 days in the Divorce Act with service, as the start date from
previous R.505. Under the Divorce Act, that part was late. Under the Rules, the matrimonial
property part was late.¹²
Almost all the Cairns tests barred an extension of time, where the
appellant swore that he did not want to decide to appeal or not, until he saw the costs.¹³

14-66
R.14.8(1) PART 14: APPEALS
4. Examples
Lack of arguable merit is often a ground to refuse time extension for the proposed appeal.¹
Lack of funds is no excuse for a party who later ﬁled an appeal in person, at very little cost.²
Timely intent to appeal is not proved by showing a previous attempt at a different remedy such
as judicial review.³
Sufﬁcient excuses for tardiness included waiting for Legal Aid (coupled with deadline to order
transcripts.⁴
For an unusual case of a year’s delay in dissenting plaintiffs wishing to attack court approval
of settlement of a class action, see the R. T. case.⁵
Long out of time, one cannot get leave to appeal from earlier orders, simply because one has
got permission to appeal from a later order, the three being substantially the same.⁶
That one
could not properly run one’s new appeal without getting permission to appeal on the earlier ones,
is a reason to deny such leave. Once someone has exhausted all appeals (or not appealed in
time), he or she cannot rely on late changes in the law, even constitutional law, as a ground for a
late appeal.⁷
Where the order to be appealed (an emergency protection order) only had 9 days more to
run, and it would be impossible to have the appeal heard and decided in 9 days, it would be futile
to allow an appeal (by extending time to appeal).⁸
But extension of time to appeal may involve no
prejudice to the respondent where various other times were extended or had no consequences.⁹
Estoppel is not available against such a deadline where the trustee in bankruptcy has a
statutory duty to perform (distributing assets) for the beneﬁt of creditors. Nor does a court of
equity’s power to relieve against penalties and forfeitures apply.¹⁰
If the time to appeal runs from service, and there is evidence that the appellant never saw the
entered order (until she got a copy from the court ﬁle), and if she moves promptly, then time to
appeal maybe easily extended.¹¹
An ex parte order extending time to appeal was upheld despite an application to set it aside,
as the new grounds were weak and would have led to no different result if given initially.¹²

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.8(2)
5. Conditions
It is fairly common to attach strict conditions to an order extending time.¹
There is discussion of
complex procedures on a motion to extend time to appeal which succeeded only on some issues
and was denied on other issues.²
14.8(2) An appellant must
(a) ﬁle with the Registrar a notice of appeal that meets the requirements
of rule 14.12 [Contents and format of notices of appeal and cross
appeal] and Form AP-1,
(i) within the time for commencing an appeal stated in an
enactment,
(ii) if the appellant is granted permission to appeal, within 10
days after the date permission is granted, or
(iii) if subclauses (i) and (ii) do not apply, within one month after
the date of decision,
and
(b) serve a ﬁled copy of the notice of appeal on every other party to the
appeal within the applicable time period mentioned in clause (a).
[Alta. Reg.41/14;128/15;61/23]
On electronic ﬁling, see Part C below. On computing the time and when it starts, see R.
14.8(1) n. A.2. Since all ﬁling is electronic now, the concept of ﬁling several copies disappears.
Presumably “a ﬁled copy” must mean one from the Court of Appeal registry, or other proof of ﬁling
before service. Filing before service has always been the Alberta practice, and this conﬁrms it.
A. Service
One must serve the notice of appeal on all the other parties to the suit affected by the appeal;
in practice it is best to serve all parties to the suit.³
An appeal is not a new proceeding, but the
continuation of the existing trial proceeding, so Rules on how to commence it (such as service on
a parent) do not automatically apply to serving a notice of appeal, and failure to do so is not fatal
if no prejudice results.⁴
A notice of appeal was ﬁled but not served, and a motion to strike the appeal as late was not
responded to and the appellant did not turn up for that motion. He had been guilty of misconduct
throughout the litigation, and appeared still to be harassing or draining the respondent of assets,
and he had paid few of the costs earlier ordered. Given no excuse or explanation, the appeal was
struck.⁵

R.14.8(2) PART 14: APPEALS
Substitutional service is often needed, but if the afﬁdavit to get it (ex parte) is poor, later the
order and the service may be set aside after time to appeal has expired, thus maybe killing the
appeal, which may be very unjust, especially if the respondent knew about the appeal.¹
Neither the criminal appeal Rules nor the civil ones (which apply in cases not provided for)
mention service in another province of a notice of appeal. No permission thus to serve is required,
because a (Crown) criminal appeal is not commencement of new proceedings.²
The Judicature Act s.24(1) requires advance service of a Notice of Constitutional Question,
only if the constitutional validity of an enactment is being questioned by the appeal, maybe not if
the attack is only on a certain decision during proceedings under legislation.³
B. Adding Parties
See also R.14.57.
Anyone named in the notice of appeal is a party to the appeal and can ﬁle a factum, especially
one whose interests are affected, even a “respondent” who wishes to support the appeal or have
the decision under appeal upset.⁴
The Rules deﬁne narrowly who is a “respondent” to an appeal (basically those named), but a
different question is who should have been so named. A party was represented and active in the
application in the court below clearly had a legal interest. As they applied promptly, it was just and
convenient to add them. Their interests might well differ and they should not have to rely on the
arguments selected by counsel for their co-defendant.⁵
A party is proper and necessary to an appeal where the issue is an injunction and the
proposed injunction would name and affect that person directly. Or where that person is a party to
the lawsuit appealed from (given the deﬁnition of “respondent” in British Columbia. Or where the
injunction was to cover all lawyers in a law ﬁrm, and the person concerned is one of those lawyers.
Who is a party to a class action is not a good analogy.⁶
Does every party to the King's Bench suit have a right to be heard on the appeal?⁷
The Alberta Court of Appeal has inherent power to add parties to an appeal,⁸
especially where
none of the existing parties wishes to support one side of the appeal, and interests affected are
not represented.⁹
The court may let a non-party likely to be affected join as a respondent to the

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.8(2)
appeal.¹
The tests for adding a party to existing litigation are reviewed.²
The Municipal Government Act says that all those affected should get notice of an application
to appeal from a Subdivision and Development Appeal Board. When that is a neighbor, no one
else can adequately represent her interests, so she should be added if she asks for that.³
It takes (in effect) an extension of time to appeal, to substitute a non-party as the appellant
after the time to appeal has expired, and so the usual four Cairns tests to extend time must be
satisﬁed.⁴
But a new appellant cannot be added where time to appeal has expired and there is no
power to extend that time limit.⁵
If the counsel for a party seeking to be added is not ready to argue
the appeal, adding that new party is tantamount to postponing the appeal, so then one must ask
whether addition is necessary, e. g. whether a later separate appeal would sufﬁce.⁶
An added party
may be subjected to stricter time limits.⁷
Where the alleged appellant denied having authorized the appeal, the lawyer ﬁling it was
not added as a party, as his interests were touched only incidentally, and much more evidence
would be needed to decide his rights.⁸
An appeal is from the formal judgment, not the reasons, so
correcting an error in the reasons is not a ground for a non-party to join an appeal or intervene.⁹
Only rarely should a non-party be added in order to oppose a motion for permission to appeal.¹⁰
In appeals from utilities regulators, the Responsible Energy Development Act gives statutory
standing to persons directly and adversely affected, and obliges the regulator to hear them at
the hearing. What is a legal interest is hard to deﬁne, but is not strictly conﬁned to demonstrated
proprietary or contractual interest. A municipality has broad powers and responsibilities, and
an applicant before the Board often has a statutory mandate to protect public safety, and it
represented the broad interests of its constituents. On any application for leave to appeal, notice
must be given to “parties affected by the appeal”. So it should be added as a party.¹¹

Legislation on engineers’ discipline says that an appeal is to be against the Appeal Board.
Where the Appeal Board was the only respondent named, because it is a neutral body, it asked to
have the Investigative Committee of the professional association added as a respondent. Without
this extra party, the court would get very little argument on the merits. That strongly favored adding
the Investigative Committee.¹
After the death of the Queen and accession of the present King, the Acting Chief Justice of

No reﬁling of documents already ﬁled is necessary. See further an October 13, 2022 Notice to the
Profession and Public by the Acting Chief Justice, on the Court of Appeal website.
C. Miscellaneous
If legislation provides for ﬁling an appeal in the appellate court, ﬁling it only in the trial court
does not start an appeal in time.²
Staff shortages led to closing the Registry front counters, with permission given to send to the
Registry papers or requests scanned electronically by fax, or at a drop box near the Registry. This
began in March 2023 and it is hoped it will end in May 2023.
Now ordinarily all documents ﬁled in the Court of Appeal must be ﬁled electronically, not in hard
copy. (In exceptional circumstances, a case management ofﬁcer may allow ﬁling hard copies. )
The Rules of Court and the Court’s Consolidated Practice Direction (Part F) were amended
accordingly in early 2023. Often a copy of the document must be served on other parties, and it is
the ﬁled version which is to be served. Concepts like multiple copies, or backwards-facing pages,
are gone now. But colored covers of documents remain. Page numbering must start with the cover
as page 1. There are formatting instructions in the Court of Appeal Management Systems (v.2, p.
2-10), and in the Consolidated Practice Direction. Someone not used to this system would be well
advised to get advice or help, or arrange to submit a draft or test version, to see if it is acceptable,
all well before any ﬁling deadline. Again the two case management ofﬁcers would be a great help
in such situations. (Electronic ﬁling of transcripts of oral evidence has existed for a number of
years, and should produce no novelty. )
An appeal may only beneﬁt or bind those parties who appealed or were appealed against,
and res judicata binds other parties to the original judgment, unless the result is indivisible
and executing both decisions is impossible. If one party is added to the appellate proceedings,
without further steps the Court can bind him or her too.³
Where a trial judge has interpreted a will
incorrectly, but one set of beneﬁciaries affected by the interpretation has not appealed, the part of
the trial judgment about that set may not be altered.⁴
If one party appeals and another does not,
the Court of Appeal sometimes may amend or reverse the judgment totally, thus beneﬁtting also
those who did not appeal.⁵

municipalities have always been allowed to “intervene” in important hearings, to represent the interests of

14-72
R.14.10 PART 14: APPEALS

Related Provisions
3.69 (joinder) ; 3.71 (separating claims)

Though ordinarily there should be a separate appeal for each order to be contested, this Rule
recognizes exceptions where one combined appeal would be more efﬁcient, e. g. an appeal from
both a substantive order and a refusal to reconsider it.¹
It is impossible originally to combine appeals from two orders decided further apart than the
statutory time limit to appeal, and later applying two such appeals may well be a waste of money.²
In family litigation, if an interim order is appealed to the Court of Appeal and then the ﬁnal
order is made, an appeal from the ﬁnal order needs a fresh notice of appeal. The ﬁnal order is
more than conﬁrmation of the interim one.³
A Court of Appeal Case Management Ofﬁcer has power to settle disputed terms of an order
given. Giving ofﬁcers administrative non-substantive duties does not violate s.96 of the 1867
Constitution Act.⁴

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.12(1)

Related Provisions
14.1(2) (application of rules to cross appeals) ; 14.6 (cross appeals) ; 14.23(4) and 14.25(2)

(factum on cross appeal) ; 14.24(2) (cross appeal in fast track appeal) ; 14.32(1) (argument of
cross appeal); 14.66(2) (discontinuance) ; 14.81 (service)
A notice of cross-appeal which does not seek to have the trial judgment varied is improper and
must be struck out.¹
To cross-appeal one must be a party to the lawsuit on the appeal.²
Former criminal appeal Rule 853 required notice of a cross-claim in a sentence appeal, and
a letter was not enough. A formal notice with particulars must be ﬁled and served. And failure to
argue the topic in the trial court may bar the claim in the Court of Appeal, especially if the main
appellant (respondent on this cross topic) is self-represented, and the Crown has delayed in
raising the point, and the main sentence appeal needed leave.³
An administrative tribunal can change the decision below in favor of a respondent who has not
ﬁled any kind of cross-appeal.⁴

Notices of Appeal and Cross Appeal
Contents and format of notices of appeal
and cross appeal

must be in Form AP-2.
(2) A notice of appeal must contain the following information:
(a) the parties’ names, in the same order used in the style of cause in the
court appealed from, with an indication of the status of each on the
appeal and in the court appealed from;
(b) the name and ﬁle number used in the court appealed from;
(c) whether the whole or only part of the decision is appealed, and if
only part is appealed, which part;⁵
(d) whether the appeal is required by these rules to be, or it is anticipated
the appeal will be, managed as a fast track appeal;

R.14.12(3) PART 14: APPEALS

On the effect of naming someone as a respondent, see R.14.8(2) n. B, supra.
An Alberta notice of appeal gives notice that there is an appeal, and what is being appealed,
and of the contention that the court appealed to has jurisdiction, and of the relief claimed. The last
is the least important objective.¹
After a notice of appeal, other parties rely on the judgment under
appeal, at their peril.²
Subrule (2) (c) is often ignored. That is dangerous, since the notice of appeal in a civil case
need give no grounds (and the “issues” to be listed in the form of notice of appeal do not bind
or have the same legal status as grounds). The “information” part of a notice of appeal does not
govern what the appellant can or cannot put in his or her factum.³
To appeal the whole formal
judgment when one really quarrels with only one or two paragraphs of it, could lead to heavy
costs burdens.
Even in a criminal appeal, where the Rules called for grounds of appeal, omitting them from
the Notice to Appeal was not enough to strike out the appeal.⁴
If the notice appeals liability, it need not say expressly that it proposes to vary the trial costs.⁵
A proposed amendment was to give full details of the relief sought, and so did not enlarge the
scope of the appeal, and brought the Notice into compliance with R.14.12(2) (f).⁶
And whatever
the Notice of Appeal says about relief will not constrain the panel deciding the appeal as to what
relief is proper.⁷
If leave to appeal is needed and is granted on one issue only, any other issues put in the
Notice of Appeal are improper and may be struck out.⁸
(3) A notice of appeal or cross appeal must include
(a) where permission to appeal was required, particulars of or a copy of
the order granting permission to appeal, and
(b) particulars of or a copy of the judgment, order or other decision
being appealed. [Alta. Reg.41/14]

14-75
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.13(1)
Subdivision 3
Types of Appeals
Standard appeals
14.13(1) All appeals that are not fast track appeals are standard appeals.
(2) A case management ofﬁcer may direct that any appeal must or must not be
managed as a fast track appeal. [Alta. Reg.41/14]

Related Provisions
14.33 (scheduling standard appeals) ; 14.39 (directions from case management officer)

In an appeal over whether one counsel in a lawsuit had a conﬂict of interest, there was a ﬁght
over whether the appeal should be classed or treated as standard track or expedited. The test for
that turned on whether the issues on this appeal would settle substantive issues. One justice of
appeal held it would not, but somewhat modiﬁed the time limits.¹
Fast track appeals
14.14(1) Fast track appeals are appeals from a decision, or part of a decision,
that does not ﬁnally determine all or some signiﬁcant part of the substantive
rights in issue.
(2) Unless otherwise ordered, appeals from the following kinds of decisions are
to be dealt with as fast track appeals:
(a) a ruling by a judge made during a trial;
(b) a decision as to custody, access, guardianship, parenting time,
decision-making responsibility, contact or support in respect of a
child;
(c) a decision concerning support for a present or former spouse or
adult interdependent partner made without a trial being held;
(d) a decision concerning only costs or liability to pay fees or costs;
(e) a decision denying summary judgment.
(3) Unless otherwise ordered, appeals from the following kinds of decisions are
not fast track appeals:
(a) a decision granting summary judgment or striking out a statement
of claim, statement of defence or third party claim;
(b) a decision refusing to open a default judgment or a noting in default;
(c) a decision permitting or directing default judgment;
(d) a decision staying an action indeﬁnitely;
(e) a decision dismissing an action on procedural grounds;
(f) certiﬁcation of or failure to certify a class proceeding.
[Alta. Reg.41/14;128/15;23/21]

Footnote
1 McMullen v. Norton Rose & Co.2018 ABCA 299, Calg 1801 0163 AC (one JA).

R.14.15(1) PART 14: APPEALS

Related Provisions

The 2021 amendment to R.14.14 explains the types of custody, etc. order whose appeals are
to be fast tracked.
An appeal about a dispute between a lawyer and his client over the lawyer’s fee is probably
not an appeal “as to costs only”? “Costs” normally refers to payments between opposing parties.
And review of a lawyer’s contingency fee agreement is deﬁnitely not as to costs alone, as it raises
other issues of principle.¹
Subdivision 4
Appeal Record
Ordering the Appeal Record
14.15(1) Subject to rule 14.20 [Contents of Appeal Record - appeals from
tribunals], the appellant must
(a) within 10 days after ﬁling a notice of appeal, order or commence
preparation of the Appeal Record,
(b) order from Transcript Management Services or any other commercial
preparer a transcript of
(i) all oral evidence,
(ii) subject to subrule (iii), only such part of the argument as is
necessary to dispose of the appeal,
(iii) all oral argument in a chambers hearing, if that hearing did
not exceed one-half day, and
(iv) any oral reasons for the decision under appeal and for any
other ruling that will be an issue on the appeal,
and
(c) within 5 days after ordering the Appeal Record and transcripts, ﬁle a
copy of the order and serve a ﬁled copy on the respondent.
(2) Unless the Appeal Record is being prepared by the appellant, a copy of any
amendment to, or countermand of, the instructions to prepare the Appeal Record
or transcripts must be ﬁled and served on the respondent within a reasonable
time after the amendment is made or the countermand given. [Alta. Reg.41/14]
Information Note
Transcripts of the oral argument at a trial are not required unless relevant, for example if a
dispute exists about whether a particular issue was raised, or whether some fact or issue
was conceded. Where formal reasons for judgment are issued by the court, transcripts of oral
reasons are generally not required.

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.15(2)

Related Provisions
14.28 (record before the Court) ; 14.70 (fresh evidence) ; 14.73 (curing non-compliance) ;

A. Need to Give to Court of Appeal the Reasons
On inadequate reasons by the judge appealed, see R.14.18(3) n.
Counsel sometimes fail to have the oral reasons in King's Bench recorded;¹
but they have a
duty to see they are recorded.²
The Court of Appeal has complained of failure to do that.³
Sometimes reasons are recorded and counsel do not have them transcribed. That also
has been criticized.⁴
That failure to transcribe led to costs personally payable by counsel to the
Registrar in some cases.⁵
If an appellant waits years, and then by the time the transcript is ﬁrst
ordered the shorthand has been thrown out, where facts are important to the case, the appeal
may be dismissed.⁶
A gap in the transcript i. e. words not reproduced nor recorded, is not enough to allow an
appeal. A serious possibility must be shown that the gap contained error or deprived the appellant
of a good ground of appeal.⁷
The Court of Appeal decided a criminal appeal. Counsel and the Court thought that no
extended reasons had been given on the critical voir dire, but it turned out they had, but were
not seen. The Court ordered the appeal reargued before a different panel, no formal judgment yet
being entered.⁸

R.14.16(1) PART 14: APPEALS
An exemption from the need to spend $570 and order a transcript of the chambers proceedings
now being appealed, was denied, even though the appellant had limited income, and no assets
of value. His evidence there was limited, and he did not swear to inability to raise the money,
especially as he had a few months to do so. The issues he was arguing did involve useful things in
the transcript. Knowing the full basis for the chambers judge’s decision would require a transcript.¹
A party who did not assert that she was impecunious claimed that there was discrimination
barred by the Charter, where the transcript management authorities wanted a deposit from self-
represented people, but not litigants ordering the transcript through a lawyer. The court gave no
effect to that submission; there was no evidence of any attempt to have that policy waived and
was no suggestion or evidence of any actual prejudice.²
A request that the government pay for
transcripts was rejected.³
B. Format
The successful appellant lost half his usual fee for the appeal record for not disclosing in
the Table of Contents where to ﬁnd the Master's reasons, and omitting the latest version of the
pleadings.⁴
A Court of Appeal should somehow record its reasons for judgment where it reverses a
trial judge, especially where the case involved questions of fact and the trial judge gave careful
reasons.⁵
C. Filing
Attaching to the notice of appeal being ﬁled, reasons of the applications judge and judge
below, and of a costs order, does not constitute ﬁling an appeal record either in form or content.
And if the appellant never tried to cure the omission, and insisted there was no omission, that
weighs against a time extension.⁶
Factum citations of the oral reasons under appeal should be to the “F” series of page numbers
in the appeal record, not to transcript page numbers (which the Court of Appeal usually does not
have in hard copy).⁷
Many counsel make that mistake.
On how to prove that documents in evidence were “redacted”, see the Roy case.⁸

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.16(1)
(b) Part 2 — Final Documents, and

A. Times and Copies
Ordinarily all documents ﬁled in the Court of Appeal now must be ﬁled electronically, not
in hard copy. (In exceptional circumstances, a case management ofﬁcer may allow ﬁling hard
copies. ) The Rules of Court and the Court’s Consolidated Practice Direction (Part F) were
amended accordingly. Often a copy of the document must be served on other parties, and it is the
ﬁled version which is to be served. Concepts like multiple copies, or backwards-facing pages, are
gone now. But colored covers of documents remain. Page numbering must start with the cover as
page 1. There are formatting instructions in the Court of Appeal Management Systems (v.2, p.2-
10), and in the Consolidated Practice Direction. Someone not used to this system would be well
advised to get advice or help, or arrange to submit a draft or test version, to see if it is acceptable,
all well before any ﬁling deadline. Again the two case management ofﬁcers would be a great help
in such situations. (Electronic ﬁling of transcripts of oral evidence has existed for a number of
years, and should produce no novelty. )
The electronic system lets a whole document be ﬁled in a sealed format, but does not allow
sealing only part of it.¹
Time deadlines for ﬁling the appeal record, and extending them, are reviewed.²
A last-minute attempt to get leave for unspeciﬁed amendments to appeal records failed, partly
because it was likely to delay a peremptory hearing date.³
An appeal struck for failure to ﬁle the appeal digest within the deadline was not restored.⁴
Once
an appeal has been ﬁled, delay in ﬁling the record or factum is not excused by difﬁculty for a lay
appellant. The Court of Appeal website even tells how to do this, and court staff can be (and were
here) asked for advice. The appellant never ordered the transcript nor took any other preparatory
steps.⁵
Filing an insufﬁcient number of copies of the appeal record was not bad enough to strike out a
(criminal) appeal.⁶
And now in Alberta, in practice only an electronic version is ﬁled.
B. Selecting Evidence to Reproduce⁷
Parties are to ﬁle with the Court of Appeal as appeal record (Extracts) only those parts of the
trial record needed on appeal, not all the trial record. A wholesale dump is unacceptable. There is
no risk to omitting something which later turns out to be useful. That task and selection starts with
the appellant. Parties to the appeal, and others wishing to be made interveners, should not ﬁle
bulky materials of elusive admissibility, relevance and materiality, or about issues not likely to be

R.14.16(1) PART 14: APPEALS
contested. A request motion to intervene will usually produce fresh evidence, but that is not part
of the appeal record for the appeal itself, and not admissible there.¹
Before the 2008 Rule amendments, most appeal records reproduced far too much evidence.
Where documents reproduced were probably unnecessary for an appeal, the appellant may lose
costs for the unnecessary ones.²
If the respondent had insisted that they be included in the appeal
record, the appellant’s costs may include reproducing them.³
But one cannot assume that parts
of the appeal book were unnecessary just because the factums or judgment did not cite them, or
because the respondent was not called on to argue.⁴
In setting costs, one should not duplicate
disbursements for appeal books and the fee for the work in producing them.⁵
Correspondence and a table of concordance are not evidence or exhibits, and should not
be in the Appeal Book. They are illicit fresh evidence and should be struck.⁶
Material in one
party’s Extracts of Key Evidence which was not before the judge under appeal, was disregarded,
there being no application to admit new evidence.⁷
One cannot smuggle fresh evidence into an
appeal without leave, by putting it into the book of authorities, which is for legal authorities.⁸
Even if the smuggler tries to call it social or legislative facts,⁹
or what evidence might later be
adduced,¹⁰
or evidence ﬁled for the motion for leave to appeal or later motions.¹¹
Nor should there
be correspondence or evidence which was not before the judge appealed from.¹²
Self-represented
litigants were told how to apply for leave to adduce new evidence on appeal. They ﬁled such a
motion, but then in their Extracts they included other even newer evidence not in their pending
motion. They were ordered to ﬁle promptly a new motion to adduce that newest evidence, and the
offending extracts were ordered segregated and sealed.¹³

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.16(2)
But the appeal record should (if the appellant describes it) contain a notice of motion to settle
the formal order and reconsider costs, especially if the appeal was ﬁled before the formal order
was entered.¹
A judge may order that bulky documents be part of the record and before the court, but not
reproduced in the appeal record.²
Of course the post-2008 “extracts” system encourages that.
See R.14.27.
But if the reasons are reproduced, the appellant also needs to put a transcript of all the
chambers argument in King's Bench chambers in the appeal record, if argument was a half day or
less.³
Where a respondent got leave to ﬁle a supplementary appeal record containing the written
arguments which had been ﬁled in the court appealed from, there should be no costs for it.⁴
One cannot evade page limits for factums by incorporating by reference briefs earlier ﬁled in
King's Bench, so Appeal Records cannot contain such briefs if that is the aim.⁵
(2) The appellant must serve a ﬁled copy of the Appeal Record and the Transcripts
on every other party to the appeal.
(3) The Appeal Record and Transcripts must be prepared promptly and ﬁled and
served forthwith after they are prepared,
(a) in a fast track appeal, not later than one month from the date on
which the notice of appeal was ﬁled, or
(b) in a standard appeal, not later than 4 months from the date on which
the notice of appeal was ﬁled,
or the appeal will be struck by the Registrar. [Alta. Reg.41/14;61/23]
If an appeal is started promptly but then a step to perfect it is inadvertently missed, it is not that
hard to cure the defect, especially if the appellant moves quickly to ﬁx the problem, and no harm
has been done. The appellant is not thwarting the overall objective of the Rules.⁶
However, the
tardy appellant seeks an indulgence and usually must pay the costs of the application to restore.⁷
The appellants failed to provide electronic transcripts pursuant to R.14.16(1) (c), so the
costs fee under Schedule C item 19 was reduced by $500, and disbursements for photocopying
transcripts were denied.⁸

Contents of Appeal Record – standard appeals
14.18(1) The Appeal Record must meet the requirements of rule 14.21 [Format of
Appeal Record] and must contain the following:
(a) a Table of Contents for Parts 1 and 2, which must list separately each
document and show the page number where the document can be
found;
(b) Part 1 — Pleadings, which must contain the relevant pleadings in the
action in chronological order, including
(i) the relevant pleadings, but the last version only of any
pleading that was amended before trial,
(ii) any amendments to pleadings made at trial, and
(iii) if the appeal concerns a decision arising from an application,
a copy of the application;
[Alta. Reg.41/14;85/16;136/22;61/23]
There has to be a way to put before the Court of Appeal accessible copies of the pleadings,
evidence, and decision from the court or tribunal being appealed. That collection of such copies
used to be called the “appeal book” and is now called the “appeal record. ”
There have always been some compulsory contents of that material, such as the originating
document, the reasons for decision being appealed, and the notice of appeal. That Part of the
Rules has changed very little, and under the current Rules those items are still in a red-covered
volume (if hard copies are ﬁled, which they ordinarily are not in 2021. )
How much of the evidence to reproduce is not ﬁxed by the Rules. Before 2008, the parties
were encouraged to agree on that. Failing agreement, a judge would ﬁx the contents.
Now all the oral evidence must be transcribed (which in practice also occurred before 2008).
As before, it will be ﬁled in electronic form. The written evidence is automatically part of the ofﬁcial
record, but each counsel reproduces only the parts which he or she chooses: see R.14.27. (And
it must be ﬁled electronically. )

Unspeciﬁed amendments to an Appeal Book and facta were denied. What was sought would
overrule a panel’s earlier refusal of an adjournment of the appeal in a long-delayed interlocutory
criminal matter, as a panel would hear it (at best) just before the scheduled appeal argument
date.¹
A Saskatchewan appeal book should not contain material not part of the record in the
proceedings appealed, even if the court gives an order allowing “new evidence”. The test is what
is in the record before the lower court, not whether the material is mentioned in the Rule.²
On failure to order a transcript promptly, and routine destruction of shorthand, see the White
case.³
At end of its appeals, the Alberta Court of Appeal usually destroys all extra copies of the

Leaving out compulsory materials may lead to a costs penalty.⁵

The court’s discretion over time extensions is broad. That includes Case Management Ofﬁcers.

2. prospect that the appeal will move ahead
3. any prejudice to the other party
4. whether the appeal is frivolous.¹
The delay was not knowing that the transcript needed approval before it or the Record could be

No ground to fear further delay therefore. No prejudice to the respondent.

14-85
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.18(3)
A. Duty to Give Reasons¹
1. Reference
On evidence of bias in a judge’s reasons, see R.13.1n.
2. Limited Duty
Underlying much of the discussion below is the fundamental principle that an appeal is from
the formal order or judgment, not the reasons.²
Reasons for decision are highly desirable for many purposes, especially if the law is unsettled
or the evidence conﬂicts.³
The trial judge has a duty to give reasons, but mere failure to do so
is probably not itself a ground of appeal.⁴
It is not ground to reverse a civil decision that the trial
judge poorly expressed herself or himself in giving reasons. There is no general and invariable
duty on a trial judge to give reasons, especially if the evidence clearly supports a ﬁnding, or its
basis is obvious.⁵
But otherwise, lack of reasons may justify (be part of the reasons for) reversal
on appeal.⁶
Inadequate reasons raise the question of whether the reasoning arc was clear from
the judgment and the evidence, or if lack of reasons allows reasoned belief that the justice (or
Applications Judge forgot, ignored, or misconceived material evidence.⁷

formal reasons), see MTM Commr’l. Tr. v. Statesman Riverside Quays 2022 ABCA 328, Calg 2201 0080

case and its live issues require, and is not ﬁxed or abstract. Lack of reasons is a ground to upset a conviction
only where the reasons are so sparse as to foreclose meaningful appellate review. If the evidence on a
key issue is confused and contradictory, reasons are important. They need not be full enough to let a court
of appeal retry the case, nor to answer every argument at trial, nor to show that the trial judge had all the
evidence in mind or had considered all of it. In the vast majority of cases, the trial judge’s pathway to the
result will be clear; often the record itself will make the path patent: R. v. Dinardo, supra (criminal case). See
also Rockall v. Rockall 2010 ABCA 278, 490 AR 135 (Sep 27) (¶’s 21, 26, 27, 31).
7 Canuck v. Yangara, supra (¶’s 52-59).

14-86
R.14.18(3) PART 14: APPEALS
3. Functional Test
The trial judge’s reasons must be adequate for appellate review. The test is functional, and
the reasons must be looked at in the context of the record.¹
A new hearing may be ordered where
the reasons do not deal with the losing party’s (appellant’s) arguments.²
Longer reasons are
necessary if the legal issue is unsettled or confused, and contradictory evidence must be resolved
on a key issue. Important is whether the Court of Appeal can ﬁnd a rational chain of analysis to tell
why the trial judge reached his or her decision.³
Brevity of reasons is not a ground for appellate
reversal, so long as the reasons show why the judge decided, give a basis for appellate scrutiny,
and make the necessary fact ﬁndings.⁴
A trial judge must give reasons why double costs are not
awarded when an offer is beaten.⁵
But the reasons need not be very full.⁶
A chambers judge need
not address expressly each and every argument raised by the party who later appeals.⁷
Reasons are the ﬁrst and main way to show a reasonable decision. They must provide a
transparent and intelligible justiﬁcation. They need not be perfect. Not reciting all the arguments,
statutes, case law or other details, is not on its own a basis to upset the decision on appeal,
especially if there is specialized knowledge and demonstrated experience and expertise. The
decision must be justiﬁed in relation to the laws and facts relevant.⁸
A King's Bench justice has to use the correct standard of review if he or she hears an appeal
from a tribunal, but the justice need not name the standard.⁹
On appeal, a trial judge’s reasons should be read as a whole and reasonably generously, not
contra proferentem, especially not presuming that he or she has made basic legal errors.¹⁰

14-87
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.18(3)
In the Court of Appeal, the standard of review is not entirely clear for whether the reasons for
decision of the tribunal or court appealed from are adequate; is it deferential?¹
So-called reasons by a tribunal giving only the issue and the result, with no analysis, and
no reference to the statutory test, are not sufﬁcient.²
Reasons of a tribunal whose members are
not legally trained get a more liberal reading.³
Just repeating the statutory language and making
peremptory conclusions without explanation, attracts no appellate deference.⁴
But cutting and pasting one party’s written argument may be insufﬁcient.⁵
A chambers judge's
reasons adopted all the winning party's submissions on the facts and the law, but did not give
any independent analysis on either, nor show how he applied the facts to the law, nor consider or
explain the arguments or reasons for rejecting the losing party's submissions. This did not meet
the 3 modern tests for adequacy of reasons, so the matter was sent back to King's Bench to
reconsider.⁶
A Subdivision and Development Appeal Board must give reasons. The policy includes the
right to enjoy property, protected by the Alberta Bill of Rights and the Canada Bill of Rights.
This duty prevents arbitrariness and improves the quality of decision making: will the proposed
decision write? And shows if a further appeal is advisable, shows that the tribunal understands the
questions and the general thrust of each side, and explains clearly why one side won. An ordinary
reasonable person should be able to understand the words.⁷
4. Imagining Other Reasons
If the reasons given by a specialist non-legal tribunal are inadequate, the court should also
consider what reasons could be offered to support the decision.⁸
5. Time of Giving
A proposition given by a trial judge only during argument is not part of his or her reasons
for decision (unless made so later, e. g. by incorporation). In argument, judges often advance

R.14.18(3) PART 14: APPEALS
propositions to elicit responses. Short of abuse, there is no reason to limit such probing.¹
Discussions with counsel during argument are not reasons.²
What later corrections or additions to a transcript of earlier oral reasons for decision are
proper, under the Court of King's Bench usual practice, are listed.³
If the judge thinks that
additional reasons or content are needed, or actual changes to what was said are needed, then
supplementary reasons should be signed and ﬁled. In this case, since some of those changes
were small and hard to read by cross-reference, they were inserted in square brackets in the
original version.⁴
An appeal court will ordinarily not look at reasons which were ﬁrst given pending appeal.⁵
Delay in giving reasons after announcing the result can lead the Court of Appeal to disregard the
reasons or even upset the result.⁶
But writing reasons after an appeal is ﬁled is not always wrong or harmful.⁷
B. Flaws in Evidence Filed
Parties are asked to review and rely upon the Mandatory Requirements and Check/Return
Forms and the Information Sheets, which are on the Court of Appeal’s website under “Registry/
Filing Information”.⁸
An appellant did not want to have to pay for transcripts, but a judge refused to dispense with
transcripts⁹
nor allow the appellant or his friend to make homemade transcripts, with or without
ﬁling with the court about 21 hours of audio recordings as backup. Whoever prepares a transcript
should be within the control of the court. One of the alternatives which the appellants suggested
(and the judge rejected) was having the transcripts made offshore somewhere.¹⁰

14-89
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.18(3)
Until the 1960’s, all of the appeal book was set in printer’s type, or retyped on a typewriter, and
so always legible. Then photocopying became common. Now appellants fairly often tender appeal
records or Extracts containing illegible exhibits.¹
That is wrong and pointless.²
It also creates a
presumption that no costs are payable for that step.³
If the document is not important, it should be
omitted. If it is important (such as the conditions on the back of the key contract), and only poor
copies are handy, then it should either be rekeyboarded, or the original be found to photocopy
or scan afresh, as the Rule now expressly states.⁴
If the appearance of the original matters, a
photocopy or scan plus a rekeyboarded transcript of it should be given. Still less is there any
excuse for poor copies of the Reasons under appeal.⁵
The same document should not appear in the Appeal Records more than once.⁶
Costs may be
reduced or imposed for defective format.⁷
Afﬁdavits and records including hyperlinks to webpages are not admissible evidence because
it may not be static. A YouTube video or any other webpage online today could be altered or gone
tomorrow. What an afﬁant swears to has to be knowable, and not altered since he or she swore.
So must the record, for anyone looking later, including the Court of Appeal.⁸
The court complained of exhibits imperfectly described in the Table of Contents.⁹
One should
not just describe a whole binder of documents entered at trial as a single document, one exhibit,
in the Appeal Record and its table of contents.¹⁰
A book of Extracts needs a “comprehensive” Table
of Contents, so an afﬁdavit with 20 exhibits needs 21 entries in that Table, not just one. The Table
of Contents of a book of extracts should give some description, not mere exhibit numbers.¹¹

Contents of Appeal Record – appeals from tribunals
14.20(1) Where the appeal is from the decision of a person or body other than
a court, the contents, format and ﬁling of the Appeal Record must follow the
format for standard appeals as set out in rule 14.18 [Contents of Appeal Record
- standard appeals], except that, subject to any direction of a case management
ofﬁcer,
(a) the contents of the Appeal Record must be prepared with appropriate
changes, as the circumstances require, in order to ensure that the
information required to resolve the appeal is before the Court, and
(b) the Transcripts need only consist of any existing transcripts of
evidence before the person or body whose decision is being
appealed.
(2) A single appeal judge may make any order required to obtain production of
records from the person or body whose decision is being appealed.

DIVISION 3
PREPARING WRITTEN ARGUMENT AND SCHEDULING

Subdivision 1
Factums
Filing factums – standard appeals
14.23(1) The appellant in a standard appeal must ﬁle an appellant’s factum that
meets the requirements of rules 14.25 [Contents of factums] and 14.26 [Format
of factums], and must serve a ﬁled copy on every other party to the appeal before
the earlier of
(a) 2 months after the ﬁling of the Appeal Record, and
(b) 6 months after the ﬁling of the notice of appeal
or the appeal will be struck by the Registrar.
(2) The respondent in a standard appeal must, within 2 months of service of the
appellant’s factum
(a) ﬁle
(i) a respondent’s factum that meets the requirements of
rule 14.25 [Contents of factums] and rule 14.26 (Format of
factums], or
(ii) a letter of intention not to ﬁle a factum,
and
(b) serve a ﬁled copy of the factum or letter of intention on every other
party to the appeal.
(3) A respondent that does not ﬁle a factum will not be permitted to present oral
argument unless the panel orders otherwise.
(4) Where a cross appeal has been ﬁled, the appellant must ﬁle and serve a reply
factum, or a letter of intention not to ﬁle a reply factum, within 10 days of service
of the respondent’s factum. [Alta. Reg.41/14;61/23]

14-92
R.14.23(4) PART 14: APPEALS

Related Provisions
13.5(2) and 14.2(3) (extending time) ; 14.25(4) (dispensing with factums) ; 14.27(2) (missing

A. Electronic Filing
Ordinarily all documents ﬁled in the Court of Appeal must be ﬁled electronically, not in hard
copy. (In exceptional circumstances, a case management ofﬁcer may allow ﬁling hard copies. )
The Rules of Court and the Court’s Consolidated Practice Direction (Part F) were amended
accordingly. Often a copy of the document must be served on other parties, and it is the ﬁled
version which is to be served. Concepts like multiple copies, or backwards-facing pages, are gone
now. But colored covers of documents remain. Page numbering must start with the cover as page
1. There are formatting instructions in the Court of Appeal Management Systems (v.2, p.2-10),
and in the Consolidated Practice Direction.
Someone not used to this system would be well advised to get advice or help, or arrange to
submit a draft or test version, to see if it is acceptable, all well before any ﬁling deadline. Again
the two case management ofﬁcers would be a great help in such situations. (Electronic ﬁling of
transcripts of oral evidence has existed for a number of years, and should produce no novelty. )
Since the 1997 amendments to the factum Rules to make the time limits meaningful, this Rule
has become important. Eliminating in-person attendance to argue some appeals has led to more
counsel electing written argument only with no oral argument. That makes the quality of factums
even more important, indeed vital.
B. Time Limits
Tests for extending time to ﬁle a factum are recited.¹
Time to ﬁle a respondent’s factum was not extended, with an explanation why the court needs
factums a number of weeks before hearing, even in a fast track appeal.²
If the appeal has poor
chances, even a small delay may not be excused.³
If a party seeks extension of a time limit for an interlocutory step in an appeal, how long the
delay was and whether there was prejudice, are important, as is whether the appeal is arguable.⁴
A short time delay with no prejudice and a good chance of success, usually leads to a time
extension, but on pain of paying costs.⁵

Footnote
1 Appellant did not reveal enough merit to allow extending the time. This was an appeal from a full oral hearing
and its resulting fact and credibility ﬁndings: Murﬁn v. Barnes 2017 ABCA 441, Edm 1703 0306 AC (one JA
Dec 21).
2 B. C. S. v. C. L. J.2006 ABCA 20, 380 AR 312 (one JA).
3 Especially with a previous history of delay: Dhala v. Dhala 2007 ABCA 389 (one JA). Long delay led to stiff

14-93
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.23(4)
Rule 14.90(1) (a) denying costs for a late factum is not usually waived.¹
A host of cases refuse
to waive the subrule,²
the court is getting stricter, and summer holidays are not a good excuse.³
See the Mitchell case⁴
on the importance of timely factums.⁵
The deputy Registrar has no way of
knowing when a factum is served, and applies her reminder showing that a factum was late, on
the assumption that the previous document which started the time for that factum running, had
been served the day that it was ﬁled. Where that assumption was rebutted, the court gave full
costs for the factum.⁶
Times to ﬁle factums are not suspended where the appellant needs leave to appeal some of
the topics (not all), but ﬁled a notice of appeal instead of a motion for leave.⁷
Where a party wants to ﬁle a late factum but the Rules deem the appeal abandoned for want
of the factum, the appropriate test is the general slip Rule, not resort to rare leave to appeal late.⁸
That the appellant’s factum was late and not ready, was a factor in not restoring the appeal to
the list, in one case.⁹
C. Supplemental Factums
After time to appeal has expired, the appellant cannot ﬁle a supplemental factum arguing parts
of the trial judgment neither appealed by the Notice of Appeal, nor mentioned in his original factum.
Especially with no motion to extend time or to amend.¹⁰
A belated request to ﬁle an additional
factum and raise a new constitutional issue may necessitate adjourning an appeal, and if the court
adjourns it, paying solicitor-client costs. That means costs of more than the actual time in court.
But not all the preparation time would be wasted.¹¹
A trial judge dismissed the suit, but provisionally
assessed damages. The Court of Appeal allowed the appeal and found liability. Since counsel for
the respondent defendant had thought that she could not argue damages on the appeal, she was
given 3 weeks to ﬁle written arguments on the topic.¹²

Reply factums are rare, and the Rules now only speak of one where there is a cross-appeal.¹
But new important authority decided after the appellant’s factum was ﬁled, could justify permission
to ﬁle a reply factum? Permission is necessary to ﬁle a reply factum²
on the main appeal.³
It is
allowed without permission, to answer a cross appeal. In that event it is limited to ten pages.⁴

On time estimates, see R.14.32(2) below.

Filing factums – fast track appeals
14.24(1) Rule 14.23 [Filing factums - standard appeals] applies to fast track
appeals, except that
(a) the appellant’s factum must be ﬁled and served before the earlier of
(i) 20 days after the Appeal Record is ﬁled, and
(ii) 2 months after the notice of appeal is ﬁled,
or the appeal will be struck by the Registrar,
and
(b) the respondent’s factum or letter of intention not to ﬁle a factum
must be ﬁled and served before the earlier of
(i) one month after service of the appellant’s factum, and
(ii) 10 days before opening day of the sittings at which the appeal
is scheduled to be heard.
(2) A party that ﬁles a cross appeal in a fast track appeal must within 5 days of
ﬁling apply to a case management ofﬁcer for the establishment of a timetable
and other necessary requirements for the appeal.
[Alta. Reg.41/14]

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.25(1)
Contents of factums
14.25(1) A factum must include the following:
(a) Table of Contents, including page numbers;
(b) Part 1 — Facts: in the appellant’s factum, a statement of facts
(including, if desired, a concise introductory statement of the legal
issues raised), and in the respondent’s factum, its position on the
facts as stated by the appellant, and any other facts considered
relevant;
(c) Part 2 — Grounds of Appeal: in the appellant’s factum, a concise
statement of the grounds for appeal, and in the respondent’s factum,
its position in regards to the stated grounds, and any other points
that may properly be put in issue;
(d) Part 3 — Standard of Review: a statement on the relevant standard of
review;
(e) Part 4 — Argument: a discussion addressing the questions of law or
fact raised by the appeal;
(f) Part 5 — Relief Sought: a statement of the relief sought, including
any special direction with respect to costs;
(g) the estimated time required for the oral argument, within the limits
set out in rule 14.32(4) [Oral argument];
(h) a Table of Authorities listing each Act, regulation and other authority
referred to, and including
(i) any neutral citation assigned to the authority by the court
that decided it,
(ii) at least one print citation for the authority where available,
(iii) a hyperlink to each authority, where available,
(iv) where only an extract of an Act or regulation is relevant, a
hyperlink to that extract only, where available, and
(v) copies or extracts of any authorities, identiﬁed separately
and appended to the factum, for which a hyperlink is not
available.
[Alta. Reg.41/14;61/23]
(i) [Repealed Alta. Reg.61/23]
Authorities now require print citations and hyperlinks, where available, and copies of authorities
for which a hyperlink is not available.
(2) Where a cross appeal has been ﬁled, the respondent’s factum must consist
of 2 sections, each of 5 parts as required by subrule (1), entitled “factum on the
appeal” and “factum on the cross appeal”, prepared in accordance with subrule
(1) with any appropriate modiﬁcations.
(3) An intervenor’s factum must be prepared in the same form as a respondent’s
factum, with any appropriate modiﬁcations.
