Information Note
Rule 14.87 [Requirements for all documents] contains formal requirements for all documents.
Extracts of Key evidence must not be attached to factums. Authorities should not be cited
or hyperlinked to proprietary electronic databases, as the Court and other parties may not
have access to them.

A. New Arguments on Appeal¹
1. General Principle
(a) Policy and Importance
Usually an appellant cannot support its appeal with a new point which was not raised in the
court appealed from.²
Courts bar new issues on appeal because an appeal’s basic aim is to correct
errors, not consider new issues, the existing record may not sufﬁce, especially as the respondent
might have called more evidence on that at trial, especially if there is no explanation for not raising
the matter earlier, nor is payment offered for extra expenses which would be created.³
And a new
issue may be unfair to the other party, who had no chance to give argument or evidence on that
topic, or even to see how adequate is the respondent. And this role increases speed and ensures
ﬁnality.⁴

Can. v. NOVA Chem. Corp.2020 ABCA 320, Calg 1801 0226 AC (Sep 16) (¶ 63).

14-97
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.25(4)
The big question is fairness; the opponent needs a fair chance to defend, and the system
needs economy and ﬁnality. Sticking to pleadings and trial arguments promotes all that.¹
Especially
where other evidence relevant to it might²
have been led at trial had it been raised then,³
or where
the ﬂaw could have been corrected at trial,⁴
or the party had agreed to go to trial on selected
issues,⁵
or someone wants to be added as a party to raise new issues.⁶
The new point may not
have been discussed below because it had been agreed below, or treated as too clear to argue.⁷
If the point was pleaded in the trial court but not pursued during the trial, then the test is whether
there is enough relevant evidence to decide the issue without prejudice to the respondent. Also
relevant is why it was not pressed at trial.⁸
Overlap and common elements in the new issue help

375850 Alta. v. Noel; cf. Heikkila v. Apex Land Corp.2016 ABCA 126, 616 AR 390 (¶’s 28-30). See further
the C. P. E. , Chapter 75, Part E.2(e). Would this especially apply to new pleas of fraud? S. G. v. Alta. Fam.
& Social Serv.2003 ABCA 75. Consent shown at trial cannot ﬁrst be attacked as uninformed on appeal,
if there is some indication to the contrary, and new evidence would be needed to decide on advice and
information: Goodswimmer (Sturgeon L. I. B. ) v. R. (Alta. ) 2015 ABCA 253, 606 AR 291. Those restrictions
on new issues on appeal have often been applied to attempts to raise the Charter for the ﬁrst time on appeal:
Aftergood v. Min. of Mun. Affairs 2006 ABCA 154, 384 AR 189 (citing previous authority) ; Homes by Avi
v. W. C. B. , supra; Webber Academy Found. v. Human Rts. Comm. , supra. There may be an exception
letting one raise a new question where it is a Charter question already decided by the same appeal court in
another similar suit? Rath v. Kemp (CA 1996) 193 AR 26, 141 DLR(4th) 25. On the need for a factual basis
for a Charter challenge. Cf. the C. P. E. , Chapter 75, Part D.3(f).
4 In a matrimonial property case, the appellant pointed out a probable error in valuating an interest, but the
court rejected the appeal, because the parties had agreed on the method and had merely asked the trial
judge to ﬁnd one number, agreeing on the other numbers: Johnson v. Johnson 1999 ABCA 362. See
further the C. P. E. , Chapter 75, Part E.4.
5 Yellowbird v. Samson Cree N.2008 ABCA 270, 433 AR 350 (¶’s 24-9) ; Hill v. Hill 2013 ABCA 137, 553 AR

R.14.25(4) PART 14: APPEALS
allow the new argument.¹
That a party’s position argued at trial was overstated, does not make a
more arguable position on appeal “new”.²
It is sufﬁcient that the topic now raised on appeal was in some manner before the judge now
under appeal, though not framed the same way. An argument about whether an afﬁdavit should
be sealed against this party or whether he should see it, sufﬁces to let him appeal the order got
by the person relying on that afﬁdavit, arguing on appeal that the afﬁdavit was not served on him
and he never saw an unredacted version of its main exhibit, the matter redacted being the price
or value being disputed.³
Other considerations relevant to giving permission to raise a new point on appeal are other
injustice from a new point, that the standard of review is correctness (not reasonableness),
injustice if the new point cannot be raised, inadequate notice below to the appellant if facts or
arguments, so he could not have raised the point below, and whether full argument is made on
appeal.⁴
Amending an existing argument, or making extra submissions on an existing issue, are
not always new issues.⁵
A new legal argument on an existing issue is allowed even from an
intervener.⁶
On a new argument raised by an intervener, see Mikisew Cree F. N. case.⁷
Is it easier
to raise on appeal a new argument, as opposed to a new topic?⁸
Constantly-changing and expanding new grounds of appeal (not argued in the court appealed
from) are on the border of abuse of process, and a ground to refuse a new ground of appeal.
Argument potentials should be exhausted in the ﬁrst court.⁹
Any other prejudice to the respondent caused by adding the new point must be cured, or it will
also bar the new point.¹⁰
There is always the danger that at trial the appellant may have deliberately
and tactically “banked” his objection to spring at a later stage.¹¹
Especially when the matter has
come from a specialized tribunal via judicial review.¹²
(b) Failure to Object in First Court
Similarly, an appellant may not be able to object to some error or ﬂaw at trial which the appellant
did not object to at the time.¹³
For example, failure to object when the other side adduces evidence

So taking the position in the Court of King's Bench

Usually a party to a civil case is bound by how his or her counsel ran the trial, and what
agreements counsel made, and is often bound by counsel’s failure to object.³
Again the fear is that the respondent or the court might promptly have corrected or alleviated
the problem at trial, if it had known the objection at trial.⁴
For example, the appellant’s silence may
have sounded like consent.⁵
A fortiori if the point was expressly abandoned in the ﬁrst court.⁶
On

the trial should not have been ex parte.⁷
A new point on appeal would also be unfair where
other parties might have joined and opposed, had it been raised in the ﬁrst court (damages not
pleaded).⁸
If evidence is tendered at trial on one basis, it cannot be supported on appeal by
alleging a different basis.⁹
(c) Examples
Even if the appellant’s pleading mentions a certain issue, if it was never raised at trial, whether
in evidence or argument, and indeed he expressly declined to argue the issue at trial, it is proper
for the trial judge to ignore the issue. It is improper for the appellant to raise that issue later on

R.14.25(4) PART 14: APPEALS
appeal. Nor should the Court of Appeal order a new trial of that issue, just because the appellant
has now developed more enthusiasm for that topic; it is not even a new point of law.¹
Semble, leave to appeal does not require the panel hearing the appeal to decide a question
not raised before the tribunal below, especially if appeals are conﬁned to questions of law, and the
record does not let the Court of Appeal see whether this question was pure law or not.²
It is doubly bad if an administrative tribunal appearing on an appeal from its own decision puts
into its factum an entirely new topic not raised below.³
In a criminal appeal, an intervener must not assume the role of third-party Crown prosecutor,
nor widen the grounds of appeal from an acquittal.⁴
That the judge below interpreted some contractual provision differently than either party is not
necessarily a new issue or an unfair surprise; a judge need not accept either party's interpretation.⁵
2. Matter Properly Founded Below
There is an exception allowing the new point on appeal, where it is certain that all the evidence
which might be led on the point is already before the court, the fact ﬁndings have been made, the
question is one of law alone, and raising it late causes no prejudice.⁶
Where a question was purely
one of jurisdiction and would involve no other facts or evidence, and there was no prejudice, it
could be raised for the ﬁrst time on appeal.⁷
On a close division and narrow facts, the Supreme
Court of Canada heard a constitutional question fully argued, and discussed by the two levels
of court before, and needing no new evidence, but formal notice of which was given only in the
Supreme Court of Canada.⁸
It may be important whether criminal law or truly penal consequences
are involved.⁹
Though an argument on appeal was not precisely that made to the trial judge, it was very
similar, and effectively showed that the new appellate argument would not be a surprise, and that
the record was adequate to review the (new) argument on appeal.¹⁰
A party could not be faulted for failing to object at trial to an evidentiary ruling excluding
documents, as the judge’s ruling was spontaneous and took that party by surprise.¹¹
When an
appellant complains on appeal that evidence was not admitted, it is relevant that the appellant
never asked to have it admitted.¹²

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.25(4)
But it is enough that someone raised at trial the point now relied on in the Court of Appeal; it
need not have been the appellant.¹
3. Miscellaneous
It may be easier for a respondent to raise a new point on appeal than an appellant. On a purely
legal point, see the Gray case.²
The tests for an intervener raising a new point on appeal are much
like those in subpart A.1 above.³
On a point going to jurisdiction (wrong court), see the Martin case.⁴
Lack of jurisdiction based
on subject matter can be raised at any time; consent does not confer jurisdiction.⁵
Most of the appellant’s arguments to the Court of Appeal had never been raised below, and
indeed were new at the oral argument stage of the leave application before one justice of appeal.
To deter litigants from raising new matters on appeal, thus multiplying the opponent’s work and
forcing the court to hear issues on an incomplete record, the court doubled the usual costs
column.⁶
Where a new point about interest is ﬁrst raised after the appellate judgment, costs may be
refused if the successful appellant’s factum did not seek that interest.⁷
And if the new issue need
not be decided on appeal, it may be left open to reargue in the courts below.⁸
An appeal court should rarely raise new issues that have not been raised by the parties, and
only to prevent injustice. It should not decide a case based on a new issue unless the parties have
had a fair opportunity to provide submissions on it. This applies to genuinely new issues that are
legally and factually distinct from existing grounds of appeal, not to mere variations or corollaries
of existing arguments.⁹
Interveners are not intended merely to repeat the arguments of one side, even with different
emphasis.¹⁰
Coming close to taking a position on the merits may be one reason to make an
intervener pay costs.¹¹

14-102
R.14.25(4) PART 14: APPEALS
B. Importance of Factums
The restrictions on time for oral appellate argument ﬂow from, and reinforce, the importance
of factums.¹
Before oral argument, a panel of justices of the Alberta or Northwest Territories or Nunavut
Court of Appeal always read the reasons of the judge appealed from, and all the factums ﬁled.
They often read other things too: salient law, or key pieces of evidence. Therefore, the justices
always come to oral argument knowing the basic facts and the issues. Often some or all of the
justices also bring some tentative views about the appeal.
Some counsel view the factum as a formality, or shufﬂe its writing off onto a junior not fully
trusted or fully informed. So they send an ill-trained understudy to perform on opening night. That
is bad neglect. The factum is not a mere rehearsal for a play to be performed later.
Counsel’s later oral argument at best might swing around one or two or three justices whom
the factums had led to a tentative contrary view. But why would counsel wish to assume that heavy
burden, by ﬁling a poor factum?
Even if counsel’s oral argument does swing around part or all of the panel, a new problem then
arises. Most appellate justices do not take detailed notes of oral argument. The Court of Appeal
cannot give an oral judgment if the judgment may make new law, or if the case is complex, or if
the oral argument is somewhat unexpected. When the Court of Appeal reserves decision, writing
a judgment will often take weeks. By then, memory of oral argument has faded. The recording of
oral argument is rarely transcribed, and not often listened to. Only the factums remain to reread,
and so the factums once again become more and more important. A persuasive careful factum
ﬁnally tends to dominate. It is a continuing advocate, and a necessary reference book.²
The Court may well refuse to hear oral argument on a topic not in that party’s factum.³
On forbidden or dangerous contents of factums, see Part E below.
C. Length of Factum
Most factums are too long. Thirty pages (the maximum permitted in most appeals) is far too
long in most cases.⁴
Even complicated appeals generally can be argued within existing limits
on factum length, which are rarely extended.⁵
Over ﬁfty years ago, a factum fairly often used to
give sparse facts and then a skeleton of argument, maybe in syllogistic form (with authorities).
Factums did not often exceed 10 pages, sometimes far less. Now even the preliminary statement
of the issue can be a page or more long in many factums. Then come detailed pages of facts,
many completely irrelevant to the issues on appeal. A wordy full written argument usually
follows, often with rhetorical ﬂourishes and several alternate presentations of every aspect of

Footnote
1 See ¶ 8 of 14 Apr ’08 Notice to Profession and 30 Jun ’04 Notice to Profession.
2 S. Maclise Ent. v. Union Secur. (#2) 2010 ABCA 63, 474 AR 307; Merrill Lynch Can. v. Soost (#2) 2010
ABCA 355, 490 AR 406.
3 Ko v. Hillview Homes 2012 ABCA 245, 536 AR 93, leave den (SCC 13 Jan ’13)
4 But not in all cases: Michel v. Lafrentz (#4) (CA 1999) 232 AR 82, 85, leave den (SCC 2000) 253 NR 199.
An appellant who ﬁled a long and very compressed factum in a very complex case, got costs of 1 1/3 times
item 14. One cannot evade page limits for factums by incorporating by reference briefs earlier ﬁled in King's
Bench, so the Court of Appeal will not approve Appeal Books containing such briefs: Kurian v. Admin. of
Motor Vehicle Acc. Claims Act 2007 ABCA 318 (one JA). The limit for a reply to a cross appeal is ten
pages.
5 McLeod Est. v. Cole 2021 MBCA 80 (Sep 23).

14-103
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.25(4)
every contention. Factums often unnecessarily contain long quotes from the evidence.¹
Such
elephantine performances are not very persuasive.
A British Columbia Court of Appeal justice criticizes long factums. Factums are an outline
of argument, to give notice of the issues and that party’s position on them, and later if decision
is reserved, to list authorities and give a structure. It is not full argument, and is based on the
facts found at trial or not disputed. The Rules say that each of a factum’s various parts are to be
concise. The Rules’ 30-page limit is for fairly complex cases. Simpler cases need a much shorter
factum. Extending the 30-page limit is rare. Importance, complication, many grounds of appeal, a
long history, or a long trial, is not enough to extend the page limit. Appeals must remain focussed.
The limit was extended to 40 pp. here because of two distinct issues, but no more.²
The sharp arrows in counsel’s quiver should not be swathed in bundles of padding.
Concise factums are encouraged, but not ones citing no authority, or reciting “facts” which are
not in evidence.³
The court also criticized a shotgun approach with dozens of grounds of appeal,
many not in the factum, and odd numbering schemes, suggesting that in another case costs could
be affected by such argument.⁴
An intervener ﬁled a long afﬁdavit, much of it argument, and so got around the usual page-
limit for written argument, and most was on issues on which leave had not been granted. So that
intervener had to pay some costs.⁵
The factum portion of the fee in Schedule C was doubled where the court called for and
allowed a good deal of extra written argument after oral argument.⁶
Conversely, where a party
largely adopted the arguments in another party’s factum, he got half the usual fees for his factum.⁷
A respondent got 1 ½ the usual fee for its factum because the appellant had ﬁled a factum 69
pages long (by adding appendices of argument, without leave).⁸
If there are two appeals to be heard together and closely related, the court may let each
party ﬁle a single factum for both appeals, twice the ordinary length (those being fast-track family
appeals with shorter page limits).⁹

On the substantive law about this topic, see R.14.75 n.

The factum’s body and table of contents require a section on the standard of review on appeal.¹
The portion of the factum discussing the standard of review was not dispensed with where there
was no reason to do so.²
E. Improper Contents
A book of authorities should not contain factual material, such as historical evidence, or
newspaper clippings.³
If that material is in the trial record, it should go into the book of extracts.
The court may strike out an intervener’s factum (and in effect end the intervention) for failure
to comply with an earlier order setting limits and terms for the intervention.⁴
And paragraphs were
struck from a factum because they went into topics on which no leave had been sought or given.⁵
The Court may order that an exhibit to a factum be removed from all copies, and the exhibit
destroyed, and the court ﬁle sealed.⁶
A proposed intervener thought a point was not being argued which should be, an alleged
separate error by the tribunal. In part, the intervener wanted to raise new matters beyond the
scope of what either party thought was in issue. But the Court of Appeal should not go gratuitously
outside the topics which the parties have put before it.⁷
Whether a tribunal can take any part in an appeal from it, involves concern about impartiality
and repute. Whether to give it any role depends on factors such as whether anyone with enough
knowledge and expertise opposes the appeal, and whether the tribunal hears two-sided individual
conﬂicts, or makes policy or has other public roles. So sometimes a tribunal could participate
to argue that its decision was reasonable. But it should not do that when existing parties to the
appeal are available to ﬁll those roles. And even if it can and does take such a role, it should
not try to expatiate on its previous reasons for decision, still less add more reasons.⁸
The Court
complained of a factum ﬁled by the tribunal being appealed, which factum argued the merits of
the appeal, even though another party argued against the appellant.⁹
The usual immunity of the tribunal from costs probably does not apply to deterrent costs
awards or administrative penalties under the Rules. It is often better in such a situation for the
tribunal to defer ﬁling its factum until the written submissions of the other parties are seen.¹⁰

7 Kikino Métis Sett. v. Abtosway 2018 ABCA 61, [2020] AJ #769 (one) (intervention denied).
8 FortisAlta. v. Alta. Utils. Comm.2020 ABCA 271, Calg 2001 0098 AC (one JA Jul 21) (¶’s 74-76).
9 Atco Gas & P. L. v. Alta. Utils. Comm. , infra. Such a factum was disregarded by the Court of Appeal, in
Camrose Police Serv. v. MacDonald 2013 ABCA 422, 566 AR 141 (¶’s 41-44).
10 Atco Gas & P. L. v. Alta. Utils. Comm.2013 ABCA 310, 556 AR 376 (¶’s 12-13), affd 2015 SCC 45, [2015]
3 SCR 219.

14-105
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.25(4)
The Court criticized a party whose factum was intemperate and alleged “facts” based on
“evidence” which was not in the appeal record and (if anywhere), had been unilaterally put into the
book of authorities of that party. That party became jointly liable for the costs of the failed appeal.¹
On consequences at lack of civility and personal opinions of counsel, and the remedies for that (a
new factum), see the Saddleback decision.²
The Court of Appeal were very critical of (self-represented) parties who repeatedly alleged
that the opponents counsel were “criminal, fraudulent, and unprofessional” in how they defended,
and wrongly claimed the delay was the opponent’s fault. This is extremely serious and having no
lawyer is not an excuse.³
An intervener’s factum should not directly argue who should win the appeal, and a preference
for one result is not a legal topic.⁴
One must not allege, state, or assume facts which are not in evidence, or not found by the
court appealed from (unless one clearly states that they are in evidence but not found).
Using a computer program to draft written argument has many dangers. Such a program is
unlikely to reﬂect all the ethical and legal and procedural rules for factums, still less to reﬂect
Alberta law. And computer drafting can even cite entirely imaginary court precedents. That could
do worse than publicly explode your appeal or your lawsuit; it could make you appear entirely
dishonest. You have only one reputation to preserve.
A single judge declined to make a ﬁnal decision about striking large passages out of one
factum and book of extracts. It would be hard to separate the issues, and it should be left for the
panel.⁵
The impugned passage must go too far beyond what is arguably proper, as the merits are
a question for three justices of appeal, not one. And it is proper to argue what is the appropriate
remedy if the appeal succeeds.⁶
Written argument ﬁled in the Court of Appeal must give hyperlinks for authorities in the Table
of authorities, which would eliminate any need to ﬁle copies of the authorities.⁷
F. Relief Requested in Factum
The failure to request in the factum an adjustment of the costs (where an appeal was allowed
in part) is not an abandonment of that aspect of the appeal.⁸

Footnote

grds 2008 SCC 14, 372 NR 239; Petz v. Duguay 2018 ABCA 29, Calg 1701 0064 AC (one JA Jan 24). One
judge struck out a number of paragraphs from the factum of an appellant, partly because it went beyond the
notice of appeal, but mostly because it contained collateral attack on previous orders, and because it was
prejudicial to the respondent in places: Turkawski v. Boyle (#4) 2007 ABCA 275 (one JA).

14-106
R.14.26(1) PART 14: APPEALS
Format of factums
14.26(1) Factums must be
(a) formatted using at least 12 point font, one-inch margins and at least
1.5 line spacing, except for quotations, and
(b) continuously paginated, with the cover page being page 1, and have
electronic bookmarks as required by rule 14.87(1) (i).
(2) Parts 1 to 5 of a factum must not exceed in length
(a) 30 pages for the parties in a standard appeal,
(b) 40 pages for a respondent who has ﬁled a cross appeal in a standard
appeal,
(c) 10 pages for an appellant’s factum in response to a cross appeal,
(d) 30 pages for an intervenor, or
(e) 12 pages for every party in a fast track appeal.
(3) Factums must contain precise references to the location, page numbers and
paragraph numbers or lines of the Appeal Record, Extracts of Key Evidence and
authorities referred to.
(4) Each factum must have covers, prepared as required by rule 14.87(2)
[Requirements for all documents], in the following colours:
(a) appellants, including appellants who are cross respondents — beige
or ivory;
(b) respondents, including respondents who are cross appellants —
green;
(c) intervenors — blue. [Alta. Reg.41/14;61/23]

Related Provisions
14.27(2) (missing documents in Extracts of Key Evidence) ; 14.28(2) (originals of records

in Court); 14.38 (fresh evidence) ; 14.45 (application for fresh evidence) ; 14.73 and 14.86
(curing non-compliance) ; 14.87 (format requirements)
A. Color Scheme
As ﬁling is now electronic no cover needs to be cardboard, but the rules for colors of covers
remain. If no one prints an electronic factum, one either should use a color printer, or insert
colored paper before printing the cover.
In multi-party references, sometimes complicated color schemes are adopted for evidence,
argument, and authorities.¹
Oddly, many appellants’ factums have yellow covers, though R.14.26(4) calls for beige or
ivory. Since appellant’s Extracts of Evidence have yellow covers, that error has become more
serious where hard copies are ﬁled.²
Some stationers used to supply covers in a greenish grey,
which the Registrar will not accept for respondents.

Footnote
1 See the checklist (on next page), item 3(e).
2 Houle v. BMW Finl. Serv.2012 ABCA 333, 539 AR 27 (¶ 29).

14-107
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.26(4)
B. Format
Citations in factums to oral reasons under appeal should be to the “F” series of page numbers
in the appeal record, not to transcript page numbers, which the Court does not have quick access
to.¹
It is amazing how often counsel make that mistake, so they and the three justices of appeal
cannot understand each other’s citations to the trial Reasons.
No longer do the Rules expressly require the appellant or any other party to provide (reproduce)
any exhibits critical to the appeal, and any rulings during the trial which are a subject of appeal.²
It
is permissible, of course. One would think that counsel would be eager to do this, but most seem
to forget.
It is even more amazing how often the whole appeal is about two clauses in a contract which
are almost impossible to ﬁnd in the evidence reproduced, and almost illegible once found. For at
least one party, that omission or obscurity is like playing Russian roulette.
Many factums omit the appendix of statutes cited which the Rule calls for. If the statutes are
bulky and are in the book of authorities, that is harmless, even permitted: see R.14.25(1) (i). But
often the statutes cited are not there either.
The court encourages cross-referencing the table of authorities (at the back of the factum and
at the head of a book of authorities), to the pages of the factum where each authority is cited. The
court praised numbering tabs in different books of authorities consecutively, so that no two books
of authorities would contain the same tab numbers.³
C. Checklist for Factums
1. Look at Consolidated Practice Directions and CAMS system for electronic ﬁling and formatting
instructions. Maybe ask case management ofﬁcer for help or a chance to ﬁle and test version
early to detect any ﬂaws (R.14.87(1) (g), (h) )
2. Time:
(a) Appellant: File and serve within 2 months of ﬁling of Appeal Record, or within 6 months
of ﬁling Notice of Appeal, whichever is earlier. (R.14.23(1) ) (Crim. appeals: see R.16.16(2) )
(b) Respondent: File and serve within 2 months of service of appellant’s factum. (R.14.23(2) )
(Crim. appeals: see R.16.16(4) )
(c) Reply facta when cross-appeal or notice of intention to vary exists: 10 days of service of
respondent’s factum: R.14.23(4)
(d) Also under fast track appellant’s factum is 20 days after Appeal Record ﬁled, or 2 months
after notice of appeal (R.14.24(1) ), respondent’s factum is 1 month after appellant’s factum,
or 10 days before opening, whichever is sooner.
3. Color of cover (R.14.26(4) ):
(a) appellant: beige or ivory (yellow is not accepted any more, as that is the color for something
else)
(b) respondent: green
(c) cross appellant: green

Subdivision 2
Extracts of Key Evidence
Filing Extracts of Key Evidence
14.27(1) Where needed to resolve the issues in the appeal, each party to an
appeal must ﬁle Extracts of Key Evidence that meet the requirements of rule
14.29 [Format of Extracts of Key Evidence],
(a) containing extracts of the transcripts, exhibits and other material on
the record needed to resolve the issues in the appeal,
(b) excluding any evidence, exhibits and other materials unlikely to be
needed, and
(c) not containing any comment, argument, trial briefs, legal authorities
or new evidence.
(2) If any document required by rule 14.18 [Contents of Appeal Record - standard
appeals] is not available at the time of preparation of the Appeal Record, a copy
may be included in the Extracts of Key Evidence or appended to the factum.
(3) A party preparing Extracts of Key Evidence must ﬁle, when or before ﬁling
that party’s factum, a copy of the Extracts of Key Evidence and must serve a ﬁled
copy on every other party to the appeal.
(4) Records that are subject to a restricted court access order must be ﬁled as a
separate Extracts of Key Evidence and identiﬁed as such.
[Alta. Reg.41/14;85/16;61/23]
Any records ordered sealed, must be separately ﬁled and identiﬁed as sealed.
Information Note
If, for example, the formal judgment under appeal (or the order granting permission to appeal)
is not available at the time of preparation of the Appeal Record, a copy can be appended to
the factum, or included in the Extracts of Key Evidence under rule 14.27(2) [Filing Extracts of
Key Evidence]. The Consolidated Practice Direction provides guidance on preparing Extracts
of Key Evidence.


14-110
R.14.27(4) PART 14: APPEALS
Related Provisions
14.27(2) (missing documents in Extracts of Key Evidence) ; 14.28 (record before the Court) ;

14.28(2) (originals of records in Court) ; 14.38 (fresh evidence) ; 14.45 (application for fresh
evidence); 14.70 (fresh evidence) ; 14.73 and 14.86 (curing non-compliance) ; 14.87 (format
requirements)
Ordinarily all documents ﬁled in the Court of Appeal must be ﬁled electronically, not in hard
copy. (In exceptional circumstances, a case management ofﬁcer may allow ﬁling hard copies. )
The Rules of Court and the Court’s Consolidated Practice Direction (Part F) were amended
accordingly. Often a copy of the document must be served on other parties, and it is the ﬁled
version which is to be served. Concepts like multiple copies, or backwards-facing pages, are gone
now. But colored covers of documents remain. Page numbering must start with the cover as page
1. There are formatting instructions in the Court of Appeal Management Systems (v.2, p.2-10),
and in the Consolidated Practice Direction.
Someone not used to this system would be well advised to get advice or help, or arrange to
submit a draft or test version, to see if it is acceptable, all well before any ﬁling deadline. Again
the two case management ofﬁcers would be a great help in such situations. (Electronic ﬁling of
transcripts of oral evidence has existed for a number of years, and should produce no novelty. )
A. Option to Reproduce Some Evidence
Beyond what the Rules require for the Appeal Record, and the electronic transcript, what (if
any) evidence (oral or written) is selected for the panel of justices hearing the appeal? Each party
decides what is needed. Each party can unilaterally decide whether it wants a given afﬁdavit,
exhibit portion of such a document, or portion of the transcript reproduced in multiple hard copies
for the panel hearing the appeal. If so, it can ﬁle a volume of such material, called Extracts of Key
Evidence. (If two parties wish to ﬁle such a book jointly, they may. ) It is also very desirable that the
ﬁrst Extracts ﬁled include documents likely to be ﬁled by other parties. See the Court of Appeal’s
Consolidated Practice Directions, as amended to 2023 (Part F) (v.2, p.2-10).
Furthermore, those individual selected extracts of evidence need not be ﬁled until that party’s
factum is due. So each counsel can draft his or her brief written argument (factum), and then
select evidence to reproduce, in light of that draft. The selection can be the backup for the factum.
Speculation about what will be argued will thus be much reduced, if not eliminated.
Reproducing all the evidence is rarely proper, and leaving out a relevant document is never
fatal. Anticipating what later opposing arguments will likely make relevant is a delicate test, never
fool proof, so usually the respondent has to reproduce some documents which the appellant did
not.¹
A tribunal being appealed usually ﬁles only one copy of its ofﬁcial Record (as the Rules
provide). It hinders the Court of Appeal if no party puts any parts of that Record into a book of
Extracts with multiple copies.²
That Record from the tribunal does not go before the panel hearing
the appeal.
The great number of pre-2023 appeal books used to contain a lot of evidence which no one
ever looked at.

Footnote
1 Barkwell v. McDonald (#2) 2023 ABCA 183, Edm 2103 0273/6 AC (Jun 15) (¶ 69).
2 Calgary (City) v. A. E. U. B.2010 ABCA 132, 477 AR 1 (¶’s 75, 238) (Apr 23).

14-111
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.27(4)
That may be because one of the lawyers assumed that any evidence not reproduced there is
gone. That is not so. Under the former Rules a judge could order (or the parties could agree) that
the ofﬁcial record for the appeal contain things which will not be reproduced.¹
That was sometimes
done where a map, chart, or object would be difﬁcult or expensive to reproduce. Rule 14.28(3)
allows the full record from the trial court to be brought to the appeal courtroom. The current Rules
about extracts make all that automatic.²
Rule 14.27(1) (c) bars comment, argument, trial briefs, legal authorities, or new evidence in
Extracts, Argument is for the factums.
Even in a criminal appeal, not all exhibits need be reproduced, and those unlikely to be referred
to should not be reproduced.³
And the court or a party can rely on any evidence received by the
court below, even if it is not in the Extracts: R.14.28.
B. Ancillary Procedure
Originally the emphasis in the Extracts was to give hard copies to the panel hearing the
appeal. But now even the Extracts are ﬁled electronically.
The respondent’s extracts should not duplicate documents in the appellant’s extracts, on pain
of denial of costs.⁴
A document should not be in the material more than once.⁵
Rule 14.29(a) calls for a “table of contents” for the Book of Extracts listing each record. It
is not sufﬁcient to list a document as one item without listing its appendices or exhibits. Those
should be separately named. A binder with 20 tabs should be 20 or 21 items in the table, not one.
And exhibits should have a description, not mere exhibit numbers.⁶
A book of extracts needs a
meaningful table of contents, describing each item, not opaque names like “Tab 14”, or “Exhibit
F”.⁷
It is also curious that at the end of an appeal in which no one has opened the bulky evidence,
counsel rarely ask for costs relief on that ground, even where the appeal succeeds and the
appellant gets costs of the appeal.⁸
A party (or the appeal record volume, if applicable) may reproduce the evidence which was
before the judge appealed from at the time of his or her decision, not what he or she received
later.⁹
Either side can have the evidence which was before the judge reproduced, even if it is bulky.
One should not try to put before the Court of Appeal evidence which was ﬁled ﬁrst for a motion for
permission to appeal (or later) ; a motion to adduce new evidence is necessary.¹⁰

R.14.27(4) PART 14: APPEALS
On poor quality copies of exhibits, and other ﬂaws in presentation, see Rr.14.18(3) n. B, and
14.92n.
On trying to smuggle factual material not in the agreement as to contents before the court in
the book of authorities, see the Pub. School Bds. case,¹
the Lovelace case,²
and R.14.16 n. B.
Smuggled argument and case law and duplication of material already ﬁled, led to a denial of
all costs for the defective book of Extracts.³
Enhanced costs were awarded, payable by a party ﬁling thousands of pages of material
unlikely to be needed, including bulky material which was factual or argumentative material not
before the judge appealed from.⁴
An appeal was pending from an order which (inter alia) declared parts of a report conﬁdential,
so one justice of appeal ordered that those parts of the report be put in a separate volume
of extracts marked conﬁdential, to be available to the Court of Appeal panel only, not to the
respondent.⁵
Extracts of evidence are to be ﬁled for a full appeal, but not for an application to the court of
appeal.⁶
C. Pointers for Book of Extracts
Further guidance on preparing these Extracts can be found in Part F of the Consolidated
Practice Directions.
1. Include all items which you cite in your argument.
2. Make each page legible. Choose an original. Or rekeyboard!
3. Add items which Court is likely to want to look at, or which the opposing party will cite.⁷
4. Respondent should not duplicate appellant’s items (except maybe a stray page or two).
5. Really full table of contents, with very clear explanations, not merely exhibit numbers - e. g. an
afﬁdavit with 19 exhibits is 20 items in the Table of Contents.
6. Cite the items from the Extracts in your factum in a way making them easy to ﬁnd.
7. Better is consecutive set of page numbers for whole book, with Table of Contents keyed to it.
8. Do not reproduce everything. An item is in the ofﬁcial record even if the parties do not
reproduce it (but they may bring it to oral argument).
9. Some editing is okay. Be reasonable.
Further guidance on preparing the Extracts of Key Evidence can be found in Part F of the
Consolidated Practice Direction.

14-113
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.28(1)
Record before the Court
14.28(1) Subject to any enactment, all evidence or exhibits received by the
court appealed from are an ofﬁcial part of the record before the Court of Appeal,
notwithstanding that no copy is ﬁled with the Court of Appeal.
(2) If an exhibit cannot be readily reproduced and will be referred to in argument,
the Extracts of Key Evidence must be accompanied with a letter to the Registrar
requesting that the original exhibit be made available at the hearing of the appeal.
(3) An appeal judge may direct, on any conditions that the judge considers to be
appropriate, that any records before the court appealed from be transmitted to
the Court of Appeal. [Alta. Reg.41/14;36/20]

Related Provisions
14.16 (appeal record) ; 14.38 (application for fresh evidence) ; 14.45 (application for fresh

evidence); 14.70 (no fresh evidence without order)
Subrule (1) was amended in 2020 so that all evidence before the lower court is part of the
record generally, even if not part of the speciﬁc Appeal Record.
Provisions for a tribunal being appealed sending up its record to the Court of Appeal come
from the old provisions for certiorari calling up the record. But ﬁling this Record in the Court
of Appeal has problems; it is not distributed to the panel, and contains much irrelevance and
duplication. If leave to appeal has been granted on issues likely to make that record unhelpful,
the justice giving leave may stay the need to ﬁle the Record¹
(and did here). If any party needed a
document they could write to the Commission and ask for a copy. The appeal Record here need
not include any transcripts, but parties could put parts of them in their extracts of Key Evidence,
if they wished.
Nothing should be put in the Extracts “out of an abundance of caution”, as R.14.28(1) says
everything on the tribunal’s record can be referred to in factums.²
This Rule lets a Justice of Appeal in Chambers use evidence ﬁled earlier in King's Bench.³
Pending an application for leave to appeal to the Supreme Court of Canada, a Supreme Court
Justice can order release of one of the trial exhibits for forensic testing. On appeal that court has
all the powers which the Court of Appeal has, and “appeal” should be construed broadly to include
an application for permission to appeal, especially to avoid a lacuna in jurisdiction.⁴

14.29 Extracts of Key Evidence must
(a) have a table of contents describing separately each document,
including each exhibit to any afﬁdavit, and showing the page number
where the document can be found;

appellants — pink;
(iii) intervenors — blue;
(d) [Repealed Alta. Reg.61/23] [Alta. Reg.41/14;61/23]
Information Note
Extracts of Key Evidence must not be attached to the factum. Key passages in extracts of
key evidence may be highlighted or otherwise identified. They may be copied with the printed
page on the right, and double sided copying is acceptable. The key parts of illegible or small
print documents should be typed out, and both the original and the typescript should be
reproduced.

14-115
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.30(2)
The main aim of a Condensed Book is to get a panel of judges hearing oral argument more
quickly to a key passage or document. Given only 45 minutes for oral argument, and the Record,
Extracts, and Factums, a Condensed Book longer than 30 pages is likely counterproductive.
On electronic ﬁling replacing ﬁling of multiple paper copies of various materials and consequent
Rules amendments, see R.14.27n. above.
Traditionally a book of authorities has covers the same color as the covers of the factum ﬁled
by the same party. On colored covers, those are still required. If one prints an electronic document
with colored covers, one either needs a color printer, or should insert colored paper when printing
the cover.
Where legislation mandates the language (English or French) in which argument must be
made, one can still give quotations of evidence or of legal authorities in their original but “wrong”
language.¹
Costs awarded for appeals have been signiﬁcantly reduced for books of authorities with poor
copies, some being only headnotes and some computer printouts, and not noting appeals from
the same decisions.²
Or for duplications of cases in the books of authorities.³
When reported cases are reproduced in the book of authorities, they should not be in the form
of an old-fashioned computer printout, with neither page numbers, paragraph numbers, nor bold-
faced type. A citation of “p.14” from such a printout is useless, because in another printout of the
case that same passage will be on a different page.
Cases should be photocopied from a law report, or printed from a more modern type of
computer database with features⁴
to permit uniform citation of speciﬁc passages.⁵
Where the
case is reported in a law report, counsel should not reproduce it only in computer form, without
even a citation of the reported version.⁶
That ﬂaw is becoming very common. Sometimes books of
authorities are rejected for that reason. The court again reminded counsel to give more than just
a single electronic citation of a case being cited, where possible.⁷
Indeed, the version of reported cases in one online full text service is sometimes not quite
accurate, and is occasionally very incomplete. Minor errors which spell checkers cannot detect are
common in Canadian and American online services. Court websites or CanLii are more accurate,
but often they do not give reported citations, nor whether the decision was later appealed, nor any
headnote. Quotations and copies in books of authorities would be safer if taken from printed law
reports, or from the website of the court whose judgment is reproduced, or from CanLii.

Subdivision 4
Scheduling Oral Argument
Oral argument
14.32(1) Unless otherwise directed,
(a) all appeals will be set down on the Civil Appeal Hearing List for an
oral hearing, and
(b) the appeal and any cross appeal will be argued at the same time.
[Alta. Reg.41/14;194/20;72/22]
The Court sits each month in each big city, except in August. The sitting in late July is short,
and for urgent cases only.
In 2020 and 2021, and into 2022, because of the Covid pandemic, most appeals were
heard by electronic attendance, and some in writing only. The Court of Appeal promptly set up
arrangements for electronic hearings in the spring of 2020, and they have been held since, and
the waiting list for appeals to be heard (electronically or in writing) is fairly short. Setting an appeal
down for hearing seems to work well, and the case management ofﬁcers are happy to receive
telephone calls and advise counsel or litigants about the procedures. But effective May 2, 2022, all
matters heard by three justices of appeal again are heard in person. Matters heard by one justice
of appeal alone, or other matters, will still be heard virtually (electronically). That notiﬁcation is
part of a three-courts announcement in the “News & Announcements” part of the Alberta courts’
website.³
A lawyer appearing in court for herself, not a client, should not wear barrister's robes.⁴
(2) On agreement of all parties, or on the direction of a single appeal judge, the
Court of Appeal may hear and decide an appeal without oral argument.
[Alta. Reg.41/24;194/20;72/22]

The 2022 amendment merely clariﬁes who may give the contrary “direction”.
All-written argument is especially useful when counsel are not in the same city or town where
the appeal is being heard, and it should be used more often. Since 2020, it has become more
popular, and usually leads to a quicker decision.
A self-represented litigant kept sending communications to the Registrar with no copy
to the other side, trying to get last-minute adjournments, and alleging failure to reply to his
communications, which was not true. He then left the country without leaving any forwarding
address. Some days later the court heard his appeal on the written material, as he was not
present.¹
For a long time electronically recording oral appellate argument was rarely done where all
parties were represented by counsel,²
but since late 2010 the Court of Appeal routinely does it.

argument, see Côté, Systematic Advocacy, Chap.19 (Juriliber 2017).
On attempts to split an appeal into three and postpone oral argument scheduled, using an
unsworn vague physician’s letter, and all the delay that would involve, see the Helm cases.³
There are time limits on oral argument: 45 minutes per party, unless leave for longer argument
is obtained at the hearing.⁴
And since 1999, counsel had been held to their (shorter) time
estimates. The Supreme Court of Canada does not ordinarily add more time for argument if there
is a cross appeal.⁵
Comments of judges during argument have often been held not to be reasons, even tentative
ones, and the reasons of the panel (and their adequacy) can be determined from the written
reasons ﬁled and given to the parties.⁶
The Court of Appeal will not release a recording of oral argument before it, even for use on a
leave motion in Ottawa. Rarely should a panel later provide clariﬁcation of its reasons.⁷

A. Appeal Lists and Timing
The Court of Appeal has posted on its website a guide called “Pre-booking a General Appeal”.
The following Court of Appeal lists are regularly posted to the Alberta Courts’ website on the
public Internet (and near the Registrar’s counters). There are two separate sections, one for each
city:
1. General Appeal Hearing Lists - These list hearing weeks, with the cases to be heard
on the speciﬁc days. There are two lists divided by courtroom. These are posted as soon
as the previous list is ﬁnished, ﬁve weeks before the sittings in questions. The names of
the justices who will sit are posted at the counter and on the Internet on the Wednesday
before the sittings.
2. Sentence List - This is also a hearing list. Nineteen days before the opening of the
sitting, this list is ﬁxed. It is posted on the Internet and at the counter the following day. The
panel of justices is posted on the Internet and at the counter on the Wednesday before
the sittings.
3. Civil Panel Applications List - On the second Wednesday before the hearing day, the
list is posted to the Internet. The justices on the panel are posted to the Internet on the
Wednesday before the sittings.
4. Criminal Panel Applications List - This list and the justices on the panel are posted to
the Internet at the same time as the Sentence List.
5. Single Judge (Chambers) List - This list includes the Chambers applications that are
heard each week. The list is posted to the Internet every Friday (for the upcoming week).
6. Sentence Speak to List - This list includes the appeals that are scheduled to be spoken
to. This list and the justices on the panel are posted to the Internet at the same time as
the Sentence List.
7. Special Sittings - These are usually posted to the Internet after they are set up. If the
matter is a continuation, the judges’ names are also posted at this time; otherwise, the
panel names are posted in the same manner as for the general appeal sittings.

14-120
R.14.33(4) PART 14: APPEALS
There is a slightly out-of-date description of how scheduling works and how important the
time limits are, and what are its aims.¹
This would be relevant also to the Fast Track, which covers
procedural and child appeals. There is criticism of staged adjournment requests concealing the
full delay contemplated.²
The practice of speaking to the list became obsolete, as the case management ofﬁcers ensure
that all appeals keep moving. So that procedure of “speaking to” was deleted from the Rules in
2023.
Costs were imposed on counsel who repeatedly failed to speak to the maintenance appeal
list, despite earlier defaults and various reminders. The appeal had been limping badly. There was
a warning to the profession that costs would be higher next time.³
If two or more appeals are listed for argument on a day of sittings, all will be scheduled for
10: 00 a. m. , so counsel on one can expect to wait for some time before they argue.⁴
A self-represented litigant was involved in several appeals relating to a vexatious-litigant order.
The case management ofﬁcer had ordered the three appeals to be heard on the same day, the
panel to decide the order of the three. The issues among the appeals overlapped and hearing
them the same day would save judicial resources. Again their order of hearing was left to the
panel.⁵
B. Expediting an Appeal
An appeal will not be expedited if there is not enough time to prepare, argue and decide it
before the crucial date, e. g. to save a trial date. Especially if delay is unlikely to work irreparable
harm, still more if the appeal may later become moot.⁶
Sometimes the time is too tight for usefully
expediting the appeal and still usefully and justly hearing and deciding the case; a court house is
not a ﬁre hall.⁷
So then the appeal will not be expedited.
An appeal from a vexatious litigant order had somehow been added to an existing substantive
appeal, but the two were separated, presumably the vexatious litigant order part to be heard ﬁrst.⁸
If counsel desire to set a timetable for an appeal, without agreeing on one, the most efﬁcient
way used to be to apply in chambers to the judge who manages the list in that City. One Justice
sitting in regular daily chambers of the Court of Appeal could also hear that.⁹
But an application
to the Case Management Ofﬁcer might be better. And a panel hearing a motion to dismiss will
often impose a timetable as a condition of not dismissing. Where an appeal (Reference) is case
managed by one justice with tight time deadlines, the full Court of Appeal should be slow to upset
procedural decisions by that Justice.¹⁰

14-122
R.14.35(2) PART 14: APPEALS
Adjournment or Stay
Where a very long trial was under way, the Court of Appeal would not strike an appeal having
to do with summary judgment; but hearing of the appeal was stayed until the end of the trial, on the
defendant’s agreement that the order appealed would not work res judicata.¹
Where adjournment
of an appeal was contested on the grounds that the key witness was elderly, and the appeal
sought a new trial, the appeal was adjourned on condition that the transcript and all other written
evidence by the witness be admissible evidence at the new trial if he was then unavailable.²
An appeal from an interim custody order, complaining of lack of live evidence, was adjourned
because the case management judge was moving to a ﬁnal decision based on live evidence.³
Delay in a fast-track appeal creates a rebuttable presumption of prejudice. But the appeal was
adjourned until after the ﬁnal decision on a pending stay motion.⁴
Cases involving child custody and access are always urgent, and usually almost the only
topics are the best interests of the child. Also, protecting the status quo is a temptation for a party
in family litigation. A lawyer who had arranged a long trip outside the country, should not have
undertaken that retainer when the Practice Directions would have shown that the appeal would be
set down at that time. Adjournment was denied, but counsel's video appearance was arranged.⁵
Courts often impose quite strong terms as a condition of adjourning an appeal.⁶
An adjournment of an appeal to get new evidence was denied, after an unexplained lapse of
two years and notice before of how to adduce new evidence.⁷
On a criminal appellant refusing to
come to Canada for his appeal, or neglecting to get immigration permission to come to Canada,
see R. v. Dauti case.⁸
It is not abuse of process for a receiver-manager of a company to issue a statement of claim,
hoping to sell the suit to a creditor and so realize money for the estate, with no intent to prosecute
the suit himself.⁹
Would that apply to appeals also? An appellant must argue his appeal or abandon
it, not adjourn or “stay” it.¹⁰
It was no ground to adjourn an appeal that the appellant was also complaining about the
judges who had heard the matter before, to the Canadian Judicial Council. Or that he had other
court matters pending, with no details as to how close in time they were, or which was set down
ﬁrst, or whether those close dates could have been avoided.¹¹

DIVISION 4
APPLICATIONS
Subdivision 1
Deciding Applications
Case management ofﬁcers
14.36(1) Unless an enactment or these rules otherwise provide, a case
management ofﬁcer, at the direction of the Court, may assist the Court with
respect to the management of matters before the Court as authorized by section

Footnote
14 of the Court of Appeal Act.
(2) A case management ofﬁcer may consult with an appeal judge or refer any
issue to a single appeal judge or a panel of the Court of Appeal.
(3) Any person affected by an administrative direction of a case management
ofﬁcer may apply to a single appeal judge within one month of the date of
the administrative direction to have the administrative direction rescinded,
conﬁrmed, amended or enforced.
[Alta. Reg.41/14;126/23]
Information Note
Parties who wish to dispense with oral argument, and have the appeal decided based
on the written argument, should contact a case management officer for directions. All of
the materials should be filed simultaneously, unless the application must be filed first to
preserve a time limit. If in urgent matters the applicant wishes to abridge the time limits,
a case management officer should be contacted for directions. The Court of Appeal Act
provides that case management officers may assist with the business of the Court without
the attendance of a judge, including with such matters as:
(a) categorizing an appeal as a fast track appeal or a standard appeal;
(b) enforcing, staying, shortening, or extending times and deadlines;
(c) setting, approving and modifying timetables;
(d) permitting filing of deficient documents, amending documents, and varying the
contents or format of documents to be filed;
(e) restoring appeals by consent;
(f) calling the appeal lists, and setting, adjourning and re scheduling matters for hearing.
1 Carbone v. Whidden 2015 ABCA 161, Calg 1401 0135 AC (one JA May 8).
2 Ford v. Templanza 2015 ABCA 302, Calg 1401 0132 AC (Oct 2).
3 One example is Moshinsky-Helm v. Helm 2022 ABCA 32, Edm 1903 0246 AC (one JA Jan 31) ; Alston v.
Haywood Securs.2022 ABCA 84, Calg 2001 0056 AC (Mar 10) (¶’s 17 ff. ).

14-124
R.14.37(1) PART 14: APPEALS

Related Provisions
14.24(2) (cross appeal in fast track appeals) ; 14.32 (dispensing with oral argument) ;

14.35 (adjourning scheduled appeal) ; 14.55 (advice and directions) ; 14.86 (non-compliant
documents)
On timing of appeals, see also R.14.32 n.
Parties to an appeal can get advice and direction from the case management ofﬁcer, but
neither she nor the court or a judge is to provide legal advice, especially about matters outside
the appeal in question.¹
A decision by a case management ofﬁcer can be reviewed by one justice of appeal, and there
is no right to appeal it directly to a three-justice of appeal panel.²
The case management ofﬁcers are not justices, applications judges, or administrative tribunals,
even though the Legislature has reposed some trust in them. Taking the matter on to a judge
under R.14.36(3) is not called an “appeal” nor a “review”, unlike judges looking over decisions
of applications judges, review ofﬁcers, or assessment ofﬁcers, where it is called an “appeal”. The
decision of a case management ofﬁcer is looked at directly by the judge who makes his or her own
decision.³
The justice of appeal owes no deference to the case management ofﬁcer, but there are
dangers in being too quick to reverse their decisions.⁴
One reason to deny a party costs, was that party's failure to replace defective and prolix
documents with proper ones, as directed by the case management ofﬁcer.⁵
Single appeal judges
14.37(1) Unless an enactment or these rules otherwise require, a single appeal
judge may hear and decide any application incidental to an appeal, including
those that could have been decided by a case management ofﬁcer.
(2) For greater certainty, a single appeal judge may
(a) grant permission to appeal, unless an enactment requires that an
application for permission to appeal must be heard by a panel of the
Court of Appeal,
(b) declare an appeal to be struck, dismissed or abandoned for failure to
comply with a mandatory rule, prior order or direction of the Court of
Appeal,
(c) when a notice of appeal or an application for permission to appeal
is not ﬁled within the time limit, strike the appeal or application or
extend the time to appeal or to seek permission to appeal,

A. Rationale and General Practice
The words “for greater certainty” in R.14.37 show that the following list in that subrule is not
exhaustive.¹
Superior courts possess an inherent jurisdiction to control their own processes, which does
not depend on speciﬁc statutory authority. This includes the ability to case manage litigation, set
and adjourn hearing dates, stay proceedings, and control the ﬁling of documents.²
One reason for single judges of appeal to decide procedural questions and not send them to
panels to decide, is that it would put before the panel hearing the appeal papers not in evidence
in the trial court and so not proper for that panel to hear; or alternatively the court would have to
delay the appeal until a third set of judges of appeal heard the application.³
Alberta practice once was to have a panel hear a motion to extend time to appeal, but the
Practice Directions were amended in 1999 to let one justice do that. The practice is still to require
a panel of three judges to hear new evidence.⁴
Motions to adduce new evidence (if returnable
elsewhere) are adjourned to the panel which will hear the substantive appeal (as are sometimes
motions for leave to intervene).⁵
It is doubtful that one judge can grant a stay of execution which
another appellate judge has refused.⁶
But in practice one judge will hear virtually any motion

1 Betser-Zilevitch v. Prowse Chowne (#2) 2022 ABCA 134, 145 Alta LR(7th) 59 (one JA) (¶’s 14 ff. ).

3 Carbone v. Whidden (#2) 2015 ABCA 177, Calg 1401 0191 AC (one JA May 21) (¶’s 10-13).
4 The panel hearing the appeal decides that. A motion to extend the time to apply for new evidence can be

May 8). An appellant kept asking duty justices of appeal sitting alone to admit fresh evidence, so she was
forbidden to repeat that: Sorochan v. St. Albert Protestant Sep. S. D.2003 ABCA 55 (one JA).
5 R. v. Rain (#2) (CA 1997) 196 AR 315, 43 CRR(2d) 374. One justice of appeal cannot ﬁnally decide whether
to admit new evidence on appeal: Resonnet v. Uttl 2016 ABCA 316, Calg 1601 0187 AC (one JA Oct 17).
The authorities are divided on whether a single judge can permit intervention, but if it is done a single judge
can deal with costs when the appeal is resolved: Imp. Oil v. R. (Lynnwood Ridge Residents’ Assn. ) 2005

R.14.37(2) PART 14: APPEALS
which does not in effect do any of those things.¹
A February 2009 resolution of the Court of Appeal
lets a justice of appeal hear intervention motions.²
Besides R.14.37, s.12 of the Court of Appeal
Act also lets one judge award costs of and incidental to any matter heard by him or her. Where
the Court of Appeal is hearing an original application for a vexatious litigant order (whether the
motion is by others or is the court’s own motion), one justice alone may decide the matter.³
One
justice of appeal can allow the use of initials instead of a party’s name.⁴
After an appeal is decided,
an application for access to court records, or for denial of such access, is within a single justice
of appeal’s jurisdiction. (It is not an application to give “directions required to give effect to any
decision”).⁵
A decision one way on some applications, such as extending time to appeal, in a sense can
end an appeal, but one justice of appeal can still hear them.⁶
Motions panels of three justices of appeal hear applications to have the Court of Appeal
reconsider the correctness of one of its earlier precedents.⁷
If an appeal is arguable and not urgent, it is wrong to move for its summary decision, especially
if the court appealed from has not decided yet, or even ﬁnished gathering evidence.⁸
A motion to
quash an appeal (for mootness) should not have been brought just a week before the appeal was
scheduled to be argued.⁹
A host of statutes, plus R.14.5(1), say that certain types of appeal can only be heard if the
Court of Appeal or one appellate judge ﬁrst gives permission to appeal. In practice one judge
hears such an application. Among civil cases, the most common type is permission to appeal from
a Subdivision and Development Appeal Board. Almost as common is permission to appeal from
a regulatory tribunal. See further R.14.5 and notes to it.
In Alberta one cannot appeal a decision by one judge refusing permission to appeal (unless
maybe that one judge wrongly declined jurisdiction and refused to hear the application on the
merits).¹⁰
No appeal lies because the statute bars an appeal without permission, and permission
was refused. That is even more true where the lower court must give permission and has refused

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.37(2)
it.¹
Whether one could hear an appeal from a grant of permission was not so clear, but it would

Since then, R.14.5(3) was amended in 2016, to
make it clear that it bars appeals from both grants and refusals of leave (permission) to appeal.
B. What One Judge Cannot Do
One appellate judge cannot
(i) dismiss or allow an appeal³
(even if deemed hopeless).⁴
Nor can he or she give substantive
relief from the order appealed, even by calling it interim.⁵
It is doubtful that one judge
alone can remove a party from an appeal, or determine whether one party has standing.⁶
But on case management, see R.14.37n. (But R.14.67 lets one judge order security
for costs on penalty of dismissal. ) The exception in R.14.37(2) (b) requires breach of a
speciﬁc Rule.⁷
(ii) split off one issue from a pending appeal and then decide it. That is not merely incidental
to an appeal, and so is not an exception (by R.14.37) to s.7 of the Court of Appeal Act
making a quorum three judges. The merits must be decided by at least three judges.⁸
(iii) remove one counsel from acting, if there is no conﬂict of interest.⁹
(iv) give mandamus or a similar order under his or her ex-ofﬁcio powers as a King's Bench
justice, when hearing an application ﬁled in the Court of Appeal.¹⁰
(v) Stay a decision of a tribunal (not a judge), unless that is incidental to a live appeal to the
Court of Appeal on application for leave for such an appeal.¹¹
(vi) Give an order about whether a certain law ﬁrm can appear on the matter in other courts
(besides the Court of Appeal), as it plays no role whatever in the appeal, and so is not
incidental to it nor within the jurisdiction of one Justice of Appeal.¹²
A hopeless appeal may be struck off the list by one justice of appeal.¹³
And R.14.65(1) (a) says
that the court or one justice of appeal can restore an appeal to the list.

14-128
R.14.37(2) PART 14: APPEALS
A motion about whether a layman could represent other parties was adjourned, because
an appeal in the same lawsuit was pending on the parallel question of his representing them in
another court. It was undesirable that one justice decide the question before three decide the
almost identical question.¹
There is doubt whether one judge sitting alone can ﬁnd legislation unconstitutional.²
When
the constitutional validity of legislation is raised during an interlocutory motion before one justice
of appeal, that justice can and must decide that question under R.14.37 (and here R.14.67) to
decide the motion between the parties, but cannot declare the legislation void and of no effect
which would require a new proceeding in the Court of King's Bench.³
One justice of appeal has
no inherent jurisdiction.⁴
The Court of Appeal case management judge should not ordinarily exclude an afﬁdavit ﬁled
on an appeal, even a reference from the government, especially on grounds of irrelevance.⁵
Generally, one Justice of Appeal can amend a notice of appeal.⁶
If names of (or substitution
of) new parties were not in issue before the Court of Appeal, on a motion to one justice of appeal
after the appeal is decided, the style of cause cannot be amended, but a motion to King's Bench
would be in order.⁷
C. Miscellaneous
One-justice applications (motions) are heard (probably electronically) at 9: 30 a. m. each
Wednesday or Thursday which is not a statutory holiday. Its written notice can be ﬁled without pre-
booked, but checking beforehand with the Registry to ensure adequate time is recommended.⁸
For urgent applications where long enough notice to the respondents is doubtful, the Registry
will try to accommodate, if extra written materials to support an abridgement of time, are also
ﬁled.⁹
One cannot consolidate an appeal ﬁled as of right with one needing permission to appeal,
where such permission was denied. Consolidation of appeals at a late date may be denied if little
expense would be saved and some be wasted, and independent appeals were unlikely to lead to
inconsistent verdicts.¹⁰
Consolidation of two appeals is difﬁcult if security for costs was ordered on
one, but not the other, but they may be heard consecutively.¹¹

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.38(1)
Leave to appeal from one Justice of Appeal’s decision, to a panel is needed but is a poor
candidate for that. And actual harm or loss should be a clear result, not mere possibility of future
loss. Certainly a reasonable chance of success would be needed. Creating a precedent is a weak
consideration where the facts are unclear.¹
It is very doubtful that a decision by one Justice of Appeal on a stay pending appeal to the
Supreme Court of Canada, can be appealed to three Justices of Appeal.²
Sec.11 of the Court of Appeal Act (barring a judge from sitting on appeal from himself or
herself) does not apply to having sat on another application in the same lawsuit, if it is not the
order that is the subject of the appeal. Any attempt to argue that point would be frivolous and
vexatious.³
Though one justice of appeal alone can give a stay, an appeal to be heard soon may mean the
stay should be partial and very limited.⁴
The Criminal Code gives an automatic stay of a restitution order, pending appeal. But whether
a writ ﬁled during such a stay was a nullity should be determined by one justice of appeal under
R.14.37, not by a King's Bench justice,⁵
The Extradition Act incorporates the procedure in the
Criminal Code, so one justice of appeal can upset bail given by another justice of appeal after the
accused has breached the bail conditions, and can declare the bail forfeit, which may be a civil
matter under the Judicature Act anyway.⁶
Where procedural relief is sought, and the case has a long history, the memorandum of
argument should begin with “a chronology of the relevant events leading to the issue to be
determined”. And applicable past judgments and rulings should accompany, also in chronological
order.⁷
An expedited motion was scheduled, but then the lawyer moving had to put it over because of
an evident conﬂict of interest. The lawyer had to pay costs personally.⁸
One justice of appeal may set aside his or her unentered order, if there is reason to believe
that during argument the justice and one counsel may have misunderstood whether a point was
conceded.⁹
Sometimes a single Justice of Appeal exercises his or her own authority under R.14.37.¹⁰
Court of Appeal panels
14.38(1) A panel of the Court of Appeal may decide any application, including
those that could have been decided by a single appeal judge.

A. Rehearing Appeals¹
1. Timing
A decided appeal’s decision can sometimes be altered after more argument, but only before
the formal Court of Appeal judgment is entered.²
2. Stiff Test
(a) Generally
Rehearing an appeal already decided is exceptional,³

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.38(2)
rare.¹
Exceptional, special or unusual circumstances are needed, and reargument is discouraged.²
The test is high.³
The purpose is not to see if any justice of appeal changed his or her mind.⁴
It is not for
evidence or errors not changing the result,⁵
nor to give a second chance to prove damages at a
new trial,⁶
nor to reargue what has already been argued, as only in extraordinary circumstances
do parties get a second chance to make their case.⁷
There should be no permission to reargue an appeal with arguments which should have been
made the ﬁrst time,⁸
nor for alleged errors which are not germane.⁹
Endless wrangling and never-
ending rehearings must be avoided.¹⁰
Error in the original decision on appeal is a matter for an application to the Supreme Court of
Canada for leave to appeal, not for an application to the Court of Appeal to rehear its decision.¹¹
The tests are reviewed, and authorities cited¹²
as well as similar tests.¹³
Egregious error,¹⁴
fraud, or new evidence not discoverable before, should be shown.¹⁵

14-132
R.14.38(2) PART 14: APPEALS
After a single justice of appeal has refused an application, and after leave to appeal has been
refused, that does not remove the single judge's power to reopen argument on the application
if no formal judgment has been ﬁled; but such reopening will be extremely rare, and the test is
extremely strict.¹
(b) Examples
Proper grounds to reargue an appeal include evidence proving that the Court of Appeal was
misled as to the issue or the record.²
Or that the Court of Appeal overlooked or misapprehended
evidence signiﬁcantly, risking a miscarriage of justice.³
Or patent errors in the decision or
calculations.⁴
The Court of Appeal will rehear a case or allow reargument only if it has overlooked
or misapprehended evidence in a signiﬁcant aspect,⁵
or if it has misled itself or has been misled
as to the record before it,⁶
the nature of the issues,⁷
or the question to be addressed, and there is
a risk of miscarriage of justice.⁸
The test is whether by error the previous decision did not express
the court’s intent because of overlooking or misapprehending the evidence signiﬁcantly, with
resulting risk of miscarriage of justice.⁹
The slip Rule on correcting a judgment is too narrow to
argue the merits.¹⁰
Still less can reargument be got by adducing further evidence, either as new evidence, or as
evidence from another lawsuit.¹¹
The Court of Appeal will not rehear an appeal because of arguments which were raised the
ﬁrst time, even if not discussed in its previous Reasons for Decision. But an afﬁdavit is possible
and often necessary. Nor can the party seeking reargument now take a tack inconsistent which
that it pursued in its original appellate argument. A rehearing was granted on one issue not fully
addressed at the initial hearing, and where all parties raised some new issues.¹²

14-133
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.38(2)
Ordinarily one cannot have an appeal reopened by showing that the case law has changed in
the meantime.¹
Especially if the point of law was obvious or the case law divided, all along.²
A second request to rehear a decided appeal faces an even steeper slope.³
Rehearing an
appeal may be denied because of delay,⁴
especially if the grounds urged for it were substantially
the same as those of an intermediate (failed) application for leave to appeal to the Supreme Court
of Canada.⁵
On application, the Supreme Court of Canada will correct small aspects of a judgment, such
as interest or costs, but is very reluctant to hear an application to change major parts, or to allow
oral reargument.⁶
3. Costs
Requests for rehearing have been too frequent and are to be discouraged by costs.⁷
Extra costs for beating an offer pending appeal should be explored at the appeal hearing, but
may nevertheless be dealt with later.⁸
Rule 10.30(1) allows costs be awarded or set after judgment
has been entered, thus modifying the functus officio rule. Because of the presumption in R.
14.88, often the Court of Appeal makes no speciﬁc direction about costs, but a request for a more
speciﬁc direction should be made to the court within two months. That two-month period can be
extended by the Court.⁹
Absent some speciﬁc undertaking or court order, or litigation misconduct, the costs cannot be
increased for harm to the winning party caused by the delay during the time taken to decide the
appeal.¹⁰
4. Miscellaneous
Applications to rehear are always heard in writing only not orally, to prevent the bandwidth
creep inevitable on an oral argument.¹¹
The Court’s invariable practice is to receive argument on
whether to reopen a decided appeal, in writing only. An oral rehearing would be very unusual. A
hearing before a different panel would be unprecedented.¹²

14-134
R.14.38(2) PART 14: APPEALS
An order denying permission to appeal (by one justice of appeal) is ﬁnal and not open to
reconsideration much later.¹
At ﬁrst the Supreme Court held that it could only rehear appeals, not
leave (permission) motions.²
Then they held it could be done in very rare cases.³
A party failing to appear for argument on an appeal cannot have the judgment later reopened,
except for very special circumstances, and if he was not guilty of any default. Erroneous advice
by the Registry is not enough if an inaccurate question was put to them.⁴
A party who does not
show up for his or her appeal and loses, would need very good, clear medical evidence to excuse
that default.⁵
A Justice of Appeal who had granted leave was persuaded to rescind his decision before entry
and allow it to be reheard before a different Justice. That occurrence is no ground to ignore the
relevant Rules and to try to appeal the reopening, and to try to have a panel of the Court of Appeal
restore the earlier leave to appeal.⁶
A plaintiff repeatedly failed to show up for cross-examination on her afﬁdavits and did not pay
costs awarded against her, and had been found guilty of contempt. A motion for reconsideration
was moot, as the dates for questioning had passed. Scarce judicial resources in Alberta militate
against hearing moot appeals or motions. And a new evidence motion is academic unless a
pending appeal from striking out the applicant’s pleadings succeeds. So the new evidence motion
was stayed.⁷
B. Who Decides Motions When
See R.14.37 and notes to it. On the papers to be used, see Rr.14.40 and 14.41. On learning
which judges will sit on a certain day or case, see the Consolidated Practice Directions, Part A.2.
If a motion for new evidence is returnable before one justice of appeal, it is adjourned to
the panel hearing the appeal proper.⁸
The motion may originally be made returnable before that
panel,⁹
and that is the usual procedure now.
The present Rule sets time deadlines. It is too late to proffer new evidence after argument is
complete and the Court of Appeal is about to give judgment.¹⁰

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.39
A request to reopen and reconsider a decided appeal (even before entry of formal judgment)
requires ﬁling a formal application.¹
A request to reargue an Alberta Court of Appeal decision, in
the same appeal, should be done by formal written application, not by a mere letter.²
Two members of a panel which heard an appeal authorized a third member to decide a post-
pronouncement application made before entry of a formal Court of Appeal judgment.³
If the Court of Appeal rules on whether a statement of claim should be struck out and leaves
it in because the statement of claim is arguable, the Court of Appeal does not ordinarily tell the
plaintiff how to plead his or her case. If more direction is needed or requested, the Court of King's
Bench should give it, especially if there is a case management judge for this appeal. Rule 14.38(2)
(d) does not apply, and does not bar the Court of King's Bench from giving such directions.⁴

[Alta. Reg.41/14;61/23]
Information Note
All of the materials should be filed simultaneously, unless the application must be filed first
to preserve a time limit. If in urgent matters the applicant wishes to abridge the time limits, a
case management officer should be contacted for directions.

Related Provisions
14.27(2) (missing documents in Extracts of Key Evidence) ; 14.28(2) (originals of records in

Court); 14.38 (fresh evidence) ; 14.38 (powers of panel of Court) ; 14.45 (application for fresh
evidence); 14.73 and 14.86 (curing non-compliance) ; 14.87 (format requirements)
On June 23, 1999, the Court of Appeal resolved that motions panels of 3 judges should sit
once a month, rather than every second month. They do not sit in July or August.
All Court of Appeal ﬁlings are now done electronically, and the Consolidated Practice Directions
must be consulted for that topic. See v.2, p.2-8.
A Rule like this one’s subrule (2) embodies natural justice and so should be interpreted
purposively and generously, not technically. It gives protection both to parties, and to non-parties
who are affected and so are entitled to be heard. Someone who may be affected by a motion may
become a party to that motion, even if he or she is not the party to the suit.¹
A would-be intervener kept ﬁling more material on a motion to intervene. The latest material, 2
months after the motion for leave to appeal had been submitted to a panel to decide, was rejected
by the Registrar and not ﬁled. This was afﬁrmed by a judge.²
A judge praised a clear, understandable, well-written memorandum ﬁled for or against an
application, especially when the opponent is self-represented.³
If the Court of Appeal has previously held that certain documents are privileged, an application
to reopen or rehear that decision should not reproduce or attack any of the documents previously
held privileged. If it is truly necessary to disclose it to the court, that should be ﬁled in sealed
format.⁴

A. Criteria for New Evidence¹
1. Generally
Most cases express several tests (often 4)²
for permission to adduce before the Court of
Appeal evidence not tendered in the court appealed from, but they usually boil down to two
hurdles:
* due diligence would not have secured the evidence in time for trial,³
