R.14.45(2) PART 14: APPEALS
* the evidence must be practically conclusive.¹
The Palmer test was “rearticulated” by the Supreme Court in the Barendregi case,²
says the
Saskatchewan Court of Appeal.³
Because of this test, additional evidence is rarely admissible on
appeal.⁴
Due diligence on an application to rehear refers to whether the new evidence could have been
obtained before the appeal, not before the motion to rehear.⁵
“Practically conclusive” means on an important and relevant issue, not about an earlier order
not appealed and now res judicata.⁶
For example, the new evidence should be on a central point,
credible, and consistent with agreed facts.⁷

L. M. B.2007 ABCA 232 (lay litigant warned before trial that letters would not sufﬁce) ; Shearform Constr. v.
Cook Duke & Co.2008 ABCA 247 (knew of and had access to the documents for years) ; Nelson v. Stelter
2011 ABCA 203, 510 AR 153, leave den (SCC 5 Apr ’12) (even though the judge wanted more evidence) ;
Kostic v. Scott Venturo 2022 ABCA 392, Calg 2201 0093 AC (Dec 2) (¶’s 17-19) ; Abbott Labs. v. Spicer
2023 SKCA 55 (Mar 10). That the jurisdiction of the court is challenged is no excuse: Phillips v. Avena
(Phillips) (#4) 2006 ABCA 19, 384 AR 34. Due diligence is a requirement, not merely one thing to weigh: R.
v. Gouthro 2010 ABCA 188, 482 AR 310 (¶ 16) (sentence appeal) ; R. v. Olenguena 2015 ABQB 205, JCC
1013 06116 S1 (Mar 27) (new medical report, but could have been requested any time in last 3 or 4 years) ;
T. L. Y. v. M. L. Y.2015 ABCA 386, Calg 1401 0166 AC (Dec 10) ; R. O. v. D. F. , infra (expert report ordered after
trial court’s judgment, and pre-existing questioning but transcript ordered later). But in a criminal case, due
diligence does not trump the other factors for new evidence; whether it was reasonable now to lead certain
evidence at trial depends on what the appellant reasonably believed at trial would be the other side’s case:
R. v. J. A. A.2011 SCC 17, [2011] 1 SCR 628. See also R. v. Carr 2010 ABCA 386, 493 AR 223, and R. v.
Walle 2012 SCC 41, [2012] 2 SCR 438 (¶’s 69-90). See further the C. P. E. , Chapter 75, Parts D.2(b) and (f).

14-141
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.45(2)
In an appeal from an interlocutory order, the exclusionary rules should be applied strictly.¹
The
usual rules apply also to legislative facts, though they are diluted somewhat.²
Sometimes an application to admit “fresh” evidence does not really tender fresh evidence; it
is some of the disclosure previously ordered and previously available, and is untested by cross-
examination. Allowing such late information would undermine the whole purpose of requiring
disclosure before the ﬁrst hearing, especially after persistent refusal to disclose earlier.³
Aside from the usual tests for new evidence on appeal, the new evidence cannot contradict
agreed facts put before the trial court. They are ordinarily incontrovertible, not merely admissible.
Miscommunication between client and counsel, or ﬁnding more facts later, is not enough to
change that, especially if the counsel has authority, no conﬂict of interest, and is competent.⁴
New evidence cannot be introduced to allow argument of a whole new issue or motion.⁵
These Palmer rules for new evidence can also be applied to a court’s reviewing its own
previous decision with new evidence.⁶
The tests for new evidence on appeal are used more laxly when the best interests of children
are involved.⁷
2. Events Since Trial
The Supreme Court of Canada throws doubt on some earlier cases, stating that new evidence
about post-trial occurrences must be linked to an error by the trial judge, not merely to the appeal
court’s curative power.⁸
The Court of Appeal refused new evidence proffered by both sides, as
the principle of assessing personal injury damages is once-and-for-all. Nor would the Court of
Appeal receive evidence about the downturn which occurred between the end of the evidence
and granting the trial judge’s judgment, as neither party had tried to adduce any such evidence.⁹

14-142
R.14.45(2) PART 14: APPEALS
Here is that older dubious case law. May an appeal the court make an exception and adduce
further evidence if it is about facts which occurred during trial, or after trial?¹
In trial courts, even
such “new events” evidence is dubious.²
Any matrimonial suit always sees smaller changes along the way. So that is no excuse for
bypassing the Palmer test. And if there had been major changes, the proper remedy would be to
move to reopen the trial.³
3. Consent or Unfair Conduct
More dependable case law allows admission of new evidence in several situations: consent;
or a statute allowing that; or the respondent doing something to thwart the appellant from leading
the evidence at trial; or the respondent having a duty to provide the information;⁴
or the trial not
yet having been held and the basic facts were not contested and there would be no signiﬁcant
prejudice.⁵
Palmer does not apply to evidence going to the validity of the trial process itself, nor to
evidence to get an original remedy in the Court of Appeal (such as a stay for abuse).⁶
There is no want of due diligence at trial, and so new important evidence can be led on appeal,
where the evidence existed before trial, but its signiﬁcance or necessity could not reasonably be
seen before both counsel had closed their cases (e. g. at the argument stage).⁷
There may be continuing disclosure obligations on the Crown pending a criminal appeal, but
it would have to be shown reasonably possibly helpful for an orthodox new evidence application,
and that Crown duty only covers what the Crown possesses or controls. Information from someone
other than the Crown must meet the O’Connor 4 steps.⁸
If an appellant grounds the appeal (or part of it) on ineffectiveness of the appellant’s counsel
in the court below, generally the appellant must expressly waive privilege.⁹
4. Evidence by Respondent
It is possible that a respondent might adduce new evidence on appeal, but even a respondent
has to comply with the usual tests above.¹⁰
The Palmer rules for new evidence are especially
important if the respondent wants to ﬁle rebuttal evidence to the appellant’s new evidence, and
the respondent seems to meet the Palmer tests.¹¹

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.45(2)
5. Statutory Power
The usual rules for admitting new evidence on appeal do not apply where a statute gives an
appeal court original power to address breaches of Charter by a Minister.¹
B. Need for Permission from Court of Appeal
See also R.14.70.
On appeal, one cannot ﬁle or refer to evidence which was not tendered to the tribunal appealed
from,²
unless one ﬁrst brings an application and gets permission from the Court of Appeal to do
so.³
Without permission, an appellant cannot put new evidence into his Extracts of Key Evidence,
nor cite it in his factum, especially after the time to move for new evidence has expired without
such a motion.⁴
Nor can a party merely put new factual material into his or her authorities.⁵
So the
Court of Appeal may just ignore such offending materials.⁶
Nor can an appellant produce new evidence or argument and have it placed in the appeal
record, by ﬁling with the Court of Appeal an application and afﬁdavits in the court appealed from,
e. g. a motion to rehear or reconsider the earlier motion which he lost and is now being appealed.
Such material can get before the Court of Appeal only by an application to the Court of Appeal
panel to receive fresh evidence.⁷
The same is true of material for a stay of execution.⁸
If there
is no application to adduce new evidence, factual submissions in argument on the appeal, not
supported by the record of evidence, must be ignored.⁹
An application to adduce new evidence must be ﬁled before the applicant ﬁles a factum, so
applying late needs leave. Though the threshold is low, leave was refused here. Relevant are
the reason for the delay, the effect of late ﬁling on the appeal itself, and prima facie merit to the
application for fresh evidence. Generally (though not always), due diligence is required and was

R.14.45(2) PART 14: APPEALS
lacking here. And failure to show the relevance or importance of the new evidence was also a bar
to the leave.¹
The Court of Appeal had refused an application to adduce new evidence as being too late.
The party seeking leave in Ottawa had put that same rejected material in the record offered on its
motion for leave to appeal, but a Supreme Court judge struck that out.²
An application for permission to appeal from a subdivision and development appeal board can
include some new evidence, since this application is before the appeal is heard, and s.689(1)
(a) of the Municipal Government Act says “On the hearing of the appeal. . . ”. And a few types of
grounds of appeal, especially procedural or natural-justice ones, may require more evidence even
for the appeal itself.³
The Court of Appeal has a strong presumption that trial counsel were reasonably competent,
so an argument by appellant that his counsel was not, requires evidence to rebut the presumption.⁴
It is very hard to argue incompetence of counsel without leading evidence and waiving privilege
for the relevant evidence.⁵
If the evidence is about alleged incompetence of trial counsel, then the
appellant must waive solicitor-client privilege and must agree to testify.⁶
C. Procedure
One justice of appeal alone ordinarily cannot hear a motion to adduce new evidence on
appeal; the panel hearing the appeal must decide that.⁷
However, where the Court of Appeal
is about to hear a constitutional reference, a late afﬁdavit full of new expert evidence, leaving
not enough time before the hearing for the opposing parties to get their own experts and cross-
examine, and reply with another afﬁdavit, may be rejected by the case management judge as
prejudicial because late.⁸
The appellant’s factum should deal with both the situation if the evidence is admitted, and with
the situation if it is not.⁹
The time limit in R.14.45 should not be extended (by one justice) where the evidence is clearly
totally irrelevant.¹⁰
It may not always be necessary to allow cross-examination on the new evidence to decide
whether it is admissible.¹¹
If cross-examination is allowed, it should be held in front of an examiner,
well before the Court of Appeal hearing, and the three justices of appeal should merely get a

Application to reconsider a previous decision
14.46 An application to reconsider a previous decision of the Court of Appeal
must be ﬁled and served and must be returnable prior to the ﬁling of, and prior
to the deadline for ﬁling, the applicant’s factum.
[Alta. Reg.41/14]

Related Provisions
13.5(2) and 14.2 (extending time) ; 14.38(2) (e) (application to reconsider precedent) ; 14.72

(no reconsideration without order)
The principles under which leave to reargue past Court of Appeal precedents is granted, are
discussed in R. v. Arcand,⁸
and more fully elsewhere above.⁹
Such a motion is heard ahead of the appeal by a motions panel of three justices of appeal.¹⁰
It cannot be given at the hearing of the appeal by the panel hearing the merits.¹¹
Conversely, the
earlier panel only gives leave, and does not decide whether the earlier precedent was mistaken.¹²

R.14.47 PART 14: APPEALS
If such leave is granted, the Chief Justice of Alberta is notiﬁed, and she decides whether the
appeal will be heard by three justices of appeal or ﬁve.¹
The relevant factors are age of the impugned precedent, any settled public expectation,
appellate authority elsewhere, overlooking statute or binding authority, “some simple, obvious,
demonstrable ﬂaw, ” and whether a memorandum or a circulated reserved decision is in issue.²
Leave to reconsider a previous Court of Appeal precedent was denied because it was recent,
the alleged conﬂict with Supreme Court of Canada precedents was not apparent, and the alleged
errors were small.³
No leave is needed if the topic is not legal, and was a conclusion on the facts of that case, and
so it can just be distinguished.⁴
Application to restore an appeal
14.47 An application to restore an appeal that has been struck, dismissed or
deemed abandoned
(a) must be filed and served as soon as reasonably possible,
and
(b) must be returnable no later than
(i) for a standard appeal, 6 months after having been struck,
dismissed or deemed abandoned, or
(ii) for a fast track appeal, 3 months after having been struck,
dismissed or deemed abandoned. [Alta. Reg.41/14;36/20]

Related Provisions
14.37 (applications incidental to an appeal) ; 14.62 (delay) ; 14.64 (missing deadlines) ; 14.65

(restoring appeals)
This Rule was heavily reworded in 2020, to make ﬁling and service due as soon as reasonably
possible.
The six months under this Rule can be extended, and the motions to extend it and to restore
can be combined into one.⁵
Though times under the Rules may be extended, if this time is not
extended, once it passes the appeal cannot be restored. Restoring it depends on the reason for
the delay and the strength of the appeal.⁶
On the tests for restoring an appeal, see R.14.65n.

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.48
Stay pending appeal
14.48 An application to stay proceedings or enforcement of a decision pending
appeal may be made
(a) to the judge who made that decision, or
(b) to a single appeal judge, whether or not the application was made to
the judge who made the decision, and whether or not that application
was granted or dismissed. [Alta. Reg.41/14]
Information Note
The preferred practice is to apply first to the judge who made the decision, but the rule does
not preclude a direct application to a single appeal judge.

Related Provisions
14.37 (applications incidental to an appeal) ; 14.68 (no automatic stay)

A. General
1. References
There are general Canadian references on this topic.¹
On the tests for a stay, see Part C below.
2. Automatic Stay?
The general rule in Alberta is that there is no automatic stay created by a pending appeal.²
The
fact that a party has announced his intention to seek a stay of an order (but never has so moved)
does not oblige his opponent to withhold further action.³
The legislation is different in many other
provinces, and under some federal statutes. But an application to the Supreme Court of Canada
for leave to appeal creates no automatic stay.⁴
However, a few Alberta statutes create what is much like an automatic stay, by requiring a
certiﬁcate of no appeal to act on a judgment: note the Divorce Act, the (federal) Bankruptcy and
Insolvency Act, and the Land Titles Act.⁵
In all other cases, one can ignore a pending appeal and
proceed to act on an order or judgment by enforcement (execution) or otherwise, unless and until
the Court of Appeal reverses the order or judgment, or until a judge gives an express stay.

2006 ABCA 121, 391 AR 202 (one JA). Somewhat similar reasoning was applied to the Land Titles Act, in
Indus Dev. Corp. v. Ansar Dev. Corp.2015 ABCA 400, Calg 1501 0299 AC (one JA Dec 17) ; cf. Green
Theme Design v.0974016 B. C.2024 ABKB 7, JCC 2101 07041 (Jan 4) (¶’s 108-12). The need for a
certiﬁcate of no appeal, and lifting it, is not the same as a stay of a stay: Starke Cap. Corp. v. Strategic
Acquisition, infra.

14-148
R.14.48 PART 14: APPEALS
3. Informal Interim Stay
There can be a brief stay of enforcement before appeal, even without a formal written
application, and that is fairly common.¹
Ordinarily such an interim stay needs an undertaking to
ﬁle and serve a notice of appeal at once.
4. Relevant Appeal Needed
Where there was no (outstanding) appeal from an order nisi/order for sale in a foreclosure, but
a later appeal from a purely incidental order for possession, and the status quo endangered the
property, there should not be a stay.²
There cannot be a stay where the appeal court would not hear that class of appeal, or where
no further proceedings after decision (except for costs) are contemplated.³
There cannot be a stay
if leave to appeal is required but has been refused.⁴
But there can be a stay once leave to appeal
is granted.⁵
The court has no jurisdiction to stay an order if any appeal from it has been struck (by the
Registrar).⁶
A motion for a stay is especially unnecessary where it could do no good even if successful,
because the order to be appealed and stayed is not the one putting the operative duty on the
party moving.⁷
The stay would have to be of the very thing ordered below, not a matter merely resisted.⁸
No stay is possible if the act complained of was not directed by the court below in a judgment
or order, e. g. publication of the trial judge’s reasons.⁹
A single justice of appeal can stay a decision only if it was by a judge (not a tribunal), or there
is pending before the Court of Appeal an appeal or an application for leave to appeal, and the stay
is incidental to that appeal.¹⁰
5. Nothing to Stay
A stay is a court order that suspends or narrows (for a deﬁned period) the effect of the previous
court order. A stay may direct a party or third party whose acts affect the interests which a party
seeks to protect in the proceedings, to refrain from taking some act authorized by the previous
court order.¹¹

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.48
If the King's Bench refused all relief, strictly speaking there cannot be a stay of enforcement,
as there is nothing to stay. At best it could be an injunction pending appeal, and the tests for that
are stiffer than for a stay.¹
A stay order must have utility, so it requires some possible future act
which the judgment appealed authorizes or compels and which would be efﬁcacious if performed,
not an unauthorized act.²
Nor can there be a stay of a declaration about law; precedent cannot
be suspended by a stay, as a stay only affects that suit and parties, not other law suits.³
Where
a court ﬁnds that some aspects of legislation are contrary to the Charter, it has to balance the
interests of the public and those relying on the statute, with the interests of those whose Charter
rights are being violated by the legislation. Often that will mean a partial declaration of invalidity
of the statute, not a full or unconditional one. The restrictions on validity of the statute may be
as to topics or statutory provisions, as to time periods (such as a temporary stay in invalidity of
the whole statute), or as to certain persons only (such as those whose own Charter rights are
infringed). Some of these “tailored remedies” (restrictions on invalidity of the statute) are rarer or
harder to select, than others. This is discussed at length and with differing criteria, in the A. -G.
Ont. v. G. decision.⁴
If a King's Bench judge is staying enforcement, an appeal court judge cannot stay execution
of a future unpronounced King's Bench order.⁵
On what to do if some collateral consequence of the decision below was not ordered by the
judge below, see the Dugandzic decision.⁶
6. Legislation Needed
A Court of Appeal has no inherent power to order a stay, only the powers given by legislation.⁷
One must distinguish a stay of execution from a stay of proceedings.⁸
On whether this Rule applies to appeals from other tribunals, see the Civil Procedure
Encyclopedia.⁹

R.14.48 PART 14: APPEALS
On stays pending appeal to the Supreme Court of Canada, see the Civil Procedure
Encyclopedia.¹
Judges of a provincial court of appeal can also give stays pending an application
to the Supreme Court of Canada for leave to appeal.²
The Supreme Court of Canada’s individual
judges have power to give bail pending appeal to the Supreme Court of Canada from contempt,
even civil contempt.³
Where a big penalty was levied and then paid, but much of the ﬁndings of
guilt were set aside, the amounts should not be repaid before the Supreme Court of Canada
dismissed a leave application.⁴
Rule 14.48 seems to have been applied by analogy to a stay pending appeal from a Master
to a King's Bench judge.⁵
B. Nugatory Appeals
Part of this topic heavily overlaps with the irreparable harm discussed in subpart C.3 below.
But irreparable harm to the appellant is not the only evil of a nugatory appeal; it abrogates the right
to the appeal itself and any of its remedies.⁶
Rules 14.48 and 14.71 go some distance to let the appeal court later correct what was done
in the Court of King's Bench which has to be corrected.⁷
But sometimes things done in the meantime before the appeal is decided would be irreversible.
The appeal would then be called “nugatory”. That may come from the law, which will not undo a
company amalgamation,⁸
nor reverse a transfer of land after purchasers for value have intervened.

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.48
Or it may come from the facts, as where money was levied by execution and given to a poor plaintiff
who has since spent it, or where a company’s location and business are to be changed,¹
or where
Ministers were ordered to answer discovery questions.²
Payment to someone able to pay is not
irreversible, nor is running up expenses remediable later by costs.³
Or irreversibility may come
from both, as where the order appealed overrules a claim to privilege and orders inspection.⁴
In
such situation, because of irreversible intervening acts, the appeal is said to be nugatory.⁵
In Part C below, the tests for a stay are reviewed, one being irreparable harm. A nugatory
appeal is a good example of irreparable harm.⁶
Irreparable harm may be presumed if refusing a
stay of execution could render the appeal nugatory, even if it is about discovery, not substantive
issues.⁷
Irreversible harm refers to harm which will follow the stay application, not irreversible harm
suffered before the application. Harm to reputation may be cured by the appeals later being
allowed.⁸
Irreversability of some small aspect is not enough, e. g. loss of one reserved date for an
application, or negatoriness of a premature appeal which could be repeated later.⁹
But generally
irreparable harm refers to impossibility of a later effective cure, not to size.¹⁰
Preventing such frustration of the appeal is the usual reason for a stay.¹¹

R.14.48 PART 14: APPEALS
An appeal from an injunction is not necessarily negatory if it would be heard after the injunction
expired, if the injunction was interlocutory and its undertaking as to damages would be about
ongoing harm to the defendant; but here the undertaking would prevent irreparable harm.¹
C. Tests for Granting a Stay²
1. Onus and Evidence
Prima facie the respondent is entitled to the fruits of its trial victory.³
A stay of enforcement
is the exception⁴
and so requires evidence.⁵
Evidence with details is needed, not a mere bald
conclusion.⁶
If the law applied in the ﬁrst court is public, public interest is presumed and so is
public harm, and the applicant must rebut that.⁷
And the seeming strength of the appeal (in light of the standard of review) is relevant.⁸
2. Criteria for Non-Money Judgment
(a) The Tests
The usual tests for a stay of execution of a non-money judgment are much like those for an
interlocutory injunction.⁹
The party seeking the stay has the onus of giving evidence to prove¹⁰
three things.

the order appealed, not from the act of some other person or body.³

On irreparable harm, see also Part B above. Irreparable harm may also be seen as an
aspect of the third factor, balance of convenience.²
The third criterion is that the balance of convenience of factors³
favors giving the stay.⁴
Balance
means the harm from no stay even if the appeal later succeeded, would be worse than the harm
from a stay if the appeal later failed. Balance of convenience involves many possible factors,
varying case to case.⁵
Years of delay in seeking the stay can also push the balance against the
applicant.⁶
Balance of convenience is often the heart of the analysis.⁷
Only at that stage does the
court (or judge) look at relative factors, especially at harm to the respondent to the motion if the
judgment is not stayed.⁸

14-156
R.14.48 PART 14: APPEALS
If balance of convenience is in issue where a governmental authority with a duty to protect
the public interest has made an order, the court must assume some harm to the public if the
court restrains those actions, whether or not the Charter is invoked. For example, the Municipal
Government Act requires every municipality to have a land use bylaw to achieve the overall greater
public interest.¹
The balance of convenience covers all the interests affected, including preserving
court resources and shielding other litigants from abusive behavior.²
Public interest should be looked at only in the third stage (about balance of inconvenience),
not at the second stage (about irreparable harm). Even though the 3 parts are not watertight
compartments. Public interest only sufﬁces if the applicant represents the public interest, e. g. is
the Attorney-General.³
(b) Children
The tripartite test is modiﬁed in a child custody case,⁴
because the interests which matter are
those of the child, not usually those of the parents.⁵
It is proper to consider together the second and third branches of the test, if the best interests of
a child are in issue,⁶
and then balance of convenience of the parents may have much less weight,
especially in a Hague Convention case.⁷
In a child welfare matter, it is very difﬁcult to separate
irreparable harm and balance of convenience.⁸
And in such a case, the court can legitimately look
at how strong the appeal seems to be.⁹
In such a case status quo can be weighty, and several
residence shifts can be disruptive for a child, so staying an order to change residence may be
somewhat easier to get.¹⁰
Hague Convention child-return orders are not the same as the usual custody or place-of-
residence orders. The Hague Convention requires promptness.¹¹
The balance of convenience may
be affected by one party's history of disobeying or thwarting performance of, previous orders on
the same subject.¹²

14-157
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.48
(c) Miscellaneous
The proposed stay must have some utility; barring an act by someone already deprived of
authority to do that act may be useless.¹
It is not possible to stay (before an appeal is decided) the
precedential effect of the decision below.²
Granting or withholding a stay is no kind of statement that the appeal has or does not have
merit.³
An appeal court should not micromanage procedure and timing in the trial-level court.⁴
A tribunal asked to stay its decision pending an appeal can craft conditions on the stay
appropriate to its ﬁndings of guilt.⁵
On terms and conditions, and security’s effect on the balance
of convenience, see subpart 3 below.
A stay may be denied if there is no time to hear and decide the appeal before it would become
nugatory or moot, and it would be unjust or impossible to expedite it sufﬁciently.⁶
The stay may be partial, and limited to the part of the proceedings which would cause
irreparable harm,⁷
or would be freestanding.⁸
But where a stay is sought between ﬁnding liability
and awarding or imposing a penalty or remedy, what is sought is really a stay of proceedings, not
of enforcement. That is litigation by instalments, which involves many evils, and usually should be
avoided, which is a weighty consideration.⁹
The law on staying constitutional decisions pending appeal is reviewed.¹⁰
The usual 3-part test from injunction law may not ordinarily apply to a stay pending appeal
from a bankruptcy Registrar to a judge?¹¹
There are dicta that equitable defences (such as not having clean hands) may apply to stay
motions.¹²
If the stay would be tantamount to an injunction, it is equitable relief and the appellant seeking
the stay must do equity and act fairly?¹³

14-158
R.14.48 PART 14: APPEALS
3. Criteria for Money Judgment
(a) Criteria
Where a money judgment is appealed, the onus is on the appellant to justify a stay.¹
At one time it was sometimes said that the tests are arguable appeal,²
irreparable harm,³
and
exceptional circumstances.⁴
Such a stay is not easily given.⁵
But most⁶
of the newer cases drop that separate test for money judgment cases, and use⁷
the
same 3-part test as in non-money cases.⁸
One case reviews the case law, and concludes that
the three-part test for non-money judgments is adequate and preferable here, and so no different
test is apt.⁹
Irreparable harm usually, but not always, means an injury which cannot be properly
compensated by awarding damages.¹⁰
Having to satisfy a money judgment, even by liquidating
assets, is not irreparable harm.¹¹
Nor is the usual stress of litigation.¹²
Sometimes being forced to pay money can cause irreparable harm, but it would take evidence.¹³
Paying money which an impecunious opponent will not be able to refund if the appeal succeeds,
would be irreparable harm.¹⁴

14-159
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.48
In exceptional circumstances, a stay may be granted even if the tripartite test is not met, as the
fundamental question is whether a stay is just and equitable in all the circumstances.¹
Money judgments raise issues which are much less common with other types of judgment.
For a money judgment, usually the key questions are whether the plaintiff would be able to
repay the money if the appeal succeeded,²
and whether the defendant’s assets are likely to
disappear or be dissipated pending appeal.³
A plaintiff is not to be lightly deprived of the fruits of
his or her victory.⁴
That the appellant is acting in the public interest is irrelevant.⁵
Where a fund or
other property is already held in court or in trust, the court tends to leave it there, pending appeal.⁶
Paying huge solicitor-client costs a few months before the appeal was heard, was the wrong
balance of convenience.⁷
Evidence of irreparable harm must be more than speculative, hypothetical, or merely arguable,
and mere administrative incconvenience is not enough.⁸
To get a stay of monetary contempt sanctions, it is not enough to swear baldly that one
lacks funds to pay; clear convincing or cogent evidence is needed. An expensive lifestyle and
unexplained disappearance of assets can be evidence the other way. Contempt ﬁnes are owed to
the court, not to other parties. Costs against a contemnor have a sound policy basis.⁹
(b) Security
Often the real issue is whether either party is willing to offer any kind of security,¹⁰
because it
affects both irreparable harm, and balance of convenience. So the judge will often give a stay on
terms that the party getting the stay (appellant) do something, or will withhold a stay on terms that

Procrane (Sterling Crane) v. Thompson 2016 ABCA 61, Edm 1603 0023 AC (one JA Mar 2) ; Kostic v.

334 (one JA) ; Pyne v. W. C. B.2006 ABCA 370, 401 AR 225 (one JA) (enough money released to fund
appeal); Dallin v. Montgomery (#3), supra. The onus of proof on the appellant defendant is not heavy:
Dallin v. Montgomery (#3), supra. That is what irreparable harm means in this context: Colborne Cap. v.
542775, supra; cf.420 Invs. v. Tilray, supra (¶’s 21-23) ; cf. Giesbrecht v. Prpick, supra (¶ 22). Inability
was found and a stay given, in Bodner v. R.2002 ABCA 20, 299 AR 150 (one JA). The government sought
a stay of execution pending trial, fearing that the other defendants would not be able to repay if the appeal
succeeded. A non-party subrogated plaintiff had large cash assets, and undertook to repay all sums paid if
the appeal succeeded, so no stay was granted: Fullowka v. Pinkerton’s of Can. and Gov’t. of the N. W. T.
2006 NWTCA 02 (one JA).

And security or other terms can often dramatically

But it

14-161
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.48
Of course a stay will be denied if the duty to pay costs is not affected by the appeal, where its
subject is different and will not impact the costs award.¹
4. Lifting Automatic Stay
Where a statute imposes an automatic stay of execution on ﬁling an appeal, but by statute
a judge can lift the stay, the judge asked to lift it uses the familiar 3-part test for interlocutory
injunctions.²
On transitional legislation and whether an automatic statutory stay comes within the words
“granted a stay”, see the Medovarski case.³
D. Examples
Many other examples are given in Parts A to C above: see the facts recited in parentheses
after case citations in the footnotes.
Stays of execution have been granted of a judgment to build an expensive school for a small
number of students, the respondent not having much money,⁴
of a disciplinary decision on a
strong appeal with danger to the public,⁵
of a declaratory judgment,⁶
of a restructuring of a society
hard to undo later,⁷
where the appeal is scheduled for early hearing,⁸
where the money to be
received was needed to live on,⁹
or where the order appealed would have required litigating the
same topic twice.¹⁰
A stay may be denied where many people are affected, and prompt arbitration is desirable,¹¹
where the applicant lives outside Alberta and has no ﬁnancial interest in the appeal's outcome,¹²
where the prisoner seeking bail is likely to hide assets,¹³
or he could have had his appeal heard by
them, if he had been prompt,¹⁴
the appeal is of a preliminary ruling in a hearing not ﬁnished yet,¹⁵
or where the stay would use the money received to fund the appeal.¹⁶

R.14.48 PART 14: APPEALS
Contested examination for discovery questions to a party ordered but under appeal, may be
temporarily ordered answered in writing and temporarily sealed.¹
Discovery of a new party, and
new legislation, in an old slow-moving suit, was stayed, as there was no risk that the information
would become unavailable.²
Election contests produce unique factors to consider under the familiar tripartite test.³
An interlocutory injunction was stayed pending appeal, partly because there should not be
interlocutory electoral relief about the same as ﬁnal relief.⁴
If a stay pending appeal happens to give the appellant all that she is entitled to, the appeal
should be dismissed.⁵
Where support of a child is involved, the child's best interests are paramount, which inﬂuences
all three branches of the test.⁶
One judge could give interim maintenance pending appeal, but
should not where no change of circumstances is shown, and a very substantial change would
be necessary after two concurrent courts had denied it.⁷
Though the government’s Maintenance
Enforcement Branch has statutory power to collect maintenance payments, it is doubtful that it
can question the propriety of a judge's stay of execution, even on consent.⁸
E. Who to Apply To
On whether the stay application must be a panel of three Justices of Appeal, see n. A.4 above.
There used to be doubt about whether one could apply both to a King's Bench justice and
to a justice of appeal for a stay,⁹
but then the Rule was amended to make it clear that one can
do that.¹⁰
Alberta practice has been that one justice of appeal can rehear a failed application to a
King's Bench judge for a stay pending appeal, if the ﬁrst application was not to the trial judge. If a
King's Bench judge is asked to give a stay pending appeal of his own decision, he or she cannot
simply decline to do so on the grounds of not caring to hear such applications. Nor does it become
impractical for that King's Bench judge to do so simply because he or she cannot have as full
control over conditions for the prosecution of the appeal as a Court of Appeal justice would.¹¹

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.48
whether the justice of appeal can still act where the King's Bench justice refused to stay is now
subject to some doubt because of an appeal from Ontario calling them for special circumstances.¹
One Justice of Appeal may vary or terminate a stay granted by the trial judge.²
A trial judge
had ordered discharge of a caveat and lis pendens, with a stay of that discharge pending appeal.
To “stay” that stay would be immediate discharge, and so a variation of the judgment, not a
stay or mere suspension. One justice of appeal cannot give the “stay” requested, as it would be
immediate relief in effect allowing the appeal. Leaving the stay given by the trial judge in place,
would not work irreparable harm in any event.³
But once one justice of appeal has refused a stay, it is doubtful that another justice of appeal
can interfere with that decision.⁴
Refusal of a stay by one appellate judge cannot be challenged
before three, whether by way of appeal or a new motion?⁵
A justice of appeal who heard a stay application can later be a member of the panel which
decides the appeal.⁶
The King’s Bench justice gave a very short interim injunction (resembling a stay) until an
appellate justice could consider an injunction or stay, and maybe expediting the appeal.⁷
When there is an appeal to the Court of Appeal from the College of Physicians and Surgeons,
a stay of execution pending appeal can be given by a King's Bench justice?⁸
Rule 14.48 was applied by analogy to an appeal from a Master (now Applications Judge) to a
King's Bench judge.⁹
On a stay pending a motion for leave to appeal to the Supreme Court of Canada, see the Alta.
Enr. v. Grant case.¹⁰
F. Delay and End of Stay
One condition always lies on counsel getting a stay: that the appeal will be prosecuted promptly;
if that condition is not expressed, then it is implicit.¹¹
Therefore, it is often better to give a stay for a
short time, not simply until whenever the appeal is heard.¹²
If the appeal limps along slowly, then
the court may either speed it up, lift the stay,¹³
or refuse to extend times and so kill the appeal.¹⁴

14-164
R.14.48 PART 14: APPEALS
A stay may be refused if the appellant delays in seeking it.¹
But how quickly the appeal
can be heard is also relevant, as is conditions to remove prejudice from the delay. Those extra
considerations may be enough to overcome past delay by the appellant.²
An existing stay can be lifted or narrowed for change of circumstances, such as a partial
abandonment of the appeal.³
A stay pending appeal is not part of the judgment appealed from, and the judge who gave it is
not functus and can later lift the stay, which is discretionary.⁴
For purposes of an undertaking to hold funds until an appeal is “disposed of”, disposition
does not occur as long as there are any live issues. If leave to appeal is denied, but a motion is
brought claiming that leave is not necessary, only when the court disagrees and holds that it has
no jurisdiction, is the appeal “disposed of”.⁵
A stay may be lifted if complying with the order later makes the topic moot.⁶
G. Miscellaneous
A stay of an order to sell property (in a contested estate matter) was denied. The applicant
seeking the stay had deﬁed three court orders and returned to live rent-free on the property, so
he lacked clean hands.⁷
It was contempt to argue a stay motion without revealing to the judge that the matter was moot,
because the money had gone despite the order sought to be stayed.⁸
A judge told a litigant that
if he wanted a stay pending appeal from a master, he should apply for it on notice. He applied ex
parte and got a time-limited stay. That was improper conduct.⁹
A party does not have to move for a stay of execution, and so there is no general rule that
costs of such a failed motion should be left to the panel hearing the appeal.¹⁰
On an application for a stay pending appeal, the less said by the justice about the merits the
better, especially as the chambers justice deciding on a stay may be a member of the later panel
hearing the appeal.¹¹
A stay was given conditionally on expediting the application for leave to appeal.¹²
An expedited
date was scheduled for a stay application, but then the lawyer applying had to put it over because
he belatedly found a conﬂict of interest which should have been readily apparent from the outset.
The lawyer had to pay personally thrown-away costs.¹³

14-165
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.49
An application in the trial court to set aside a trial verdict is no ground to stay a parallel appeal
from the same verdict?¹
Common law and statute let a superior court stay execution of an arbitration award enforceable
as its judgment. Many grounds could support (lead to) such a stay, including promissory estoppel.²
There is an explanation of the evils of a dubious appeal from divorce itself not being prosecuted,
especially with no evident merits, as there is no power to lift the de facto stay of execution
entailed.³
A statutory stay of a Court of Appeal decision because a leave motion has been ﬁled with the
Supreme Court of Canada is not precluded because that motion is 7 days late.⁴
But if there are no
important legal issues, and the urgent matter has dragged on for some time, one justice of appeal
should not give a stay.⁵
Though that would make the appeal moot, the Supreme Court of Canada
could still hear it if the legal topic were that important.⁶
Summary judgment was on an Alberta suit on an Arizona judgment, but with enforcement
stayed until the Arizona appeals were ﬁnished. An Alberta judge would not give any further stay,
but if the Arizona appeals were dismissed and the Alberta ones still pending, the defendant could
apply again for a stay.⁷
A Master invoked the court’s supposed inherent power to stay a tribunal’s decision because it
had been got in breach of the rules of natural justice, without satisfying the usual tests for a stay.⁸
To set aside a court-ordered stay, one uses the familiar 3-part test, and the court may address
other factors too. On balance of convenience, the fact the judgment is to pay money favors no
stay.⁹

at the hearing of the application unless the single appeal judge or the panel of
the Court of Appeal otherwise permits. [Alta. Reg.41/14]

R.14.50 PART 14: APPEALS
Related Provisions
14.23(3) (no respondent’s factum) ; 14.41 (response to application) ; 14.43 (response to

application)
The reasons for this Rule are that surprise should be avoided, and the judge should be
adequately prepared. In this case, the judge only allowed the respondents to state that they
opposed the motion.¹
Time limits for oral argument
14.50 Unless otherwise permitted,
(a) subject to clause (b), oral argument on an application, including a
reply, before a single appeal judge or a panel of the Court of Appeal
may not exceed 15 minutes for each party to the application,
(b) oral argument on an application for permission to appeal, including
a reply, may not exceed 30 minutes for each party to the application,
and
(c) consolidated applications are to be treated as one application for the
purpose of this rule.²
[Alta. Reg.41/14]

Related Provisions
6.10 (electronic hearings) ; 14.32 (time limits on appeals)

For technique, i. e. advocacy questions and suggestions, see Coté, Systematic Advocacy
(Juriliber 2017), Chap.19, Parts C, D, E, F, and N.
From spring 2020 to spring 2022 the Covid epidemic led the Court of Appeal to hear all
appeals and applications virtually (by video hookup), or in writing only. Part of a May 17, 2022
notiﬁcation in the “News & Announcements” part of the Alberta Courts website says that effective
May 2, 2022 the Court of Appeal will hear live all matters heard by a panel of three justices of
appeal. However, those heard by one justice of appeal alone, and other matters, will continue to
be heard virtually (electronically), says a January 23, 2023 Notice to the Profession & Public. The
Bar prefers such virtual one-Justice of Appeal arguments.
Oral argument of an application is an opportunity for counsel to clear up misunderstandings,
supply emphasis or focus, and learn what the panel of justices are interested in, or disagree with.
And the applicant can rebut the respondent’s memorandum. Above all, oral argument lets counsel
answer the panel’s (or one justice’s) questions. It is not the occasion (nor long enough) to explain
the facts or issues, raise new arguments, nor plod slowly through all the argument in counsel’s
memorandum. So oral argument very often must be selective.

Footnote
1 Carbone v. McMahon 2015 ABCA 263, Calg 1501 0165 AC (one JA Aug 19).
2 Restrictions on the length of oral argument were in force for a number of years. First they were an experiment.
In 2008, they were made permanent and moved from a Notice to the Profession to the Consolidated Practice
Directions. Now they have been moved to this Rule.

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.51
An appeal court may well refuse to hear a point which is not in the factums.¹
Presumably it
could do the same on an application.
Where there is an appeal from an Applications Judge to a chambers judge, and it is further
appealed to the Court of Appeal, putting a new argument in the factum of the appellant is not
improper, if the topic is not entirely fresh and it is very relevant and foreseeable and requires no
new evidence.²
Applications without oral argument
14.51 A single appeal judge or a panel of the Court of Appeal may hear and
decide applications without oral argument. [Alta. Reg.41/14;72/22]

Related Provisions
14.32 (appeals without oral argument)

The May 2022 amendment to this Rule removes the need for agreement of all parties.
This Rule is especially helpful for counsel outside Edmonton and Calgary. This can save a lot
of time and money. Such waivers of oral argument, even on the appeal itself, are very common in
the United States. Many pre-appeal one-justice of appeal applications are very suitable for such
“paper” decisions; that should be done often. This is the usual mode for applications in the Federal
Court of Canada.
As of March 2023 the practice of hearing all applications before a single justice of appeal
virtually (electronically) continues. In other words, live, but via a video hookup, not together in the
same room.
Applications not heard within 3 months
14.52 Subject to rule 14.44(2) [Application for permission to appeal], if an
application is not heard within 3 months after the date the application is ﬁled,
the application is deemed to be abandoned unless a case management ofﬁcer
otherwise directs. [Alta. Reg.41/14]

The Court of Appeal will not tolerate litigation delay, so the tests for restoring an appeal struck
for non-prosecution, may be relevant?³
On an entire appeal not prosecuted, see Rules 14.56 and 14.62, and their commentary.

1 Legacy v. Red Deer (City) 2018 ABCA 393, Edm 1803 0143 AC (one JA) (¶ 46).

14-169
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.55(1)
Ordinarily all documents ﬁled in the Court of Appeal must be ﬁled electronically, not in hard
copy. (In exceptional circumstances, a case management ofﬁcer may allow ﬁling hard copies. )
The Rules of Court and the Court’s Consolidated Practice Direction (Part F) were amended
accordingly. Often a copy of the document must be served on other parties, and it is the ﬁled
version which is to be served. Concepts like multiple copies, or backwards-facing pages, are gone
now. But colored covers of documents remain. Page numbering must start with the cover as page
1. There are formatting instructions in the Court of Appeal Management Systems (v.2, p.2-10),
and in the Consolidated Practice Direction.
Someone not used to this system would be well advised to get advice or help, or arrange to
submit a draft or test version, to see if it is acceptable, all well before any ﬁling deadline. Again
the two case management ofﬁcers would be a great help in such situations. (Electronic ﬁling of
transcripts of oral evidence has existed for a number of years, and should produce no novelty. )
Despite R.14.54(c) (ii), an application for permission to appeal may consider possible grounds
for permission (topics) which were not in the initial memorandum of argument of the applicant,
nor presented to the tribunal to be appealed from, if hearing those topics on appeal would not
unreasonably prejudice the respondent nor the panel of the Court of Appeal hearing the appeal.¹
A judge praised a clear, well-written, understandable memorandum accompanying an
application, especially where the opposing party was self-represented.²
Where time, speed or delay is an issue, a chronological list of important events is helpful and
effective. It also helps solve the question of how to edit argument down to 5 or 10 pages.
Even where no page limit is given by a Rule or an order, written arguments should not be long.
Complex matters can be distilled, and not every authority needs citing.³
Filing (sometimes late) unnecessary largely irrelevant (and even unauthorized or forbidden)
very prolix documents led to denial of costs to the parties doing so.⁴
One reason why an application got no costs was failure to ﬁle a quantiﬁcation of the costs
ordered by the trial judge, as the Court asked before hearing of the application in question.⁵

14-171
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.57
The Rules deﬁne narrowly who is a “respondent” to an appeal, but a different question is who
should have been so named.¹
A party was represented and active in the application in the court
below. This body had a legal interest, and as they applied promptly, it was just and convenient
to add them. Their interests might well differ and they should not have to rely on the arguments
selected by counsel for their co-defendant.
Usually a person not a party to a lawsuit cannot appeal a decision in that lawsuit. But he or
she may if the order in question forces that non-party to do or not do something, or if it affects
the non-party's important interests.²
But such a person should apply to be added as a party, not
just appeal without being added.³
Direct economic impact on someone can be enough interest to
join him or her as a respondent, if it affects contractual rights; strict property interest is not always
necessary.⁴
It is no bar that others similarly situated are parties and they may not appeal later.⁵
An appeal is from the formal judgment, not the reasons, so correcting an error in the reasons
is not a ground for a non-party to join an appeal. He or she should be directly affected.⁶
In appeals from utilities regulators, the Responsible Energy Development Act gives standing
to persons directly and adversely affected, and obliges the regulator to hear them at the hearing.
What is a legal interest is hard to deﬁne, but is not strictly conﬁned to demonstrated propriety
or contractual interest. The court’s inherent discretion to add parties should not be conﬁned to
property or contractual rights. This Municipal District had a legal interest, and it represents the
broad interests of its constituents, so the Municipal District was added as a party.⁷
A regulator did not take any opposing part in some related appeals from itself. The court added
as a respondent a party which had been active before the regulator in its hearing, and whose legal
and economical rights were involved.⁸
Ordinarily it is very difﬁcult to have a new party added to a proposed appeal which needs
permission to appeal, before such permission has been given.⁹
But given the wording of the
Municipal Government Act, appeals from Subdivision and Development Appeal Boards may be
different.¹⁰

14-172
R.14.58(1) PART 14: APPEALS
Reviewing many portions of the legislation on police discipline, a judge concluded that on
balance, the Legislature thought that a person complaining about the conduct of an ofﬁcer had a
substantial and personal interest in the issue, and so was an “aggrieved person”.¹
Someone served with a notice of appeal who does not want someone else named in the
notice to take a full part, should apply promptly. He or she should not wait until that other person
ﬁles a factum adverse, and then move to strike the factum shortly before the appeal is heard.²
Where a respondent in the tribunal below abandoned all its rights and that respondent no
longer opposes the appeal, and has no interest in the property, it should get itself removed as
a party and not be liable to costs for any steps taken thereafter. Having a passive party who will
not give useful or meaningful argument probably would not help overcome mootness anyway.³
Another landowner was added later to a development appeal. Existing parties would not sufﬁce,
as the City (the statutory respondent) admitted that its position differed somewhat from the new
would-be respondent. The City could not be counted on to represent its interests.⁴
An appeal which had not been prosecuted diligently, and which the trustee in bankruptcy did
not want to pursue, was not restored to the general list. Only the trustee had the right to pursue
that.⁵
If an appeal succeeds, a party to the lawsuit who did not appeal and was named as a
respondent to the appeal, even if he argues in favor of the appeal, will not get costs of the appeal
or his appearance.⁶
The death of the late Queen led to a blanket Court of Appeal order changing “Her Majesty” to
“His Majesty” as a party in every appeal where she was a party.⁷
Intervenor status on appeal
14.58(1) In addition to persons having a right to intervene in law, a single appeal
judge may grant status to a person to intervene in an appeal, subject to any
terms and conditions and with the rights and privileges speciﬁed by the judge.
(2) A person granted intervenor status in the court appealed from must apply
again to obtain intervenor status on an appeal.
(3) Unless otherwise ordered, an intervenor may not raise or argue issues not
raised by the other parties to the appeal. [Alta. Reg.41/14]

14-173
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.58(3)
Related Provisions
2.10 (intervenors) ; 14.25(3) and 14.26(2) (d) (intervenor’s factum) ; 14.37(2) (e) (leave to

intervene
A. Tribunal Appealed From
The tribunal against which a judicial review order has been made cannot appeal that order,
even if the tribunal is named in the Court of King's Bench style of cause.¹
The tribunal appealed from may be heard to defend its jurisdiction, but usually not the
correctness of its decision or other issues in the appeal.²
If the tribunal takes part in the appeal,
that creates concern with the impartiality and repute of the tribunal and of the system.³
That the
legislation gives the body appealed from a right of audience, does not affect such limits.⁴
On costs
for improperly taking sides on appeal or judicial review, see Part B.2 below.
Though usually the tribunal subject to appeal should not take any other rôle respecting
the merits, it may be different where there is no other respondent.⁵
Whether an administrative
tribunal can make representations on appeal from its own decision is a discretionary matter. Such
representations are useful if no other well-informed party appears on that side. The danger of the
tribunal’s losing impartiality depends on the tribunal’s structure and on the nature of the case at
hand. It is also relevant whether the tribunal generally adjudicates disputes between opposing
parties, or whether it also makes policy or regulates or investigates. A development authority
appearing before a Subdivision and Development Appeal Board may explain its decision, as it is
customary not to give reasons for granting or denying a development permit.⁶
Adding something
to its reasons in its appellate submissions is not necessarily inappropriately shoring up its
original reasons for decision, if the “new” matter was implicit, or if the tribunal is just explaining its
established policies and practices to the appeal court; tribunal decisions need not repeat such
matters every time. But entirely new matters should not be argued.⁷
Even if it is a statutory party
to the appeal, no longer can the tribunal being appealed from argue the merits, if both parties are
represented and a sufﬁcient adversarial context already exists; the tribunal cannot even argue
standard of review, and true jurisdictional questions rarely arise.⁸

2014 ABCA 231, 374 DLR(4th) 489. The historic roots and procedural aspects of certiorari (an old writ) are
the reason that the tribunal’s name is in the style of cause.

14-174
R.14.58(3) PART 14: APPEALS
Sometimes the solution to lack of a true respondent may be to name some other interested
person as a respondent, especially in a subdivision and development appeal board case.¹
If the tribunal is the only respondent likely to appear on the appeal, that may be awkward, but
is not ground to deny permission to appeal.²
B. Interveners³
1. Allowing Intervention
(a) Attorney-General
Where the constitutionality of legislation is in question, statutory provisions for notice to the
Attorneys General are mandatory,⁴
and failure to give it invalidates a decision made in the Attorney
General’s absence, without having to show prejudice.⁵
A tax appeal should be adjourned to give
notice to the federal Ministers where their interests might be different from those of Alberta.⁶
In an aboriginal case, one provincial Attorney General and other people were allowed
to intervene, but only on issues already raised by the existing parties, especially as the new
constitutional issue which the new Attorney General wanted to add would require a new large
factual record.⁷
It is up to the Attorney General whether and when to intervene in an appeal. Normally an
afﬁdavit from the Attorney General explaining the effect of the case on the government, or its
special expertise, is very desirable, but here the effect on both governments was pretty clear, and
the other Attorney General had ﬁled such an afﬁdavit. The Attorney General is the government’s
ofﬁcial legal adviser. The history of that ofﬁcer representing the Crown is well over 500 years old.⁸
(b) General Tests for Intervention
The common eight criteria for intervention are listed, and a ninth is added.⁹
They are either
being affected or having special expertise; need for the intervener’s help; danger that the parties
will not protect some interest; prejudice or delay to the parties, e. g. by widening the dispute;
and pushing political issues on the court. The Supreme Court also now says that in hearing
applications to intervene, it will be mindful of the need not to imbalance unduly the arguments
before it.¹⁰

14-175
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.58(3)
The proposed submissions by someone seeking to intervene in an appeal should be necessary
to help the court properly decide the issues, a fresh perspective, and not merely support of an
existing party’s position. In many cases, counsel for the proposed intervener and counsel for the
party it supports, can work together to ﬂesh out the party’s arguments on appeal. An intervention
is not intended to give a party second counsel.¹
Often an association or society can intervene where the interests of its members are
signiﬁcantly and directly affected (e. g. a union or a trade or manufacturing organization).²
Intervention is more strictly tested now than some years ago, because now there is a lot of
case law on the application and effect of the Charter.³
Other relevant factors are delay, prejudice,
widening the dispute, or making the court a political forum.⁴
Intervention is discretionary and
should be sparingly exercised.⁵
(c) Public Interest Interveners
The Court of Appeal may allow other persons, such as advocacy groups, to become interveners
without being added as parties.⁶
Public interest standing needs a serious justiciable issue.⁷
Being
affected⁸
and similarly situate, plus expertise, may sufﬁce for intervention.⁹
A number of cases say
that the test is some special expertise or fresh perspective, or fresh information, or being specially
affected by the result.¹⁰
But the mere fact that an advocacy association represents many people
similarly situate to one party to an appeal, is not enough ground to let it intervene, if it has no fresh
interest or viewpoint.¹¹
An intervention may be denied if there is already going to be full argument
by the parties of the issue which the intervener would argue.¹²

14-176
R.14.58(3) PART 14: APPEALS
Interventions are not allowed to support one party, nor to give one person’s view of an issue
already before the court.¹
The “interest” which suffices cannot be an intellectual, jurisprudential, or conceptual interest.²
A would-be intervener was not involved in the events litigated, nor directly affected. A general
interest or effect by the result of the appeal which the would-be intervener or the public at large
have, is not enough to be a material interest for intervention.³
Nor did the applicant show that it
had any fresh perspective,⁴
and their submissions had been before the courts a number of times,
and had been discussed by academics and the public.⁵
Extra case law or commentary on the
case law, is not a fresh perspective.⁶
A non-proﬁt “class action clinic” helping poor litigants and acting as part of a law faculty’s
teaching, was really a law firm without a client, with no legal viewpoint, just preference for one
result, and opinions on factual matters.⁷
Furthermore, the intervener's proposed submissions could widen the dispute and expand the
litigation well beyond where the intervener's presence would be needed to help the court.⁸
A body representing most of the members of one industry is not a public interest litigant.⁹
(d) Procedure
A request to intervene should be made at the earliest possible stage, especially to avoid
adjourning the appeal.¹⁰
A request to intervene made shortly before the appeal is to be heard, is in
effect a non-party’s request to adjourn the appeal, and should not be allowed if the non-party now
applying must have known about the appeal earlier.¹¹

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.58(3)
Usually whether a plaintiff has standing is best decided conclusively (if the issue is raised
early) at the early stage, not after pleadings, discovery, evidence, experts, and a trial, all of which
would be wasted if there were no standing. Here the judge was not asked to decide standing only
on an interim basis, nor to decide merely that it was not obviously absent. It was proper to make
the decision on standing permanent.¹
It is rare that there is an application or leave to intervene on a mere application for leave
to appeal; in such circumstances it is hard to know whose interests may be affected or what
contribution can be made by the proposed intervener.²
The authorities were formerly split on whether one appellate justice could give intervener
status. Where that had been granted, and after the appeal ended there was an application by
the intervener for costs, one justice could deal with it.³
In an urgent case, one justice heard the
motion.⁴
In February 2009, the Court of Appeal resolved henceforth to let one justice of appeal
hear each intervener motion, and R.14.58 now conﬁrms that.
A motion for permission to intervene has two steps: subject of the appeal, and the proposed
intervener’s interest in it.⁵
In a big constitutional appeal there were a number of interveners, some wanting as many as

Footnote
30 pages for their factum. Each was limited to 10 pages, as their issues were narrower. They got
15 minutes for oral argument, except Attorneys-General, who got 30. Some interveners wanted to
ﬁle other evidence, but the judge directed that they bring applications for leave to do so, showing
the speciﬁc material intended, its length, and its relevance.⁶
The topics for the proposed intervention should be deﬁned with particularity.⁷
It is important that the intervener have something useful and new to add to what the existing
parties will say; any intervention often needs conditions to achieve that and prevent duplication
of argument. Any question of law permitted for the intervener’s argument should be deﬁned with
particularity.⁸
If leave to intervene is given, very often the order giving leave will impose conditions about
topics such as time, size of factums, limits on what issues may be addressed, avoiding duplication,
oral argument, and costs.⁹
(e) Examples
A large city was allowed to intervene in an appeal against a small municipality on a tax
assessment question, to access more experience and expertise.¹⁰
A city whose bylaw enforcement's
(peace ofﬁcer's) powers of arrest were in issue, was allowed to intervene on that issue only, before

R.14.58(3) PART 14: APPEALS
the Court of Appeal.¹
A proposed intervener was suitable both for public interest and for private
interest. The latter was representing an element of the democratic government of Métis people.
It was not certain that what the intervener would say, would replicate what the existing parties
would say.²
An association whose members would be heavily affected by the appeal may be a suitable
intervener.³
A borderline case for intervening succeeded because of lack of opposition and lack of prejudice,
but the two interveners were conﬁned to one joint ﬁve-page factum on one speciﬁed topic.⁴
In litigation over constitutional rights to education in French, a judge refused to allow some
other francophone school associations on the national level or from a province elsewhere to
intervene. They had no direct interest, and this would delay matters.⁵
An appeal was scheduled to be argued only a few weeks away, and the intervention application
should have been made earlier. However, the appeal was not urgent, so intervention was allowed
and the appeal adjourned for two months. The applicant's draft factum and the actual factum were
to be substantially the same. The respondent could ﬁle a concise reply. No new issues or evidence
were to be raised by the applicant. An afﬁdavit already ﬁled for the intervention motion was not
part of the Appeal Record.⁶
In constitutional litigation where no one is personally affected by the issue, sometimes
reasonable hypothetical future situations are considered, though they raise many difﬁculties.⁷
2. Procedure After Leave to Intervene
The Supreme Court of Canada expects all intervener submissions to be useful to the Court
and different from those of the parties. The purpose of an intervention is not to support a party but
to advance the intervener’s own view of a legal issue before the Court. Despite the participation of
interveners, the case remains a dispute between its parties. However, the fact that an intervener’s
submission aligns it generally with one party over another does not, without more, make the
submission inappropriate. Interveners should not take a position on the outcome of an appeal,
whether in written or oral argument. Interveners must not challenge ﬁndings of fact, introduce
new issues,⁸
or try to expand the case. In considering applications to intervene, the Court will be
mindful of the need not to unduly imbalance the arguments before it. The Court always retains
a discretion to take any steps it sees ﬁt to prevent an unfairness to the parties arising from an
intervener’s participation in an appeal.⁹

14-179
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.58(3)
An intervener in the Court of Appeal cannot get leave to appeal to the Supreme Court of
Canada where neither the Attorney General nor the individuals want to appeal.¹
A guardian ad
litem has no standing to seek leave to appeal to the Supreme Court of Canada from a judgment
regarding medical treatment of the severely retarded infant?²
Where the parties do not want
to appeal something, the Supreme Court of Canada has never allowed an intervener with no
personal interest to do so. Giving such a person respondent status in an existing appeal is quite
different.³
People are to ﬁle as appeal record (Extracts) only those parts of the trial record needed on
appeal. That is also true of those wishing to be made interveners. They should not ﬁle bulky
materials of elusive admissibility, relevance and materiality, or about issues not likely to be
contested. A request to intervene will usually produce fresh evidence, but that is not part of the
appeal record for the appeal itself, and not admissible there.⁴
Evidence which the plaintiff wanted to tender, but was not adduced in King's Bench, may be
removed from an intervener’s book of authorities. Nor can one put before the panel deciding an
appeal, an afﬁdavit ﬁled for an interlocutory Court of Appeal motion.⁵
An intervener’s argument is limited to topics on which the intervention was allowed.⁶
Argument
on other topics was struck out from the factum of an intervener, with leave to the appellants to
insert it in their factum.⁷
An intervener cannot usually introduce a new argument on appeal,⁸
especially if there is no basis for it in the evidence in the Appeal Book.⁹
But a judge gave a new
intervener leave to ﬁle an application for leave to adduce fresh evidence, which application would
(as usual) be decided by the panel hearing the appeal.¹⁰
One does not get intervener status merely to repeat the arguments of one side, even with
different emphasis, which merely creates an appearance of imbalance. A useful contribution is
needed.¹¹
On a criminal appeal, an intervener must not assume the role of a third-party prosecutor.¹²

R.14.59(1) PART 14: APPEALS
Several interveners were limited to one joint factum, and only the panel hearing the appeal
could allow them oral argument.¹
In the Supreme Court of Canada, usually interveners argue orally after the party whose position
they support; in other words, argument proceeds side-by-side, so no party gets sandwiched.²
The case law on costs against interveners is reviewed.³
Generally interveners bear their own
costs, but where one intervened in the public interest and really in substance was a corporation
representing virtually all of the affected people, it got its costs.⁴
The court may impose terms on
an intervener, and it may have to pay costs, especially if it signiﬁcantly lengthens the appeal.⁵
And
doubly so if it is not really a public interest body, and what it proposed to say (when unsuccessfully
applying to intervene) came very close to taking a position on the merits of the appeal and aligning
with one party.⁶
A party to the suit who did not appeal, but gave written and oral argument fully
supporting the appellant (which the Court of Appeal allowed) is a de facto intervener who does
not receive costs if the appeal succeeds.⁷

Related Provisions
4.24 (offers to settle) ; 14.88 (costs)

Formal offers are important, and would become meaningless if courts declined to apply
the costs consequences intended to ﬂow from successful offers.²
The general principles are
discussed, and the requirements of how to satisfy the Rules on offers to settle are reduced to one
only, in the H2S case.³
Costs of an appeal are not affected by an offer to settle the King's Bench lawsuit; a fresh
written offer to settle the appeal is needed.⁴
Where an offer made at trial is not quite exceeded by
the offeror, and then on appeal the interest is changed so that the ﬁnal judgment now does exceed
the offer, the offeror cannot get double appellate costs if he did not renew his offer pending appeal.
But the costs in the trial court should be doubled.⁵
The Rules on formal offers and extra costs from them apply to offers to settle pending
interlocutory appeals, and are not restricted to offers to settle the whole lawsuit.⁶
The King's Bench Rules on formal offers speak of a “plaintiff” and a “defendant”. But that is
presumed to refer here to the appellant and the respondent, unless that is unsuitable under other
Rules: see R.14.2(4) (b), (c).
These are not special circumstances allowing the court to waive a Rule about formal offers:
allegations of lack of clarity (when the offer is clear enough) ; that the litigation in the Court of
King's Bench was partly for the executor’s personal interests as a beneﬁciary; or that the offer was
conditional on settling some ancillary issues (about passing accounts).⁷

Bad enough misconduct on appeal could be special circumstances disentitling special costs;

The Court of Appeal may award double costs to a successful party who has made an offer

Courts are often skeptical about an offer to accept an
abandonment or discontinuance without costs, but there is no one-size-ﬁts-all, and it depends

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.60
Subdivision 4
Judicial Dispute Resolution on Appeal
Judicial dispute resolution of an appeal
14.60 An arrangement for a judicial dispute resolution process on appeal may
be made in accordance with Part 4 [Managing Litigation], Division 3 [Dispute
Resolution by Agreement], Subdivision 2. [Alta. Reg.41/14]
Information Note
The judicial dispute resolution process in the Court of Appeal is set out in the Consolidated
Practice Direction.
Related Provisions
4.18 (dispute resolution)

The Court of Appeal offers such free mediation services. See a notice entitled “Dispute
Resolution Services at the Court of Appeal” on the Court of Appeal website, which in turn cites
other Notices, a list of Justice of Appeal participating, the relevant Part of the Consolidated
Practice Directions and of the Rules, a Quick Reference Guide, and other helpful papers.
If the appeal is interlocutory, the Justice running the dispute resolution would probably try to
settle the whole lawsuit. The Court of Appeal encourages these “JDR’s” and they often succeed
in settling everything. For some unknown reason, Edmonton counsel make less use of this free
facility than do Calgary counsel.
Suspension of time periods
14.61(1) Once a date has been scheduled for judicial dispute resolution, time
limits in respect of the appeal are suspended until an order or direction is made
under subrule (2).
(2) If judicial dispute resolution is not successful,
(a) the appeal judge conducting the judicial dispute resolution will set
time limits for ﬁling and serving any remaining materials on the
appeal, or
(b) if no order is made under clause (a) a case management ofﬁcer may
provide a direction setting the time limits for ﬁling and serving any
remaining materials on the appeal. [Alta. Reg.41/14]

Related Provisions
14.2(3) (extending time) ; 14.64 (missing deadlines)


14-184
R.14.62 PART 14: APPEALS
Subdivision 5
Delay in Advancing Appeals
Dismissal for delay
14.62 A panel of the Court of Appeal may dismiss an appeal if it is satisﬁed that
delay in advancing the appeal has resulted in signiﬁcant prejudice to a party.
[Alta. Reg.41/14]

Related Provisions
4.31 (delay) ; 14.38 (applications to appeal panel)

The Court of Appeal will not tolerate litigation delay, which erodes conﬁdence in the justice
system.¹
And wastes the public’s and both parties’ resources.
The Rules and all court procedures aim to deal with all appeals as diligently as possible. Rules
giving penalties for delay, such as striking out the appeal, do not set the times for taking steps,
which are shorter. For example, preparing an appeal digest must begin within 10 days, and the
digest must be prepared promptly. That must occur in much less than the time for striking out (15
weeks). Permission to appeal is always granted on the implicit condition that the appellant will
prepare as expeditiously as circumstances permit.²
It is not enough excuse for delay that the litigant or counsel is busy with other matters. Someone
who appeals must arrange his affairs so that he can meet all the applicable deadlines. The court
system cannot wait to accommodate his personal schedule, especially where there is a stay of
execution, which gives a duty to prosecute the appeal.³
It used to take a panel of three judges to dismiss an appeal for want of prosecution (if nothing
had been done for a long time). But now R.14.65(3) deems an appeal abandoned if it is struck off
the list and there is no motion to restore it within six months.
And a delay in prosecution may be grounds for one justice of appeal to lift a stay of execution
pending appeal (or bail).
It was proper for one judge to dismiss an appeal for want of prosecution where nothing had
really happened for over 2 years since the appeal record was ﬁled.⁴
Panels of three judges sit every month (except July and August) to hear a variety of motions,
including motions to dismiss. Any respondent can move at any time to dismiss for want of
prosecution, and sometimes the Deputy Registrar brings a motion to the same effect.⁵

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.63
A panel dismissed an appeal because a judge had set a timetable, none of the times in it were
met, the appellant was still not ready to go ahead, and had not explained these ﬂaws.¹
Appeals have been dismissed for want of prosecution for repeated failures to obtain and ﬁle
a transcript.²
If the public has an interest (as in a criminal matter), speed is valuable to the public, and
adjournments will not easily be granted.³
This Rule is used less often in Alberta than the Rules about striking an appeal from the list
(Rr.14.37, 14.64, 14.90, and 14.92). Saskatchewan has a Rule giving a deadline; breach of which
requires the appellant to show that it is in the interests of justice to let the appeal proceed. If it is
manifestly without merit, that itself kills the appeal. If it has arguable merit, then reasons for the
delay, changes of moving it along, and prejudice to the respondent become material.⁴
Powers of a single appeal judge

may
(a) make a procedural order or otherwise give directions to expedite the
appeal, or
(b) dismiss the appeal, if it has not been signiﬁcantly advanced for over

[Alta. Reg.41/14]

8 months on an appeal seeking again to postpone the impending trial. The appellant changed lawyers twice
and was slow with ﬁnancial arrangements. The appeal was dismissed summarily: Pier v. Pier (CA) [1996]
AUD 2523. An appeal was struck out after two-and-a-half years during which nothing had even been done to

Restoring appeals
14.65(1) An appeal or application for permission to appeal that has been struck
by operation of these rules or the provisions of any order, or by failure of any
party to appear when required, may be restored
(a) with the ﬁled written consent of the parties or by order of a single
appeal judge on application under rule 14.47 [Application to restore
an appeal], and
(b) on the payment of the fee for an application to restore an appeal in
Schedule B.
(2) An order or written consent restoring an appeal must set deadlines and
directions for the ﬁling of any outstanding materials, and if the appellant fails to
comply with any of those deadlines or directions, the appeal is deemed to have
been struck again.

A. Who Can Restore an Appeal
Now this Rule lets one justice of appeal restore to the list, with or without conditions.¹
(Or a
case struck off can be restored to the list by consent. ) A motions panel of three justices is no
longer necessary.
B. Tests to Restore an Appeal
1. Policy
Rule compliance is important, especially under the current Alberta Rules.²
Tardiness is serious,
and there is an analogy to time limits to appeal, where extending them is difﬁcult.³
2. Discretion and Tests
The tests for restoring an appeal largely overlap with the tests to extend time to appeal, in
Cairns v. Cairns.⁴
The basic test is the interests of justice, which has a number of aspects, which are set out in
the "tests" below.⁵
The tests for restoration are reviewed.⁶
The various criteria, to be shown by

378; Frydman v. Pelletier, supra; I. B. U. v. L. A. H. , supra; Mihaly v. Assn. of Prof. Eng. etc.2017 ABCA
15, Edm 1603 0056 AC (one JA Jan 12) ; Gray v. McNeill, supra; Martinez v. Chafﬁn, supra (transcripts
never properly ordered); Daniels v. Prime 2019 ABCA 206, Edm 1803 0190 AC (one JA) ; Rana v. Rana,
