2020 ABCA 278, Edm 2003 0028 AC (one JA Jul 22) (delay very long and no transcripts). A bare assertion of
inadvertence is not a sufﬁcient reason for allowing an appeal to be struck off for failure to take the necessary
steps: Can-Cell Ind. v. Lippa 2010 ABCA 409, 493 AR 389. Incompetence of counsel is rarely an excuse in
a civil case: Morbank Finl. v. Field, supra (¶ 21) ; Waterhouse v. Chutskoff Est. , supra; but cf. Composite
Technologies v. Shawcor 2016 ABCA 350, Edm 1603 0142 AC (one JA Nov 8). But a clerical error in a
law ﬁrm can be an excuse: 932282 Alta. v. Royal Bank, supra (¶’s 24-25). Nor is waiting for a formal order:
Kure v. Kure 2017 ABCA 215, Calg 1701 0074 AC (one JA Jun 29). It is weighty that the reasons given
make no sense: Walsh v. Chotalia 2015 ABCA 244, Edm 1403 0171 AC (one JA Jul 17) (¶’s 14-16). It was
unreasonable for the appellants to rely on another lay person estranged from the appellants to run the suit,
and then take two years’ silence to be normal or reassuring and not inquire: Garry v. R. (#2) 2007 ABCA 327
(one JA). Part J appeals are urgent, especially those over child guardianship: A. C. (M. C. ) v. Child, Youth &
Fam Enhancement, supra; cf. Beck v. Ackmann, supra. Disappointment with one’s lawyer or being upset
at what is going on is not sufﬁcient excuse: Gordon v. Holmen 2015 ABCA 42, 593 AR 378. Desire to wait
for a decision in other litigation is not a valid excuse, if no connection or beneﬁt is demonstrated, or if beneﬁt
from the connection could be given later: Piikani N. v. Kostic (#__) 2017 ABCA 350, [2017] AJ #1307 (one
JA) (¶’s 22-26). Doubt as to the strength of one’s case do not sufﬁce as an excuse: Holden v. Holden 2022
ABCA 341, Edm 2203 0099 AC (one JA Oct 17) (¶’s 55-68).

v. Rolling Mix etc.2003 ABCA 286 (one JA). Or the appellant had given up on the appeal and deliberately
did not speak to the list: Petersen v. Paplinski 2004 ABCA 58 (one JA). Or he was sophisticated, yet

Not all the criteria have to be satisﬁed before an appeal will be restored?⁵
They are not rigid
preconditions, and all are weighed.⁶
Restoring an appeal struck off the list is discretionary.⁷
A
mere slip is no bar, but prejudice or problems with the appeal may be.⁸
The test for weighing the likely merits of the appeal is fairly lax.⁹

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.65(3)
more than very vague grounds of appeal with no speciﬁc error alleged, and a mere conclusion in
argument.¹
After six months have passed, the tests are explanation for both delays, continuing intent to
appeal, no prejudice, and arguable merit.²
Continuing intention to appeal may be necessary.³
Continuing intent to appeal and promptness
in curing after learning that the appeal had been struck off, interact, so lack of one suggests lack
of the other.⁴
Intent to proceed with the appeal (if it is restored) is also a criterion.⁵
The judge
should see if the appellant shows real prospects of moving the appeal forward.⁶
It is signiﬁcant
that on the hearing of the application to restore, the appeal is still not ready or perfected, and what
was missing before is still not tendered.⁷
Whether there is prejudice is an element to consider,⁸
and at some stage prejudice can be
presumed.⁹
The factors are the same ones, whether the appeal had been struck off or had been deemed
to be abandoned.¹⁰

R.14.65(3) PART 14: APPEALS
3. Onus and Evidence
Sworn evidence of the applicant’s assertions about those criteria are needed.¹
But where well
over a year’s delay has occurred, prejudice may be presumed.²
The applicant needs to give more
evidence about the interests of justice than a bald conclusion.³
Who has the onus of showing lack of merit seems unclear.⁴
The Rules’ timelines are not to be ignored or lightly dispensed with, but they can all be extended
under Rr.13.5(2) and 14.2(3).⁵
If the excuse for delay in prosecuting an appeal is a medical condition, usually expert evidence
is needed, including the effects of the condition. For example, a broken arm alone should not keep
a lawyer from his or her ofﬁce. But some other serious medical conditions obviously prevent doing
legal work.⁶
On prejudice being presumed at some stage, see Part B.2 above.
4. Examples
The tests tend to be applied laxly or broadly when the tardiness was for a very short time
and the attempts to restore were prompt.⁷
Especially if the initial slip or hold-up was a very small
forgiveable error, or being caught in a web of requirements hard to understand or meet, or was
the fault of the system or court ofﬁcials, not of this litigant.⁸
Where a mere slip of counsel has caused an appeal to be struck, and the error is immediately
discovered, and being cured, usually the appeal will be restored, unless it appears to lack any
merit, or no intention is shown by the appellant to proceed, or there has been prejudice, or the
delay has been lengthy and no reasonable explanation is offered.⁹
The date set by the Rules for striking off is a legislative declaration of undue delay.¹⁰
It is more
difﬁcult to restore an appeal after it is deemed abandoned under R.14.65(3), six months after it
was ﬁrst struck.¹¹
It is harder to restore an appeal if its subject is urgent, e. g. child custody or access.¹²
A medical condition may be a good excuse for a time, but not a very long time.¹³

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.65(3)
It is harder to restore an appeal if it needs permission to appeal (being for a small sum), and
does not have permission.¹
If there was no right to appeal in the ﬁrst place, the motion to restore
is moot.²
Where security for costs of a pending appeal is not put up within the time ordered, and the
order says that the appeal is then automatically struck, the normal consequence is that the appeal
is deemed abandoned under R.14.67(2). But if the Registrar instead strikes the appeal, that is a
very slight difference.³
If the appellant asks to have the appeal restored, the test for restoring an
appeal deemed abandoned is substantially the same as that for one struck, though applied more
strictly. An appeal struck for failure to post security should not be restored unless the security has
been put up or the appellant has attempted to put it up.⁴
To restore it would require that all harm
be otherwise repaired. It is rarely in the interests of justice to restore an appeal struck for failure
to post security.⁵
There is no special exception for self-represented litigants.⁶
Not knowing the relevant deadlines
or Rules is no excuse.⁷
C. Prejudice
Many cases refuse to restore an appeal because the delay would prejudice the respondent.⁸
Conversely a small accidental delay promptly cured, with no prejudice to the other side, often is
forgiven.⁹

R.14.65(3) PART 14: APPEALS
Prejudice through fading memories and lost documents or witnesses, does not stop accruing
just because the parties agreed to let the suit sit idle.¹
Even almost a year’s delay in perfecting an
appeal lets the judge infer prejudice.²
Leaving a professional regulator’s practices under a cloud for a long time is prejudice.³
Appeals from parenting orders have great impact on the lives of children, and so delay can
cause prejudice.⁴
The same is true of appeals from family law or fast-track appeals, especially
child and spousal support orders which are not being obeyed.⁵
There had been a 14-month delay before the restoration motion was ﬁled, and no evidence
of continuing intent to prosecute the appeal in the meantime. With this much delay, prejudice is
assumed.⁶
Usually failure to restore an appeal would cause the appellant irreparable harm, but if it would
not, that is a ground not to restore it.⁷
Restoring an appeal dismissed for want of prosecution is possible, but only subject to a very
stiff test requiring very special circumstances which caused the initial default.⁸
D. Terms
If an appeal is struck off the list for any reason, one justice can restore it, but usually only
on terms, especially if the appeal has little evident merit.⁹
Such terms may include taking steps,
such as paying solicitor-client costs and giving security for costs, or giving security for prejudice
in the form of an undertaking as to damages.¹⁰
But the appeal is then temporarily in limbo, and is

not dismissed. Indeed there should be deadlines and ﬁling directions, providing a deemed striking
afresh if those time limits are not met.¹
Restoration requires all harm done be otherwise repaired.²
Where a condition of an order to restore is that money be paid into court, and it is not done in

E. Miscellaneous
One factor weighing against restoring a procedural appeal is that before the appeal could
(if restored) be heard and decided, an already-scheduled trial will be held which will render the
procedural issue moot or unnecessary.⁷
But where an appeal is stalled because the Registrar has not done something necessary, the
appellant is not to suffer, despite a Rule that a year’s inaction means a deemed dismissal, and
despite the appellant’s failure to move earlier.⁸
An appeal was deemed dismissed because of failure to ﬁle factums, and even though there
was inaction by counsel, vague general Rules were used to restore the appeal, as the appeal was
meritorious.⁹
Though the court has no power to restore after the six months expires, semble the court can
extend the six months under the general Rules and so restore the appeal, if merit and an excuse
for the delay are shown.¹⁰
The 6-month limit to restore can be extended, but only for good reason,
and a mere mention of illness, with no supporting evidence is not enough.¹¹
Especially if there was
a previous history of delay.¹²

A more stringent test applies to a second application to restore an appeal, when the ﬁrst order

If an appeal is struck because of inaction, the lawyer who was running it should often retain

Subdivision 6
Discontinuing an Appeal
Discontinuance
14.66(1) The appellant may discontinue the appeal by ﬁling and serving a notice
to discontinue in Form AP-4, and the respondent is entitled to a costs award for
having responded to the appeal.
(2) The discontinuance of an appeal does not operate as a discontinuance of
a cross appeal. [Alta. Reg.41/14]

Related Provisions
4.36 (discontinuance) ; 14.1(2) (cross appeals) ; 14.11 (starting a cross appeal) ; 14.76

(consent orders)
If a suit is discontinued, that ends a pending appeal in it, even if it is a constitutional issue and
there are interveners.³
Where R.860C called for signature by the solicitor of the party on a notice of abandonment,
a signature by another solicitor helping the solicitor of record and instructed to sign “per” after the
name of the solicitor of record, sufﬁced.⁴
To upset an abandonment of appeal on the grounds that
it was not authorized, requires some sworn evidence.⁵
Where the results of other appeals made a third appeal moot, but it had been arguable, no
one got any costs of it.⁶
Subdivision 7
Security for Costs
Security for costs
14.67(1) A single appeal judge may order a party to provide security for payment
of a costs award pursuant to Part 4 [Managing Litigation], Division 4 [Security for
Payment of Costs Award].

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.67(2)
(2) Where a party does not provide security as ordered, the appeal is deemed to
have been abandoned and the other party is entitled to a costs award.¹
[Alta. Reg.41/14]

Related Provisions
4.22 and 4.23 (security for costs)

A. Introduction²
This Rule is about free-standing security for costs of an appeal. Apart from this Rule, posting
security for costs is often made a condition of many other kinds of interlocutory relief, such as
restoring to the list a case which had been struck off.³
B. Grounds
1. General
(a) New Rule and Policy
The old stiff test for security (in former R.524) is gone, and the more lax test in R.4.22 now
applies.⁴
So most pre-2014 case law on the tests for security in the Court of Appeal no longer is
reliable. Such older cases refusing security are very often now inapplicable.
A litigant cannot use the court process to his advantage while effectively disavowing in
advance any judgment against him. Ordering security for the debt is to show that the court is not
impotent “in the face of those who choose to order their affairs so as to keep all their options for
themselves. ”⁵
The tests for security for costs of an appeal are likelihood of enforcing a costs order against
Alberta assets, ability of the respondent to pay costs, merits of the suit, any undue prejudice to
the respondent, and other relevant matters. Usually failure to pay past costs plus shown inability
to pay costs if the appeal succeeds, is enough.⁶
Use of the new Rule is discretionary, and balances the reasonable expectations and the rights
of the parties.⁷

Footnote
1 Rather similar to 1968 R.524(2), 1914 R.360, and 1944 C. R.616.
2 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 34, Part A; Brown, Civil Appeals §§ 8: 5100 to 8: 5422 (Carswell) ; Trisura Guar. Ins.
v. Duchnij 2021 ABCA 78, 26 Alta LR(7th) 1 (one JA). The afﬁdavit called for by old R.594, modiﬁed for an
appeal, was necessary in the Court of Appeal: Sorochan v. St. Albert Protestant Sep. S. D.2002 ABCA
181, 317 AR 337 (one JA). No obvious parallel is found in the new Rules.
3 See Liu v. Matrikon 2007 ABCA 269, and Johnston v. Hader 2010 ABCA 47, 22 Alta LR(5th) 353 (one JA).
And see R.14.65 n. D.
4 Access Mtge. Corp. (2004) v. Arres Cap.2017 ABCA 373, Calg 1701 0241 AC (one JA Nov 14) (citing this
book and reviewing recent Court of Appeal authority).

R.14.67(2) PART 14: APPEALS
One cannot defeat a motion for security merely by invoking “access to justice”.¹
That does not
include a right to sue with no fear of costs.²
The equities, what is just and reasonable, are relevant.³
On a security motion, there may be a tentative assessment of the merits of the appeal?⁴
And lack of any obvious error in the judgment appealed is prima facie evidence that there is no
reasonable prospect of success.⁵
But the court cannot look at inadmissible evidence, such as
offers to settle.⁶
(b) Examples
In the past, the courts were not completely consistent in their tests or approach to appellate
security. However, the appellant’s lack of assets⁷
and modest prospects of the appeal’s succeeding⁸
are together enough to justify security for costs.⁹
Liquid assets and absence of any attempt to
raise funds, militate in favor of security, not against ordering security.¹⁰
Very modest assets, no
real income, and failure to pay a judgment, with no other information, and no evidence of undue
prejudice to the appellant, sufﬁce to order security.¹¹
Someone in future entitled to receive large
sums, but for years having refused to pay large costs, should be ordered to give security now.¹²
So may sufﬁce the fact that all the appellants assets are mortgaged or hard to sell and there
are already unpaid costs.¹³
Conversely the existence of signiﬁcant immobile assets possessed here

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.67(2)
by the respondent (matrimonial property) shows that any ultimate costs award to the respondent
would be easy to collect, militating against security.¹
Doubtless many other circumstances could
also sufﬁce.²
Not surprisingly, non-residence usually sufﬁces for security as well.³
Security for
costs was ordered because of delay,⁴
non-residence, and insolvency of the appellant company.⁵
Where the party is resisting security for costs, has failed to pay costs, and failed to give any new
meaningful evidence of ﬁnancial status or that of his companies, that is very relevant. It depends
on all the facts and the onus of showing it.⁶
Possessing a contingent asset such as a claim in another suit, occasionally could be enough
to bar security for costs, but usually its worth is not certain enough. Case law says that assets
have to be real, substantial and exigible, i. e. available here for seizure and sale by a bailiff.⁷
(c) Lack of Funds
The appellant’s inability to post security is often a two-edged sword, as it usually equally
indicates inability to pay costs. However, where the appellant has valuable illiquid assets tied
up by the lawsuit, then inability to post security before the end of the suit is a factor in denying
security.⁸
Even if poverty is relevant to security in trial courts, it may not be, in appeal courts.⁹
If the appellant alleges that a security order would bar his or her appeal, evidence of that is
needed.¹⁰
For example, lack of cash is not enough; the appellant must prove there is no way to raise
any kind of security.¹¹
If the appellant's ﬁnancial status is very unclear, the appellant must at least
prove that the appeal has some reasonable prospect of success. If he or she does not, security
may be ordered, as “access to justice ” does not mean litigating without costs consequences.¹²

14-200
R.14.67(2) PART 14: APPEALS
(d) Security by Defendant
Even in the Court of Appeal, it is harder to get security for costs from a defendant, even if he
is the appellant; he did not start the lawsuit.¹
2. Onus of Proof
See also subpart 1(c).
Once such circumstances as lack of exigible assets, poor merits, non-residence, or non-
payment of costs are shown, the onus shifts to the appellant to show otherwise, e. g. that he
would have some reasonable prospect of success.²
It is a powerful circumstance, that execution
(enforcement) against the appellant has already failed.³
Or that the appellant company is being
kept alive only to run the appeal.⁴
Absent evidence that the principal of the company could not continue to run the appeal (as he
has been doing), inability to continue does not bar security.⁵
3. Dishonesty
Grounds like those for a Mareva injunction may justify a security order: hiding assets, untrue
information on examination in aid of enforcement, evidence of dissipation, expensive lifestyle, and
big cash receipts.⁶
If it is bad enough, there may have to be large security for the amount of the
unpaid judgment under appeal.⁷
C. Amount and Type of Security
If solicitor-client costs later are possible, is that a ground for generous security?⁸
Unusual security for costs of an appeal was ordered, consisting only of a charge on matrimonial
property of the appellant, should the appellant later lose and have to pay costs (with no cash or
other security deposited and no stay of execution). That was ordered because given the standard
of review, the appeal would be uphill, and the appellant made it clear that she had very few assets.⁹
If the respondent delays in applying for security, that may be a ground to give reduced or no
security,¹⁰
or to limit security to costs of future steps.¹¹
But that is not a ground to deny security
entirely unless there is serious prejudice to the appellant.¹²
Signiﬁcant delay by the respondent

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.67(2)
in moving for security, until shortly before the hearing, the appellant having incurred most of its
expenses in the meantime, may bar any order for security.¹
Where a letter of credit was put up as security pending appeal to release seizure of goods, the
winner may recover as costs the expense of the letter of credit.²
Security in the Court of Appeal is ordinarily for future costs only.³
Absent other relevant facts,
usually the security ordered is only for costs of the appeal, not of the past trial.⁴
But if the costs below were increased for the appellant’s litigation misconduct, and he seems
to be continuing in the same manner, the Court of Appeal security may be big enough to allow for
such possible increased costs.⁵
D. Miscellaneous
Security applications should be heard promptly and not await decision of other interlocutory
applications in the suit.⁶
A repetitive litigant was subjected both to a vexatious litigant order and an order to give security
for costs of his appeal.⁷
If an appeal needs permission to appeal, such permission should be denied, if the respondent
makes out a good case for security for costs, and the would-be appellant admits that he cannot
post security.⁸
Where a hopeless appeal was struck off the list, leave was given to move later before a panel
to restore it, but if so, security for costs would be an issue.⁹
If the security is not posted on time, the appeal automatically ends (unless the order says
otherwise), but the court can later bless tardy security and restore the appeal, if all harm is
repaired.¹⁰
After months to post security, in the last 48 hours the appellant with weak merits was
denied, double the time originally given. He had no funds, nor prospect of raising them.¹¹

14-202
R.14.68 PART 14: APPEALS
Where the security is not put up within the time speciﬁed, the normal consequence is that the
appeal is deemed abandoned under R.14.67(2). If the Registrar strikes the appeal rather than
deeming it abandoned, that is a very slight difference. If the appellant then asks to have the appeal
restored, the test for restoring is substantially the same as that for one struck, though applied
more strictly.¹
If a party faced with a motion for security for costs alleges that such security is unconstitutional,
the single justice of appeal hearing the application can decide that issue enough to rule on the
motion.²
Such a justice of appeal held the Rules providing for security for costs were common,
necessary, and constitutional, especially if the appellant is vexatious.³
The Supreme Court of Canada does not require security for costs of an appeal, even on special
applications for security (save for the nominal statutory $500 which the Court discourages).⁴
Such
special security is not impossible, but it has never been ordered. Security as a condition of a stay
of execution or a stay of proceedings, whether by a Justice of Appeal below or a Supreme Court
of Canada Justice, is a different question.⁵

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.70

Subdivision 2
Basis on Which Appeals Are Decided
No new evidence without order
14.70 Unless an order is granted under rule 14.45 [Application to admit new

Related Provisions
14.16 (appeal record) ; 14.28 (record before the Court) ; 14.38 and 14.45 (application for fresh

evidence); 14.75(a) (receiving fresh evidence)
See also R.14.45 n.
Under R.14.70, the respondent obtained an order striking out parts of the appellant’s factum
and extracts of key evidence, which reproduced material not in evidence before the trial judge.
That they were referred to in the trial is not enough to mean they were in evidence. That the
material might be relevant and have probative value, is not a reason to ignore R.14.70.¹
In terms
of mechanics, the court simply ordered that a new factum and new extracts be ﬁled, identical
except for the deletion of the contested materials and references to them.
Appeals are conducted based on the record in the trial court. Discovery of documents and
pre-trial questioning are trial procedures, not appellate procedures.²
This is not the proper forum
to seek discovery of documents, even if an application to admit fresh evidence is contemplated.³
On the rather different situation where the Court of Appeal has pending a Reference from the
government, and not an appeal, see the Greenhouse Gas case.⁴
Interlocutory decisions
14.71 An interlocutory order of the court appealed from does not restrict the
ability of the Court of Appeal to decide an appeal, despite there having been no
appeal from the interlocutory order.⁵

14-204
R.14.71 PART 14: APPEALS
Related Provisions
14.5 (permission to appeal interlocutory orders)

There are many reasons to discourage appeals to the Court of Appeal on interlocutory
matters.¹
One is that they are often pointless, given R.14.71. If an interlocutory decision has any
bearing on the ﬁnal result, this Rule lets the appeal court disregard the interlocutory order when
later deciding the substantive questions in the lawsuit.²
And a notice of appeal need not expressly
appeal from the costs awarded at trial, if it appeals the merits of the judgment.³
Therefore, usually an interlocutory decision before any later substantive decision works no
irreparable harm, and so no stay of execution pending appeal of the earlier interlocutory decision
should be given.⁴
Where an order in the Court of King’s Bench set the procedure for a later application to strike
the plaintiff’s claim, it was interlocutory and did not have to be appealed; only the later decision
about striking out had to be appealed, though appealing both orders out of an abundance of
catuion was not improper (and delay between the two orders meant two separate appeals) to
meet deadlines for appealing.⁵
However, since case management ofﬁcers directed that there be
two appeals, the successful appellant got as costs both ﬁling fees and costs of both factums and
only single fees for other work on the appeals.⁶
In several other contexts, one encounters the question of which orders are interlocutory, and
which ﬁnal.⁷
It is doubtful that interlocutory decisions are immutable, and probably the lower court
can change most without an appeal.⁸
Decisions on which matters are ﬁnal or interlocutory for
afﬁdavit purposes are discussed in R.13.18 n. B.
It is highly doubtful that an order quashing an award and remitting the matter to rehear is a
ﬁnal judgment, for appeal purposes.⁹
Presumably it is therefore interlocutory.¹⁰
A court order can
be ﬁnal (for conveyancing purposes) even before appeal times expire, especially when it was

13 NR 160.

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.72
unopposed, so the chance of an appeal is slight.¹
For appeal purposes, an indeﬁnite stay of
proceedings is ﬁnal.²
Where a Court of Appeal recommendation is required by statute to remove
a judge from ofﬁce, a recommendation is an appealable ﬁnal judgment.³
But a mere decision on
admissibility of evidence on a reference is not a ﬁnal judgment.⁴
For appeal purposes, neither an
order to give discovery, nor an order dismissing someone from the suit for failure to give discovery,
is a “ﬁnal decree”. One cannot change a procedural order into a substantive one by disobeying it.⁵
In a criminal prosecution, for purposes of appeal by a non-party, an order that he produce certain
documents which he considers relevant is ﬁnal.⁶
Part J of the Court of Appeal’s Consolidated Practice Directions carefully avoided the word
“interlocutory”, and so a very old Court of Appeal decision interpreting that word was not useful
in the Part J context.⁷
Binding precedents
14.72 Unless permission has been granted under rule 14.46 [Application to
reconsider a previous decision] by a panel of the Court of Appeal, no party may
argue that a prior precedential decision of the Court should be reconsidered.
[Alta. Reg.41/14]
Information Note
This Rule does not prevent a party from arguing that a prior precedential decision has been
overruled by a decision of the Supreme Court of Canada, or by a legislative change. It also
does not prevent argument about the ratio decidendi of the prior decision, nor whether it can
be distinguished on recognized grounds.

1.4 (procedural orders) ; 1.5(4) (curing irregularities) ; 4.11 and 4.14 (procedural powers) ;

In urgent cases, a judge may order an appeal processed quickly and heard early, even in a
special sittings. But for some years now the fast-track procedure (mostly for procedural matters or
those involving children) has much reduced that need. Adjournments are hard to get where the
public has an interest in the appeal.²
Many of the Alberta Court of Appeal’s judgments are reserved after oral argument, and
delivered solely in writing without even the briefest further oral sitting (unlike the pre-1988 practice
of the Supreme Court of Canada). Briefer Alberta Court of Appeal judgments are often given
orally, but even then one judge often reads out a (possibly handwritten) memorandum of reasons,
which thereafter often will be keyboarded, printed, and placed on the court ﬁle and websites. Oral
reasons for judgment are sometimes recorded and transcribed, and in recent years are almost
always placed on the court ﬁle and websites.³
Court shorthand stenographers do not usually
attend the Alberta Court of Appeal.
The Court of Appeal practice about advance notice of the time and date of giving reserved
written decisions is found in the Court of Appeal’s Consolidated Practice Directions (in vol.2 of
this book).
Big parts of the 1992 policy of the Alberta Court of Appeal on circulation and consideration
of reserved judgments, were repealed at a Court meeting on June 8, 1999. The Supreme Court
of Canada’s practice in reaching decisions and drafting reserved judgments is described in the
Wewayakum case.⁴
There is a discussion of the scope of discussion of policy and decisions involving members of
a tribunal who did not hear argument.⁵

14-207
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.74
Reasons given by a panel for their decision should rarely be clariﬁed or explained, still less
from tentative comments made during oral argument.¹
A King's Bench justice ordered that a copy of his reasons for decision commenting on a
lawyer’s behavior be sent to the Law Society. A judge, like anyone else, is entitled to refer conduct
of a lawyer to the Law Society. The Law Society is not bound by any comments which the Court of
Appeal makes, as the Court of Appeal and the Society discharge different functions. Nor should
the direction to send a copy have been made part of the formal order; it was mere information
for the beneﬁt of counsel and the parties. It was part of controlling proceedings and regulating
conduct of its ofﬁcers, not part of adjudicating the merits of the lawsuit.²
Application to dismiss an appeal
14.74 On application, a panel of the Court of Appeal may dismiss all or part of
an appeal and may make any order that the circumstances require, including a
costs award, if
(a) the Court of Appeal has no jurisdiction,
(b) the appeal is moot,
(c) the appeal is frivolous, vexatious, without merit or improper, or
(d) the appeal or any step in the appeal is an abuse of process.
[Alta. Reg.41/14]

Related Provisions
3.68 (striking pleadings) ; 14.38(2) (a) (dismissing or allowing appeals) ; 14.90 (sanctions)

An application to dismiss an appeal summarily was denied, and it was left to proceed in the
usual way.³
Such summary applications are not encouraged, as they can double the appellant’s
work, and that was one reason to increase the costs of that application.⁴
One justice of appeal cannot split off one issue from a pending appeal and then decide it. That
is not merely incidental to an appeal, and so is not an exception (by R.14.37) to s.7 of the Court
of Appeal Act making a quorum, 3 judges. The merits must be decided by at least three judges.⁵
Nor is it wise to try to have a motions panel of the justices of appeal decide in advance the one
issue. The logistics, such as times for oral argument, are not suitable. Nor is there any procedure
for deciding appeals summarily. And such summary decision saves little if any resources, on the
part of counsel or the Court. And if deciding the one preliminary issue did not resolve the whole
dispute, then full argument of the other issues would be needed.⁶

1 McDonald v. Brookﬁeld Asset Mgmt. (#2) 2016 ABCA 419, Calg 1501 0131 AC (Dec 23).
2 Kennedy v. T’ee. of 1985 Sawridge Tr.2017 ABCA 439, Edm 1703 0252 AC (Dec 19).
3 Boyd v. JBS Foods Can. (#1) 2015 ABCA 120, Calg 1401 0234 AC (Mar 27).

14-208
R.14.75(1) PART 14: APPEALS
An appeal was dismissed by a panel under R.14.74 as frivolous, vexatious, without merit,
improper, or an abuse of process. The appellant had been ﬁling interim appeals to the Court of
Appeal before the full process in the statutory bodies had been exhausted, and had unused routes
to appeal there. A preliminary application over whether the discipline tribunal has jurisdiction is
generally a poor idea. And the merits appeal to a statutory tribunal was imminent.¹
Quashing an appeal as “manifestly without merit” has a high threshold that should only be
utilized in exceptional circumstances. The applicable test is whether it is possible that a single
ground of appeal may succeed. Courts cannot quash some grounds of appeal while permitting
others to go forward.²
Disposing of appeals
14.75(1) Unless an enactment otherwise provides, when deciding an appeal, the
Court of Appeal may
(a) receive further evidence,
(b) draw inferences of fact,
(c) give any judgment or order that ought to have been made by the
court appealed from,
(d) direct the resumption or continuation of any proceeding before the
court appealed from,
(e) vary or reverse a ﬁnding on any question, without interfering with
the ﬁnding or decision on any other question,
(f) direct a new trial on the whole or any part of the decision under
appeal, or with respect to some or all of the parties, and
(g) give any other decision or direction required to resolve the appeal.
[Alta. Reg.41/14]
A. Cross References
On when one can appeal, see the notes to R.14.4(4) in this book, and see the Civil Procedure
Encyclopedia.³
On new evidence adduced on appeal, see the notes to Rr.14.38, 14.45 and 14.70.
B. Powers
Rule 14.75(1) is only about the mode of receiving evidence (live, on commission, etc. ) ; it gives
no right to discovery.⁴
If a court of appeal has to decide on unfamiliar law, it cannot hear evidence
on it, but can look at all the authorities cited by the advocates.⁵

Using Rr.14.75(c) and 14.86, the Court amended a notice of motion (written application) ﬁled
in King's Bench,¹
and that is fairly common.
A statutory enlargement of the powers of the judge appealed from entails a similar enlargement
of the powers of the Court of Appeal on appeal.²
C. Factual and Legal Questions and Standard of Review³
1. Fact Findings
(a) Trial Judge Can Reject Evidence
A trial judge need not entirely adopt the evidence of any witness, even the expert appraisers,
so long as he or she does not misconstrue the evidence.⁴
It is the right and often the duty of a trial
judge when faced with conﬂicting expert witnesses to choose the evidence of one over the other,
so long as his or her choice is reasoned.⁵
Not adopting evidence is not (of itself) a reason to upset
the decision under appeal.
(b) Court of Appeal's Usual Deference to Fact Findings
(i) General Test
A host of cases⁶
say that an appeal court should not substitute its own fact ﬁnding for a different
fact ﬁnding at trial unless there was palpable and overriding error at trial.⁷

14-210
R.14.75(1) PART 14: APPEALS
Canada in Vavilov has settled that test, and one does not look at the facts, nor the circumstances
or expertise of the tribunal to adjust to standard of review.¹
“Palpable” means plain to see, and
where one need not reconsider all the evidence to identify it, or untruth or inapplicability in light of
the evidence accepted or the fact ﬁndings, and this is a tougher test than reasonableness.²
It is
not enough for an appellant to show the appeal court some good evidence tending the other way,
so long as there was some evidence which would support the trial fact ﬁnding, and no clear error
in the trial reasons or charge to the jury.³
It is hard to ﬁnd palpable and overriding error, if there is
some evidence to support the trial judge’s fact ﬁnding or inference.⁴
Nor should an appeal court
second guess the weight appropriate for various types of evidence, and factual conclusions are
usually inextricably linked to the weight assigned.⁵
“Overriding” means the error affected the result in the lower court, or went to the very core of
the outcome there.⁶
That also applies to the probative value of conﬂicting expert evidence.⁷
The same test of
palpable and overriding error applies to the trial judge’s fact inferences.⁸

Many older cases held that it is easier for a Court of Appeal to reverse inferences or characterizations of
undisputed facts than to reverse direct fact ﬁndings: Bain v. C. V. Ry. (PC) [1921] 2 AC 412, [1921] 3 WWR
44, 47; Victory (R. M. ) v. Sask. Guar. & Fidelity Co. [1928] SCR 264, 269-70, [1928] 2 DLR 829; Trudel
v. Grant [1955] Que QB 746, 749-50, affd [1957] SCR vii; St. Pierre v. Tanguay [1957] Que QB 844, affd
[1959] SCR 21; see Shields v. Shields, supra. Then for many years the Supreme Court did not refer to
that rule, and gave many decisions in conﬂict with it: The palpable and overriding standard also applies to a
question of mixed law and fact like a ﬁnding of negligence: Housen v. Nikolaisen (and Shellbrook (R. M. ) )
2002 SCC 33, 286 NR 1 (¶’s 11-25). And to inferences: Madge v. Meyer 2001 ABCA 97, 281 AR 143. Cf.
Laﬂamme v. Prudential-Bache Commodities Can. [2000] 1 SCR 638, 253 NR 155, 176-77 (¶’s 41, 42).
And palpable and overriding error is necessary to reverse inferences of fact, or questions of mixed law and
fact like a ﬁnding of negligence: Housen v. Nikolaisen, supra (¶’s 19-20, 26-36) ; cf. McPhee v. C. P. R.
(1913) 49 SCR 43, 53-54, 5 WWR 926. They seem to have reverted to the old rule in a few cases: RJR-
Macdonald v. A. -G. Can. [1994] 1 SCR 311, 164 NR 1; cf. Gould (Yukon Human Rts. Comm. ) v. Yukon
Order of Pioneers [1996] 1 SCR 571, 133 DLR(4th) 449, ¶ 4 (a case on judicial review, not appeal). See
further the C. P. E. , Chapter 75, Part L.8. Then they repudiated the old rule again: H. L. v. A. -G. Can.2005
SCC 25, [2005] 1 SCR 401 (¶’s 54, 72-74).

14-211
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.75(1)
(ii) Topic of Facts
On a nonprosecution motion, whether an action has been signiﬁcantly advanced is a decision
entitled to deference on appeal. So are decisions about inordinate delay, its inexcusability, and
whether there was signiﬁcant prejudice.¹
A fact ﬁnding can be upset if plainly wrong and contrary to almost all (or all) the evidence.²
Or
if there was no evidence to support it, especially a ﬁnding of litigation misconduct.³
Errors may be
bad enough to upset fact ﬁndings.⁴
The court of appeal can reverse the ﬁnding, if the trial court
ignored conclusive and relevant evidence, or misunderstood the evidence, or drew erroneous
conclusions from it.⁵
There is a description of reasons for an appeal court to reverse a trial judge’s
fact ﬁndings.⁶
Palpable and overriding error was found in a few cases.⁷
Deciding the case on a basis not pleaded is palpable error.⁸
Where the trial judge omits all
mention of an important issue, see the Indl. Teletype case.⁹
A jury verdict can be set aside on appeal even if not “perverse”, which term would suggest
moral turpitude. The test is unreasonableness: could reasonable people come to that conclusion
on that evidence as a whole and acting judicially have so decided?¹⁰
A jury verdict should be
examined with respect, but not awe.¹¹
The trial judge’s advantage over the Court of Appeal on fact issues is even greater when the
judge has taken a view at some other location and his or her reasons show that it inﬂuenced the
judge.¹²

14-212
R.14.75(1) PART 14: APPEALS
Deference to fact ﬁndings extends also to procedural or jurisdictional fact decisions.¹
Whether
a (family) case has enough real and substantial connection with our province to give our courts
jurisdiction, is a fact question, not to be lightly upset on appeal.²
(iii) Related Legal Questions
A court of appeal owes deference to a trial judge’s fact ﬁndings, even when the trial judge
made errors of law in applying the law to those fact ﬁndings.³
One ﬂeeting reference in a civil trial
judgment to inability to “conclude with certainty” is not the wrong standard of proof, especially if
later the judge recites “to prove on a balance of probabilities”.⁴
(c) What is a Question of Fact
An expression of the trial judge’s opinion on a factual question is a fact ﬁnding; no formal words
are necessary.⁵
If a trial judge applies the right legal standard of care, these are fact ﬁndings which get the
usual deference: whether an act met that standard or was negligent;⁶
whether municipal action is
policy or operative;⁷
applying the test for relevance to the facts of an individual case.⁸
Mixed questions are questions about whether the facts satisfy the legal test.⁹
Applying a legal
test to a set of facts is a mixed question¹⁰
and if the court alters the underlying legal question then
a legal question arises.¹¹
Whether a limitation period has expired is a question of mixed law and

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.75(1)
fact.¹
So is applying a Rule of Court to a ﬁxed set of facts.²
So is whether there was a substantial
advance in a lawsuit, under R.4.33.³
Questions of causation are typically issues of mixed law and
fact.⁴
Whether an action has been signiﬁcantly advanced under R.4.33 is a question of mixed fact
and law (which includes measurement), reviewed for palpable and overriding error.⁵
On mixed law
and fact, see further Part C.4(d).
Legal questions can be covert, without being expressly spelled out by the trial judge.⁶
But
appellate courts should be slow to ﬁnd such covert legal questions, as every mixed question has
some legal question inside it. Merely ﬁnding that the result was wrong so concluding that there
must be a hidden legal error, is not enough.⁷
The expectations, understanding, and practice of a certain industry are matters of fact, not
law, and use of a single court decision from elsewhere as a precedent cannot be used to displace
evidence on the point.⁸
If a trial judge is required to consider certain facts (e. g. context), it is error not to do so.⁹
If a chambers judge decides that a case is not suitable for summary resolution, on appeal
that decision is entitled to deference.¹⁰
In an appeal from summary judgment, the test¹¹
is error of
law, or unreasonable weight given to relevant considerations.¹²
For identifying the proper tests,
the standard is correctness.¹³
On factual questions, the test is palpable and overriding error.¹⁴
Any aspect of summary judgment involving evidence or facts, even partly, invokes a deferential
standard.¹⁵
Refusal to dismiss a claim summarily, absent clear error, gets deference and practically
speaking is hard to upset.¹⁶
A court of appeal should give a deferential approach to fact ﬁndings

refusal to grant summary judgment because he ﬁnds a doubt, or agrees with the respondent who opposes

R.14.75(1) PART 14: APPEALS
at a summary trial, whether the evidence was oral or documentary. There are many reasons for
deference.¹
The same test holds for striking out pleadings, but since evidence is forbidden there, the Court
of Appeal is in as good a position as the Chambers Judge. But it will not substitute its opinion
unless the Chambers Judge was unreasonable.²
Even in a criminal case, the court of appeal should not speculate as to possible alternative
facts not supported even by the appellant’s own evidence.³
(d) Credibility Findings
Appeal courts’ reluctance to upset fact ﬁndings at trial applies all the more strongly if credibility
or demeanor of witnesses is relevant.⁴
It is difﬁcult to attack a trial judge’s credibility ﬁnding
because the trial judge spent more time or a stricter review on the losing side’s evidence than on
the winning side’s evidence, especially as the role of one single piece of evidence is very limited,
and the trial judge’s treatment must be inﬂuenced by what counsel argue or emphasize, not on
matters they do not argue or emphasize.⁵
The trial judge also has an advantage over the appeal
court where the personality, motive, intent, good faith, and character of the witness is directly in
issue in the suit.⁶
Credibility involves more than honesty: it includes ability to see, evaluate, recall,
and recount, without distortion.⁷
Some fairly common personality disorders seriously warp the
memory. Great emotion or stress can also do that. So the witness’ good faith is not the only aspect
of credibility.
But credibility ﬁndings are not an absolute bar to an appeal court: for example, the appeal court
can see whether the evidence which the trial judge accepted (ignoring that which he rejected)
supports the judge’s ﬁndings.⁸
Credibility ﬁndings at trial on peripheral topics do not bar allowing
the appeal.⁹

14-215
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.75(1)
In a contested matter, established tools to assess credibility include inconsistencies and
weaknesses in one witness’ evidence; ability to review independent evidence; ability to assess
whether one witness’ evidence is plausible, based on what a practical and informed person would
think, not false or frail assumptions about human behavior; a witness’ demeanor; and motives
of witnesses to fabricate. A judge need not believe or disbelieve all of a witness’ testimony, and
different parts can get different weight.¹
A judge put considerable weight on a witness’ answering
questions or not answering them, and consistence with the witness’ other evidence and the
evidence from other sources. And reasonableness and probability of a witness’ evidence. See
evasiveness in answering was bad for one witness.²
(e) Rationale for Appellate Deference³
Appellate deference is not conﬁned to cases where the judge (or tribunal) has an advantage
by seeing and hearing live witnesses.⁴
The deference is given in order to conserve resources,
and to promote autonomy and integrity of trials, as well. There is no point to retrying something
on the merits (absent error in principle or unfair procedure) : it just wastes time and money and
destroys certainty.⁵
Any kind of factual conclusion involves weighing evidence and hence appellate
deference.⁶
If fact ﬁndings involve no palpable and overriding error, then only palpable error in the
inference-drawing process allows interference on appeal.⁷
(f) Fact Findings by Tribunals
Even without a privative clause, a court of appeal owes curial deference to a Securities
Commission’s penalty order,⁸
a discipline conviction by a self-regulating profession,⁹
or a
decision of the Surface Rights Board.¹⁰
But is there curial deference, and is the standard of review

R.14.75(1) PART 14: APPEALS
correctness, if there is no effective privative clause, and the legislation uses wide words to give
the powers of the appeal court? The Supreme Court of Canada has expressed great deference
to expert tribunals, e. g. on their interpretation of legislation, treaties, and the law of ﬁduciaries.¹
There used to be three standards of review on appeals from tribunals: correctness,
reasonableness, and patent unreasonableness.²
But patent unreasonableness now is gone,
leaving only the other two.³
The Supreme Court again changed the standard of review from a
tribunal. It is presumed to be reasonableness,⁴
but statutes can change that or suggest a different
standard. If statute provides an appeal, then legal questions are decided on a correctness
standard and factual ones on palpable and overriding error. Even on judicial review with no
different statutory standard, constitutional questions and general very important questions of law
are reviewed on the correctness standard.⁵
On selecting the standard of review for an appeal from
a specialized tribunal, see the Imp. Oil case.⁶
Even if a statute expressly lets the Court of Appeal draw inferences, it should still not upset fact
ﬁndings by the tribunal appealed from unless they are unreasonable or palpably and demonstrably
wrong, whether or not the issue is credibility.⁷
Since standards of review on appeal and on judicial review overlap so much, it is useful to note
the Supreme Court’s explanation of the meaning of “reasonable” in this context.⁸
Where a judge was asked to give judicial review, then whether he or she used the right
standard of review is tested on appeal on the standard of correctness.⁹
The same is true when a
statutory appeal tribunal reviews a decision of a lower body.¹⁰

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.75(1)
What is the appropriate standard of review for judicial review is a question of law, and has to
be set by the court, and cannot be ﬁxed by an agreement between the parties.¹
Or whether procedural fairness is a question of law inviting no deference on appeal, see
subpart C.2(f) below.
(g) Second Level of Appeal
A further appeal to a higher court on fact questions raises logical questions, on which the cases²
conﬂict. The highest court need not give any deference to fact ﬁndings by the (intermediate) ﬁrst
appellate court,³
and that seems to be the approach of the Supreme Court of Canada (express or
implicit) in almost all cases⁴
in the last 45 or so years.⁵
Before then, concurrent fact ﬁndings used
to be hard to upset,⁶
even if the result in the two courts was different.⁷
The standard of review of judicial review of a specialized appellate tribunal, on appeal from
the judge to the Court of Appeal, is discussed.⁸
If a judge hears an appeal from an arbitrator, on a
further appeal to the Court of Appeal from the judge, the standard of review is correctness, even
if the judge made new fact ﬁndings.⁹
(h) Giving Reasons for Fact Findings
In reviewing the reasons of a trial judge, an appeal court should not parse the reasons carefully
and exactly, nor look at isolated sentences or passages. The whole thing should be read together,
each part in light of the other, and each qualifying the other.¹⁰
And without skepticism.¹¹
Reasons
for judgment by the judge are to be read reasonably generously, and not contra proferentem.¹²

14-218
R.14.75(1) PART 14: APPEALS
There is a review of a number of the requirements where a tribunal is obliged by law to give
reasons for its decision.¹
Absence of reasons is probably not enough itself to upset fact ﬁndings.²
The effect of absence
of reasons (or extreme brevity) depends upon how strongly the evidence points one way.³
Reasons
largely cribbed from one party’s written submissions are not necessarily inadequate, unless they
contain cogent evidence that the judge did not come to grips with the issues and decide them
impartially and independently. Relevant are the nature of the case, what was copied, degree of
copying, and how the copying functions in the reasons as a whole.⁴
Is there any standard of review
when an appeal court considers adequacy of reasons of the tribunal? It is not completely clear
whether deference should be shown, or whether correctness is the exact term.⁵
On adequacy of the reasons for decision under appeal, and the impact of that on appellate
review, see further Rr.9.2n. C, 14.15 n. A, and 14.18 n. A.
Occasionally the legal result of fact ﬁnding A makes fact ﬁnding B irrelevant, and so the Court
of Appeal need not consider whether fact ﬁnding B was correct or not.⁶
2. Damage Awards, Apportionment, and Family Law
As with fact ﬁndings, it takes palpable and overriding error, or grossly excessive or inferior
amounts, or use of a wrong principle, or omitting a relevant factor, to let an appeal court change
most damage awards.⁷

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.75(1)
discretion, are not grounds for an appeal court to interfere with it.¹
It is a factual question to be
decided by the jury, and upset on appeal only for palpable and overriding error.²
A jury has an even
wider margin to depart from a proper ﬁgure than does a trial judge sitting alone. One award was
held not to be wholly disproportionate nor shockingly unreasonable.³
Examination of leading cases of high authority suggests that such disproportion in amount
probably must be as big as a multiple (or small fraction) of the proper amount to permit appellate
intervention.⁴
It is doubtful that (say) 50% too much or too little is a big enough discrepancy for
appellate intervention.
But there are limits to punitive damage awards, and appellate courts should police them more
carefully than they police general damages. The test is whether general plus punitive damages
exceed what is rationally required to punish the defendant.⁵
It is easier for a Court of Appeal to
vary a punitive damages award than other types of damage award.⁶
Similar appellate deference is owed to apportionments of liability,⁷
especially concurrent
ﬁndings by trial and appellate courts.⁸
A matrimonial support order is also said to be discretionary, for appeal purposes.⁹
There is
also a deferential standard of appeal from an order respecting child support.¹⁰
An appeal court
in a family law case should accord considerable deference to the trial or chambers decision,
and not overturn it unless it exceeds the bounds of what is reasonable.¹¹
There must be an error
in principle,¹²
or clear misapprehension of the evidence, or clearly a wrong award. Family cases

rather than proper relative blame test). Deference to a trial judge on apportionment of fault is conditional on
his having resolved all relevant conﬂicts of fact/evidence; declining to decide one of them (a critical one) led
to a new trial: Moulton v. Harrold 2009 ABCA 176, 454 AR 395.

12 e. g. changing de facto custody pending trial, rather than sending the matter on to trial quickly: Richter v.

R.14.75(1) PART 14: APPEALS
involve “fact based and discretionary” issues.¹
The standard there is palpable and overriding
error.²
In an appeal from a custody order, the question is whether the judge clearly acted on some
wrong principle, or disregarded material evidence.³
The standard is even highly deferential,⁴
as is
a decision about custody (parenting).⁵
Decisions in family law matters are usually fact-based and discretionary, and often engage a
high degree of deference on appeal.⁶
That is even more so if the decision is procedural, rather
than substantive.⁷
A Court of Appeal should not upset an equalization allowance in matrimonial
property litigation without error of law, or egregious error of fact; it is a discretion in Quebec law.⁸
3. Miscellaneous
Where the only question before the Court of Appeal had been whether authorities had been
followed, there is no reason for the Supreme Court of Canada to interfere.⁹
Even if there may have been some (pardonable) shortcuts in procedure, that is not a ground
for the Court of Appeal to reverse a fact ﬁnding, where the appellant does not seek a new hearing.¹⁰
On appeal from certiﬁcation of a class action, the standard of review is especially deferential.¹¹
A judge’s certifying that a certain question in the appeal is one of general importance (as
statute requires for a further appeal) somewhat lowers the standard of review of that question on
the further appeal.¹²
On appeal from a judge’s selection of the appropriate remedy after ﬁnding a breach of company
legislation, it takes a signiﬁcant disproportion between illness and cure, for the Court of Appeal
to intervene.¹³
Precedent on the standard of review on appeal in a speciﬁc type of case, obviates the need in
future such cases to conduct afresh a full analysis of the standard in context, and that continues
when the relevant Act is re-enacted in substantially the same language.¹⁴

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.75(1)
A Mareva injunction or similar (statutory) pre-trial order to preserve assets, is said to be
discretionary, and not to be simply retried or reweighed on appeal.¹
Whether bankruptcy should be found or imposed after proving one act of bankruptcy, vis-à-vis
one creditor, and whether that creditor has a collateral purpose, and whether the debtor lacks all
assets, are discretionary and so hard to upset on appeal.²
Whether a question is ﬁt for summary
decision under R.3.2(2) is partly discretionary and is owed some deference. But whether the
preconditions in R.3.2(2) are met is a question of law; the standard of review there is correctness.³
4. Legal Questions
On what is a question of law or fact, see also n. C.1(c), supra.
(a) Usual Rule of Correctness
On a pure question of law or jurisdiction, usually there is no deference: the standard of review
on appeal is correctness.⁴
In other words, the Court of Appeal substitutes its own view. Following
a wrong principle or failing to consider the applicable test is an error of law and for that the test
is correctness and there is no deference.⁵
How to interpret a statute⁶
or the Rules of Court⁷
is
usually such a question of pure law, whether or not the tribunal appealed from had expertise.⁸
How to interpret an order is tested on appeal on the correctness standard.⁹
A trial judge gets

R.14.75(1) PART 14: APPEALS
substantive appellate deference when she interprets her own order.¹
How to construe a claim in
a patent is also a question of pure law.²
So too is whether res judicata applies.³
Interpretation of
a semi-legislative policy document may be a question of law.⁴
In land use (planning) appeals, the
interpretation of a certain term in a written instrument is a question of law, not fact, but the Court
of Appeal is reluctant to interfere with the interpretation of a policy or a plan.⁵
Though the standard of review for legal questions is usually correctness, as with any question
the Court of Appeal will not upset a decision for a legal error unless it relates to a material issue in
the dispute which would have affected the result of the case if decided the other way.⁶
On questions of jurisdiction, see Part D.2(a) below.
Even if a decision (whether to extend time) is discretionary, if the chambers justice or
applications judge did not consider one of the necessary elements of the legal test, in effect the
superior court applied the wrong law, and so the standard of review on appeal is correctness.⁷
However, if a certain issue was not raised before the tribunal and so not discussed in its reasons,
the Court of Appeal has to review the decision in light of reasons which could be offered to
support it.⁸
(b) Home Statute
But an expert tribunal’s interpretation of a section of its home statute often⁹
need only be
reasonable, and so a later different interpretation of the same section in a different case is not
necessarily bad, if it is also reasonable.¹⁰
Whether the standard of review on interpretation of a
tribunal’s home statute is correctness or deferential, depends on a number of things, including
whether the legislation provides for an actual appeal from the tribunal, whether leave to appeal
is needed, the role of expertise, and whether there is only one tribunal, or one for each area or
municipality? The intent of the legislature is the critical question?¹¹
But apparently the standard of review is correctness, for the meaning of the word “privilege”
in the Commissioner’s home statute, as that is a term of art in general law and has no different
meaning in that statute.¹²

14-223
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.75(1)
(c) Miscellaneous Topics
Even if a judge hearing a judicial review adopts the proper standard of review, whether he or
she applied it properly is also a legal question reviewed on appeal for correctness, even if the
correct standard of review was reasonableness, and the judge saw that and tried to use it?¹
What are the right tests to use for a procedural or evidentiary problem is a question of law
reviewable on a correctness standard.²
If the right test is used, on appeal of a question of judge
recusal, the standard of review is deferential.³
Whether to admit expert reports into ﬁling and waive notice requirements, involves factual
issues on which the Court of Appeal owes deference to the trial judge.⁴
Who has standing may
be a question of law tested on correctness?⁵
On omitting a necessary element, see subpart (1)
above.
Some aspects of contempt of court are legal and correctness is the standard; others are
discretionary, and reasonableness is the standard.⁶
(d) Mixed Law and Fact
See also Part C.1(c) above.
A question of mixed law and fact, such as whether an act is negligence, cannot be upset
without palpable and overriding error.⁷
There is a discussion of what is such a mixed question:
the test is whether the facts satisfy the legal tests.⁸
Whether a duty of care exists is a question
of law reviewed on a standard of correctness.⁹
What is the proper standard of care (for someone
supervising a learner driver) is a question of law and so on appeal is reviewed on the standard of
correctness.¹⁰
Whether a particular act is contempt of court is a question of mixed law and fact, so
palpable and overriding error is the test.¹¹

R.14.75(1) PART 14: APPEALS
(e) Interpreting a Contract
This topic is currently puzzling, and some of the case law is difﬁcult to reconcile.
But facts can be germane to how to interpret a contract.¹
Those fact ﬁndings by the trial judge
can be upset only for palpable and overriding error.²
Any fact ﬁndings or factual inferences are
decided on the standard of palpable and overriding error.³
And now interpretation of a contract
often gets deferential treatment on appeal.⁴
How to interpret a contract used to be held to be a question of law reviewable for correctness.⁵
But now it is often⁶
accorded deferential treatment on appeal.⁷
Indeed with wider scope for
objective circumstances to be used in the interpretation, it will be common for facts to be used,
so interpreting a contract is usually a question of mixed law and fact.⁸
However, despite the
Supreme Court of Canada’s decision, interpreting a contract can be a question of law reviewed
on the correctness standard.⁹
Especially where the contract is a standard form contract used
by everyone in a whole industry or profession, and so its interpretation has great public value,¹⁰
and no speciﬁc facts are useful to aid interpretation.¹¹
The standard form lack of deference is
exceptional, and usually needs evidence to prove it exists.¹²
By analogy, the court must be correct

(not merely reasonable) in interpreting the meaning of common terms of art in contracts, much
as those generally accepted in a particular industry (unless the particular contract modiﬁed the
meaning).¹
But the interpretation of an insurance policy is to be viewed in the context of some
basic concepts of insurance, such as recovery being limited to indemnity for actual loss, and
subject to mitigation clauses in the policy.²
An extricable question of law alone for interpretation may (uncommonly) arise, such as using
an incorrect principle, or leaving out a required part of a legal test or a relevant factor.³
Even when
the test for appellate review of interpretation of a contract is reasonableness, the established
principles of contractual interpretation still must be followed, and sometimes they lead to only one
reasonable interpretation.⁴
Then the standard is correctness.
However, though questions of contractual interpretation can be factual, legal, or mixed, and
one needs delicate consideration of the narrow issue actually in dispute, in general contractual
interpretation is a mixed question, not a legal question.⁵
The test may be whether the interpretation
question is purely legal, and whether it is likely to be precedent setting.⁶
And decisions on
contractual interpretation by a commercial arbitrator (not a judge) are usually decided on the
reasonableness standard.⁷
What are the essential terms of a contract,⁸
and whether a contract to be interpreted is
ambiguous⁹
is a question of fact requiring palpable and overriding error for standard of review. But
whether alleged part performance is “unequivocally referable” to a certain alleged contract is a
question of law reviewable for correctness.¹⁰
(f) Fair Procedure
Procedural fairness is clearly a question of law reviewable on a correctness standard.¹¹
But tribunal procedure not involving natural justice, procedural fairness, or statutory
requirements, gets deference.¹²

14-226
R.14.75(1) PART 14: APPEALS
D. Discretionary and Interlocutory Questions: Standard of Review¹
1. General Principle
Appellate courts are slow to reverse discretionary orders,²
the standard being palpable and
overriding error, or reasonableness.³
A case management decision about production of documents or privilege is discretionary
and gets appellate deference, especially where the court has had to balance many competing
factors. Relevance or materiality of records, involves mixed fact and law and some discretion, and
is reviewed on the still more deferential standard of palpable and overriding error. The content
and scope of public interest immunity and solicitor-client privilege are reviewed on appeal for
correctness, but applying the law to particular documents is often mixed fact and law, reviewed for
palpable and overriding error.⁴
But there are many exceptions. One exception, allowing appellate intervention, is an error of
law by overlooking a precondition to the court’s power to act.⁵
The analysis of what is or is not a
discretionary order is often lacking, or mushy,⁶
so loose terminology can drive the decision.
A chambers decision need not be set aside on appeal just because one of several reasons
given by the chambers justice is wrong, if the matter is discretionary. The applicant would have
to show that there were no reasons upon which that discretion reasonably could have been
exercised.⁷
Still less should the decision be reversed because of looking at a few irrelevant

considerations among the majority of correct and relevant ones.¹
But what seems to be a mere

a substantive right.²
On appeals partway through a trial, see R.14.04(4) n. A.5.
Whether the judge used the right tests is a question of law and so reviewable on the standard
of correctness.³
Even a discretionary decision (refusing to remove a plaintiff said not to exist in
law) may be reversed if the issues were mischaracterized and all the evidence was contrary.⁴
Or
if wrong weight was given, the decision was arbitrary or the decision was so plainly wrong as to
amount to a failure of justice.⁵
Staying (or not staying) a suit involves a judicial discretion.⁶
The Court of Appeal can interfere
where the legal criteria for that were misidentiﬁed or misapplied, including not giving sufﬁcient or
any weight to a relevant factor.⁷
A judge who heard an injunction motion loses appellate deference if there is misunderstanding of
law or evidence, an error in an inference, a change in circumstances, or simple unreasonableness.⁸
By the Rules, costs are expressly discretionary, and hard to vary on appeal,⁹
except for error
in principle.¹⁰
The reasons for a costs award can be brief, if previous reasons for the substantive
decision make the relevant fact ﬁndings and remarks.¹¹

14-228
R.14.75(1) PART 14: APPEALS
The parties can agree to have the Court of Appeal decide trial costs, avoiding a new hearing.¹
Though party-party costs are discretionary, that discretion is subject to the Rules of Court and
the need to act judicially on the facts of the case. Appellate review is not impossible. It is open for
misdirection on the applicable law, palpable error of facts, or unreasonable exercise of discretion.²
The word “discretion” has a number of different meanings, and it can be a cause or an effect.
Sometimes it can lead to circular reasoning. The Saskatchewan Court of Appeal says that the fact
that the lower court’s decision was discretionary does not determine the standard of review; the
standard depends on the nature of the error alleged. “Discretionary” is shorthand for more than
one potential outcome, based on how the court views the facts and applies the principles. That
may be flexible or variable because there are a number of factors which may not all properly apply
on these facts, the weight to give each factor, and the nature of a governing principle.³
2. Procedural Decisions
(a) General
Not every interlocutory order is discretionary.⁴
An appeal based on trial fairness generally
raises questions of law, whether or not the decisions were discretionary, as discretions must be
exercised judicially, giving sufﬁcient weight to all relevant considerations.⁵
But appeal courts are often reluctant to upset interlocutory orders, unless they are clearly unjust
or unreasonable.⁶
Even more so, if they are purely interim, and to be adjusted or recalculated later.⁷
Therefore, an appeal from a procedural decision in chambers often attracts the “reasonableness”
standard of review on appeal. If so, that means that the appellant has to show either insufﬁcient
weight to relevant factors, arbitrariness, use of a wrong principle, an erroneous view of facts, or
likely failure of justice.⁸
How to interpret a pleading, and whether it clearly discloses no arguable cause of action
to strike out, are legal questions reviewed on appeal on the correctness standard.⁹
Otherwise
decisions to strike out pleadings get deference on appeal, as do all decisions whether to strike out

for costs, and decides in this case that one is weightier than another: Jackson v. C. N. R. (#2) 2015 ABCA
89, 599 AR 237 (¶ 13) ; cf. Thompson v. Procrane 2016 ABCA 71, [2016] AJ #237 (one JA) (¶’s 16-17).
19 Shwaykosky v. Pattison 2015 ABCA 337, Edm 1503 0038 AC (Nov 4).

14-232
R.14.75(1) PART 14: APPEALS
On applying a correct legal test (for relevance) to a fact situation, see the Enron case.¹
An
appellatae tribunal with expertise which does not state the standard of review which it applied
need not be reversed.²
Admitting expert evidence or not used to be simply called a deferential
question,³
and that is still true of qualiﬁcations and probative value,⁴
unless there was error in
principle.⁵
3. Management and Logistics
Case management judges’ decisions get high deference on appeal,⁶
even if they dismiss the
suit for want of prosecution.⁷
Appellate reversal would come only from arbitrariness, or clearly
wrong injustice.⁸
Or error in law or principle, or a clearly unreasonable decision.⁹
Factual ﬁndings
reached during case management also get their usual appellate deference.¹⁰
The same deference
is given to decisions at case conference sessions.¹¹
That deference is even greater, if practicalities govern which branches of plans or orders
matter, and which do not, and whether to modify the plan or waive the breach.¹²
A Court of Appeal
should be slow to second guess or upset a chambers justice on a question of pure logistics,
especially if he or she is a case management Judge.¹³

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.75(2)
Appeals in family matters from scheduling decisions are discouraged as they are discretionary.
The same is true of adjournment decisions, so long as not based on an incorrect legal principle.¹
But if the adjournment decision engages procedural fairness, the standard of review on appeal is
correctness. The same is true of a decision to proceed with an application in the absence of one
party.²
Deference is owed to an assessment ofﬁcer’s scheduling decisions.³
Deference is also owed
to a decision about whether a timetable will cure delay in a suit,⁴
or a case management decision.⁵
And to decisions whether earlier directives have been obeyed.⁶
Also attracting deference are
decisions about refusal to postpone a trial yet again because of a switch of lawyers,⁷
the order
in which to hear cross-motions,⁸
how long cross-examination should continue,⁹
when to decide a
preliminary issue,¹⁰
whether to hold a summary trial or use special procedures instead,¹¹
whether
to try a test case¹²
Analogous is an appeal from a justice who has been overseeing proceedings about a company
under the Companies’ Creditors Arrangements Act, because the supervising justice, is steeped in
the intricacies of the proceeding, and so is owed a high degree of deference.¹³
(2) The Court of Appeal may dismiss an appeal despite an error of law or fact, a
misdirection or an erroneous ruling on the evidence where
(a) no substantial wrong or miscarriage of justice has resulted,
(b) the decision would have been the same despite the error, or
(c) despite any irregularity, no signiﬁcant prejudice has been
experienced by any party.

Related Provisions
14.16 (appeal record) ; 14.28 (record before the Court) ; 14.32(3) (a) (failure to appear at

appeal); 14.70 (no fresh evidence without order) ; 14.71 (interlocutory orders no barrier to
appeal)

14-234
R.14.75(2) PART 14: APPEALS
A. Basic Principles of Appeals
1. The Core Question
An appeal is from the formal judgment, not from the reasons for decision of the judge appealed
from.¹
So the appeal fails if the formal judgment is correct, even if the reasons are wrong or
non-existent.²
The appeal is not from and cannot be from, any unnecessary recitals in the trial
court’s judgment.³
That approach is also used to decide whether the appeal is worth under $25
thousand, or is premature or for a useless declaration.⁴
And the appeal court’s judgment should express any corrections with reference to the trial
court’s formal judgment, not its reasons.⁵
Where the necessary appeals have been ﬁled in time, there should not be a remedy or
question incapable of being given or decided. So if an Applications Judge decides and awards
costs, a King's Bench Justice reverses the Applications Judge and does not deal with costs, and
then the Court of Appeal restores the Applications Judge's decision, the Court of Appeal can
consider whether the Applications Judge's costs award was appropriate.⁶
2. What First Court Should Have Done
The appeal court is supposed to make the order which the court appealed from should have
made.⁷
That is complicated in three situations. The ﬁrst is where the law has changed in the meantime.⁸
A new point cannot be raised on the appeal just because the law has changed, if it was not raised
below and is not in the factums, and the relevant evidence is not printed in the appeal books.⁹
The second situation is where new fact ﬁndings would be necessary, especially not where live

Secure Enr. Serv.2018 ABCA 120, [2018] 6 WWR 575; CCS Corp. v. Secure Enr. Serv.2018 ABCA 120,
[2018] 6 WWR 575 (Mar 26) (¶ 14) ; cf. MTM Commr’l. Tr. v. Statesman Riverside Quays 2022 ABCA 328,
Calg 2201 0080 AC (Oct 11). See also R.14.4 n.

142, [2016] AJ #449; PricewaterhouseCoopers v. Perpetual Enr.2020 ABCA 254, Calg 1901 0255 AC
(Jun 29) (¶ 19) ; HML Contr. v. Pinder 2022 ABCA 185, 47 Alta LR(7th) 263, affg 2021 ABQB 899 (¶’s 16-
26). See the C. P. E. , Chapter 75, Part J.2; Anderson v. McAndrew 2005 ABCA 270, 53 Alta LR(4th) 87. Lack
of reasons is not a free-standing ground of appeal.
3 Law v. Cheng 2016 BCCA 120 (Mar 16) (¶ 20) ; CCS Corp. v. Secure Enr. , supra.

7 ManuLife Bank of Can. v. Conlin [1996] 3 SCR 415, 203 NR 81. If an Act says that on appeal from a
statutory tribunal the Court of Appeal “may direct the Commission to make a decision or perform an act. . . , ”
that is only permissive; the Court of Appeal may give the order itself, and need not send the matter back:
Re Cartaway Res. Corp. (Executive Dir. of B. C. S. C. v. Hartvikson) 2004 SCC 26, [2004] 1 SCR 672. R.
518(e) was applied in a child welfare case. Where there had been errors by the trial judge, but a new trial at
such a late date would be improper. The court of appeal substituted a temporary order with a possibility of
review later: M. T. v. Dir. of Child Welfare 2005 ABCA 125, 363 AR 306.

9 C. P. R. v. Kerr (1913) 49 SCR 33, 5 WWR 782.

14-235
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.75(2)
witnesses conﬂicted at trial.¹
The third situation is where a statute forces the Court of Appeal to
send the matter back to the tribunal appealed from, and not itself decide.²
Where a trial judge dismissed a suit but provisionally assessed damages, and the court of
appeal found liability, the defendant should have a further chance to argue damages in writing to
the appeal court (if the defendant had thought that the issue was not open).³
It would be completely improper for a Court of Appeal to hear or grant an appeal for the
purpose of misleading anyone, e. g. by getting a short description of a criminal sentence which
does not give a proper understanding of the actual sentence.⁴
3. Split Panel?
Hearing appeals by three judges (or ﬁve) raises the possibility of no majority. In one case the
Court split: two for the plaintiff, two for the defendant, one for new trial. The latter concurred partly
with each side, so as to force a new trial!⁵
If an even number of judges give judgment and they are evenly divided, the appeal fails and
the order appealed is afﬁrmed.⁶
In a sentence appeal, three judges wanted three different lengths
of parole ineligibility. The dissenter would have adopted the number chosen by the sentencing
judge. The other two wanted a longer period but disagreed on what that should be. In order to
avoid having to have the whole thing reheard, the junior judge withdrew his proposed number and
concurred in the result. So two of the three then agreed on the same period of parole in eligibility.⁷
Where one justice of appeal cannot take part in giving judgment (even because of a conﬂict),
the remaining two justices of appeal may give judgment if they are unanimous.⁸
4. Appeals are Retroactive
Once an appeal court reverses, or ﬁnds that the judge below should not have sat, his acts
become retroactively void, though they are valid and enforceable until then?⁹
After the Court of
Appeal allows an appeal, the order appealed from is treated as though it never happened, and so
anything it decided cannot be res judicata.¹⁰

14-236
R.14.75(2) PART 14: APPEALS
5. Court of Appeal Becomes Functus
Once an appeal is over and its ﬁnal judgment is entered, the Court of Appeal is functus.
Barring exceptional circumstances, it can do no more, especially not give leave to appeal from
one judge’s decision settling the terms of its formal judgment.¹
6. Appeal from Nullity
If the ﬁrst court had no jurisdiction, its jurisdiction is the only question open to an appeal court,
not the merits of the litigation (i. e. the substantive questions).²
7. Miscellaneous
It is unusual to ask to intervene in an application for leave to appeal, especially if the would-
be intervener advances purely personal interests for the same relief as the applicant for leave
seeks. If a non-party wants to join the suit because of possible damage to his reputation, that is
normally not a good ground; judicial restraint should be relied upon instead.³
On what questions an
intervener may raise, see notes to Rr.3.73 to 3.75, and R.14.25 n. A supra on “New Arguments
on Appeal”.
The Court may strike out almost all the factum of the appellant if the parts removed do not deal
with the only legal issues open. After that, there may be almost nothing left of the factum, and so
nothing more for the Court to do, but dismiss the appeal.⁴
If an appeal is decided, a party to the suit who was not a party to the appeal may have to
litigate afresh the question decided on the appeal.⁵
And even if he appeared on the appeal (being
named as a respondent) and spoke in favor of the appeal, he will not receive any costs of the
appeal.⁶
A party who took the fruits of the judgment, or who blew hot and now wants to blow cold, may
not be able to appeal.⁷
If a property preservation order is improper, taking 2½ years to appeal is a less suitable
remedy than getting on to trial.⁸
The 1992 policy of the Alberta Court of Appeal on circulation and consideration of draft
judgments⁹
was largely repealed at a Court meeting on June 8, 1999. See the 2000 Practice Note
afﬁrming that stare decisis continues.¹⁰

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.75(2)
B. Grounds for New Hearing
A court of appeal usually does not have proper grounds to enter a ﬁnal judgment reversing the
effect of the trial court,¹
so often an appellant’s only hope is a new trial.²
Since R.14.75(2) bars
new trials for technicalities not going to the substance of the matter,³
when should there be a new
trial? When there has been a miscarriage of justice, i. e. when a mistake at trial⁴
may well have
affected the ﬁnal result.⁵
The following are some of the most commonly argued grounds which
often (but not always) lead a court of appeal to order a new trial:
(a) unfair surprise at trial,⁶
improper denial of an adjournment or postponement,⁷
or denial
of the right to rebut or cross-examine⁸
on unexpected evidence,⁹
relying (for credibility on
an application) on evidence ﬁled earlier on the court ﬁle but not argued nor warned of for
this application,¹⁰
late important amendments to the pleadings at trial,¹¹
denial of the right
to argue the merits,¹²
or court access restrictions on one party with no prior notice.¹³
(b) unfair interference, animus, or prejudgment by the trial judge;¹⁴
but this is often alleged on
