14-240
R.14.75(2) PART 14: APPEALS
On appeal, procedural fairness issues have their own standard of review: correctness.¹
Other less common grounds include splitting the plaintiff’s case,²
improperly declaring a
mistrial,³
incompetence of counsel,⁴
the trial judge having heard a judicial dispute resolution,⁵
preventing a party from seeing or hearing the trial,⁶
and drawing an adverse inference for not
doing the impossible.⁷
Or an appeal with persistent failure to settle the wording of any formal
order covering the matters being appealed.⁸
A formal order or orders was needed to decide most
of the issues on appeal. The other broader legal issues could not be decided in the abstract,
without wording of speciﬁc amendments. A trial judge should not produce reasons for decision
almost entirely lifted from one party’s written argument, but there is no harm in a judge’s decision
reproducing verbatim some non-contentious background facts from a party’s brief.⁹
At one time, deciding a material factual issue only on conﬂicting afﬁdavits was a common well-
settled reason to upset a chambers decision. Now many exceptions and partial exceptions to that
have been developed, largely in summary trial and summary judgment cases.¹⁰
However, there are
limits to how much can be decided merely on conﬂicting afﬁdavits, and going outside those limits
is grounds for a new hearing of some appropriate type.¹¹
Finding misconduct at trial by counsel and reﬂecting that, is serious and requires care. Where
counsel lacked a chance to defend himself and the fact ﬁndings were of events which in fact never
occurred, or had been directed by the case management judge, the Court of Appeal changed the
costs to the ordinary level.¹²
Where the parties agreed to have a certain question of interpretation tried as a preliminary
question, if the question is ambiguous and the parties (partway through trying the question)
disagree on the question's interpretation, the object of a speedy decision under R.7.1 becomes
unattainable. On appeal from an interpretation, the Court of Appeal may refuse to answer, and
send the whole suit back to the trial court to proceed in the normal method.¹³

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.75(2)
C. Factors Against New Hearing
1. Appellant’s Fault or Argument
The appeal court may well decline to intervene if the appellant caused the problem or harm
at the ﬁrst trial.¹
There is inherent mischief when a party agrees at trial to some shortcut in evidence (such
as the opponent ﬁling reports without calling their authors, though the potential witnesses are
present), but then later that party appeals and seeks to withdraw his or her consent. That promotes
a second round of litigation (new trial) and undermines any trial admissions to expedite litigation.²
2. Harmless Error
Given subrule (2), an error in the ﬁrst court may be harmless and the appeal dismissed,³
e. g.
(a) despite use of the wrong onus, the evidence made the result inevitable.⁴
(b) the inadmissible evidence admitted reveals nothing useful.⁵
(c) the error helped the appellant and did not hurt her.⁶
(d) evidence was scanty, but the amount in the suit involved is very small.⁷
(e) an expert made use of his own side’s examination for discovery answers, but had other
independent evidence of the same facts.⁸
(f) a juror was somewhat deaf.⁹
(g) peculiar circumstances almost justiﬁed a normally forbidden line of cross-examination.¹⁰

Footnote
1 Someone who elected to go to trial without a lawyer on appeal cannot get a new trial just because he
now wants to do it over again with a lawyer. He must show a miscarriage of justice: Maher v. Edm. Drain
Cleaners (#1) 2004 ABQB 829.
2 K. S. v. Dir. of Child, Youth & Fam. Enhancement Act 2011 ABCA 117 (¶ 9) ; cf. Hill v. Hill 2013 ABCA 137,

& Parks 2020 ABCA 188, Calg 1801 0379 AC (May 6) (¶’s 36-37).

R.14.75(2) PART 14: APPEALS
(h) hearing argument from the party losing on an omitted topic, would have made no
difference.¹
(i) evidence was admitted which was potentially more prejudicial than probative, but the trial
judge did not rely on the prejudicial parts, which thus had no effect.²
(j) as stated in subpara. (2) (b) of the Rule, there are other reasons remaining, free of error
and supporting the conclusion below.³
(k) the judge wrongly conﬂated two categories and so applied the wrong test, but the right
test was clearly (also) met, so the result was correct.⁴
(l) a decision on various afﬁdavits was made at once in morning chambers after hearing
argument, because the chambers justice had read all the written materials before the
chambers hearing.⁵
In a criminal case, the onus is on the respondent to show that the error is harmless, but the
Court of Appeal can raise the equivalent of R.14.75(2) of its own motion, or the respondent can
raise it in oral argument. A new trial which would almost certainly produce the same result is not
in the public interest.⁶
In a criminal case, the test is whether there is any reasonable possibility
that the verdict would have been different without the error.⁷
Letting the jury see the accused be
charged and convicted of contempt for refusing to answer cross-examination questions is not
ground for a new trial if a new jury verdict would necessarily be the same.⁸
The Court of Appeal
is to weigh how serious was the error, its likely effect on the jury, the probable guilt on the basis
of other admissible evidence, what decision the jury made, and what conclusions they must have
reached on key issues.⁹
3. Result of Harmful Error
If the court is assessing some factor such as length of sentence or value of damages or
reimbursement, an error in principle or not considering a relevant factor, lets the Court of Appeal
intervene, so long as the error inﬂuenced the outcome in the ﬁrst court. The Court of Appeal can
then look for the proper amount. It is not necessary that the ﬁgure assessed by the trial judge also
be demonstrably unﬁt in itself, for the Court of Appeal to intervene.¹⁰
There should not be a new trial just to let the appellant reshape her case and ﬁnd different
evidence.¹¹
There is no point to a small amount of temporary support being appealed if the last
payment is due before the appeal is heard, and the award is expressly interim, without prejudice,

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.75(2)
and subject to refund if the later deﬁnitive award is lower. Especially if evidence at trial is needed
to clarify some doubtful or suspicious aspects of the limited evidence. Trial is the better remedy.¹
It is important to remember that the appeal court can order a partial new trial, e. g. limited to
liability, not damages,²
or to whether costs should change because the amended result was better
than a pre-trial offer.³
A trial judge adjourned the trial partway through after he had ruled on a proposed expert
witness, and the judge then recused himself which was tantamount to a mistrial. The Court of
Appeal would not rule on the merits of the objections to the proposed expert. The record at the
new trial and the submissions about this might be totally different, and the rulings of the recused
judge no longer bind. So an appeal from that judge was dismissed.⁴
Where a new trial is ordered, the pleadings can be amended.⁵
If there was a lot of delay, the Court of Appeal may enter the proper judgment without a new
trial.⁶
Especially if the amount in issue is fairly small and most of the evidence is written.⁷
The Court of Appeal thought there had been enough evidence of bias for a new trial, but the
appellant had since died and could not testify at the new trial, so the parties asked the court not to
order a new trial, and the Court of Appeal did the best it could with the evidence to give a decision
on the merits.⁸
If a trial judge fails to make a ﬁnding about a critical fact, the Court of Appeal should try to
resolve that issue on the existing record, if it can do so fairly, especially if a decision on that point
in one direction would be inconsistent with a number of explicit fact ﬁndings by the trial judge.⁹
If the circumstances and evidence permit only one interpretation or solution, and any other
would be unreasonable, the court may impose that inevitable remedy.¹⁰
If a Court of Appeal reverses a damage award in tort, thinking that there was not the necessary
expert evidence for it, nor clear enough pleadings, but the Supreme Court of Canada disagrees
with those conclusions, these are all factual questions. So if there is enough lay evidence to
support the ﬁndings, and the trial damage award seems reasonable, the Supreme Court of
Canada should restore the trial judge and not remit to the Court of Appeal.¹¹
A Court of Appeal decision on a substantive question is res judicata even though a new trial is
ordered.¹²
However, if the Court of Appeal orders a new trial, ordinarily the rulings of the trial judge

R.14.75(2) PART 14: APPEALS
at the ﬁrst trial create no issue estoppel,¹
unless the new trial is not on all the issues.²
So there
is no need to appeal those rulings by the ﬁrst trial judge once the Court of Appeal has ordered a
new trial.³
D. Costs of New Hearing
Typically, the appellant gets costs of his or her successful appeal in any event.⁴
But costs of
the abortive trial in the ﬁrst court will depend on who wins the new trial. Or the Court of Appeal
can send the matter of costs back to the trial judge to rehear.⁵
Most often, costs of the abortive
trial are left to the new trial judge, because who wins it will have a heavy inﬂuence on Court of
King's Bench costs.⁶
In the Performance⁷
case, the respondent who opposed the appeal had to
pay costs of the appeal (with one dissent), though the ﬂaws at trial were the fault of neither party.
If one side makes a trial abortive, e. g. by loss of counsel, it should pay costs of the abortive trial,
but it is not ordinarily fair to end its suit if it does not pay them quickly.⁸
Costs turning on what the Court of Appeal decided can be imposed at once by the Court of
Appeal, without waiting for the end of the lawsuit.⁹
But the Court of Appeal cannot award costs for
steps in the King's Bench where the amount of the ultimate judgment will be decided there, and
there were formal offers. The Court of King's Bench must set those costs.¹⁰
E. Miscellaneous
If ineffectiveness of trial counsel is raised on appeal, an oral assurance of waiver of privilege at
the appeal may be enough, but there should then be an express written waiver at an early stage.¹¹
On a motion for permission to appeal, it is relevant that the appeal would be bound to fail under
R.14.75(2).¹²
F. Who Rehears?
Almost always, a new trial after a full trial means a fresh trial judge.¹³
But if the Court of Appeal
dismisses an appeal from a ruling or ﬁnding which is not the end of the proceeding, and sends the
matter back to the trial court to continue, then it is presumed that the same judge will resume the
matter unless the Court of Appeal says that another one is to.¹⁴
If the trial judge reached a verdict,

damage calculation, it was appropriate to send the matter back to the same trial judge for recalculation:
Chernetz v. Eagle Copters Maint.2008 ABCA 265, 437 AR 104.
14 R. v. Caruso 2019 ABCA 108, [2019] AJ #341 (¶’s 9-15) (here, to give a speciﬁc sentence).

14-245
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.76
but on appeal that trial judge is held to have misapplied a legal test and so wrongly excluded
evidence, this has gone far beyond preliminary procedure, and a new judge must hear the retrial.¹
If the appellant wants a fresh judge, to resume the sentencing hearing, the appellant may have
to apply to the existing judge to withdraw.²
If the trial court stayed the proceeding, which stay the
Court of Appeal reversed, the trial had started, but no evidence had been heard. The case should
be tried by the ﬁrst judge available, whether or not it was the same judge.³
And if the Court of Appeal sends a matter back to an administrative tribunal to rehear some
things, there is no ﬁxed rule whether there should be a fresh panel; some of the factors to consider
(in light of conﬂicting objectives) are listed.⁴
If the Court of Appeal remits the matter to the “trial court”, that does not mean the same trial
judge, and if he has retired, another can act.⁵
Where the law changed pending appeal, a case
management judge’s decision on a class action could not stand, but he was better suited to rehear
and decide the question than the Court of Appeal, who sent it back to rehear therefore.⁶
But if the Court of Appeal sends the matter back to the same trial judge or tribunal panel, bias
of that person or persons cannot be presumed, and must be raised at the earliest chance.⁷

Subdivision 4
Judgments and Orders
Preparation and signature of judgments and orders
14.77(1) Unless otherwise directed, Part 9 [Judgments and Orders], Division 1
[Preparation and Entry of Judgments and Orders] applies to the preparation and
entry of judgments and orders of the Court of Appeal.
(2) The Court of Appeal may authorize a single appeal judge or case management
ofﬁcer to settle the form of any order or judgment.
(3) A judgment or order may be signed by the judge or the panel who granted
it or by the Registrar. [Alta. Reg.41/14;61/23]

Related Provisions
9.1 and 9.2 (preparation of judgments) ; 14.38(2) (d) (further directions after appeal)

Orders were not entered within the three months set by Rr.9.5 and 14.77, despite several
requests from the opposite party to the appellant. The case management ofﬁcer properly extended
the time to enter them. Oversight in not telling the appellant about the request to extend time for
entry, did no harm.⁴
Settling the minutes of the order is not a time to reargue the merits, nor to seek substantive
changes, still less to engage in debating irrelevancies, which steps which may lead to personal
costs.⁵
It is not fair for a party who lost to negotiate an altered formal order, and then after its entry
to spring on the respondent an unexpected and late appeal.⁶
One judge can approve the form of an order granted by another judge.⁷
The 2023 amendment
to this Rule allows the Court of Appeal to delegate setting the form of an order or judgment to a
case management ofﬁcer. That is useful as they are lawyers, but work closely with the Registries.

14-247
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.78(1)
It is implied that a signature approving a form of formal judgment may be given in an electronic
form, and the judgment need not recite that. Only afﬁdavits need original manual signatures.¹
A suit on a foreign judgment should nevertheless yield a judgment here in Canadian currency.²
Reasons for judgment have two functions. The ﬁrst is to tell the parties and others what the
formal judgment will say. Since a judgment is presumed to take effect at once on pronouncement,
that is important.³
And the details in the Court of Appeal’s formal judgment should refer to the
formal judgment of the trial court being appealed from, not to the reasons given by the trial court
for it.⁴
The second function of reasons for judgment is to explain why the judgment was given,
which is important on appeal. A formal judgment should not try to summarize the reasons, nor
record contingent or obiter ﬁndings; it is for the result alone.⁵
See further Rr.9.2n. C, 14.18(3) n. A,
and 14.75(1) n. C.1(h).
What governs is the formal judgment of the Court of Appeal, not the reasons given for it,
though an ambiguity in the judgment can be resolved by reviewing the reasons. But the reasons
are not themselves the disposition.⁶

and orders] the judgment is effective as if it were a judgment or order of the
court appealed from.
(2) Any interested person may ﬁle a copy of the Court’s judgment in the court
appealed from. [Alta. Reg.41/14]

14-248
R.14.79 PART 14: APPEALS
The Court of Appeal can decide costs in the Court of King's Bench, if there is an appeal.¹
R.
14.78 was used to backdate a maintenance order given by the Court of Appeal.²
And to backdate
interest on a judgment.³
Where the loser at trial pays costs but then wins on appeal, and the costs
paid before are refunded, the refund should have interest added to it.⁴
Once an appeal is over and the ﬁnal Court of Appeal judgment entered, the Court is functus.
Barring exceptional circumstances, it can do no more, especially not give leave to appeal from
one judge’s decision settling the terms of its formal judgment.⁵
But where a formal judgment is approved and entered with both parties knowing that the
losing party is about to apply to have the appeal reheard, indeed the judgment is ﬁled after that
application is, the court is not functus, especially where the winning party did not intend to bar the
motion. The court has inherent power to act where the formal judgment was entered in the face of
an obstacle such as a Rule barring entry, an escrow, a promise not to enter, or a motion to reopen
where no one intends entry to bar that motion. It is also necessary there be no delay or beneﬁt
taken from the judgment or prejudice to any party, and justice favours reopening.⁶
Normally, no one could alter the costs award in an entered formal Court of Appeal judgment,
even if it turned out that (unknown to the panel) a formal offer had been bettered. But it could be
reconsidered in the very unusual circumstances of the consent (at the request) of both parties,
and where some error in wording the order of one justice of appeal in chambers seemed likely,
leading to conﬂicting orders.⁷
On what happens to security for costs once the appeal is decided, see R.14.67n. , supra.
An example of what was then the proper form for a formal Court of Appeal judgment is an
appendix to the Lameman decision.⁸
Many lawyers merely copy the form for an interlocutory
order in King's Bench. Certainly that type of style of cause is appropriate now: see 2014 Form
AP-6 (v.2, pg.1-323 of this book), which goes with R.14.87. What is the proper form for the body
of a formal judgment, and what is compulsory, may not be clear now.
Usually on ordering a new trial, performance of the judgment from the ﬁrst trial should be set
aside, but in special circumstances with likely relief on the next trial to the plaintiffs who won the
ﬁrst time, the plaintiffs could temporarily keep half.⁹
On when interest on an increased award runs
from, see R.14.80, and the Swaren case, R.14.80n.
Supreme Court of Canada judgments
14.79 The Registrar must
(a) enter any judgment granted by the Supreme Court of Canada on the
ﬁle of the Court of Appeal, showing the date of entry, and
(b) send a copy of the Supreme Court judgment to the clerk of the court
appealed from for ﬁling in that court, and the judgment may be acted
on as if it were a judgment of that court. [Alta. Reg.41/14]

1 MacCabe v. Westlock R. C. Sep. S. D. (#2) 2002 ABCA 307, 320 AR 194.

3 See R.520n. , supra.

14-249
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.80

Interest on judgments
14.80 If a decision awarding a sum of money is reversed or varied, interest is
payable on the amount of the appeal judgment from the date that the decision
under appeal was pronounced.¹
[Alta. Reg.41/14]

Rule 14.80 applies to trial costs which the Court of Appeal greatly increases, given also R.
14.79.²
The Supreme Court of Canada can vary its judgment before entry, to do what the trial court
should have done, such as amending the judgment to award interest from trial, where the Supreme
Court greatly increased the damages.³
If the successful appellant’s factum did not ask for that, she
may not get costs.⁴
Where a plaintiff is paid damages, but then on appeal the amount of damages is reduced or
liability removed, the (excess) money has to be repaid. For purposes of interest, that is money
payable under a judgment debt, with interest running from the date of the trial judgment, not from
the appellate judgment.⁵
If the trial judgment sets a different date to start interest running, and the Court of Appeal does
not change that date and merely increases the award, interest on the difference also runs from
that date.⁶
The Judgment Interest Act deﬁnes “judgment debt” as a sum made payable by or under a
judgment in a civil proceeding, which is not true of a compensation order by a tribunal. And the
more specific provisions under the Surface Rights Act take precedence over the more general
provisions of the Judgment Interest Act. So the amount would be the statutory rate applied to the
modified compensation substituted by the court.⁷

Footnote
1 Similar to 1914 R.327 and 1944 C. R.602, and 1968 R.520, though the reference to varying a judgment was
new in 1968. The Rule may stem from Reg. Gen. T. T.1867. Cf. (Imp. ) 3 & 4 Wm.4 c.42 s.39.
2 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 76, Part T.1(f).
3 W. C. B. v. Greer [1975] 1 SCR 359, 1 NR 599, 602-03. Similar is Kim Est. (Stewart) v. Routhier [1975] 1
SCR 588, 1 NR 630.
4 W. C. B. v. Greer, supra.
5 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 76, Part T.1(d).
6 Swaren v. Swaren (#2) 2009 ABCA 156, [2009] 6 WWR 614.
7 Pentelechuk v. Grand Rapids P. L. (#2) 2024 ABKB 283, JCE 2103 06839 (May 17) (¶’s 12-15).

The Crown tried to appeal a summary conviction acquittal and the lawyer for the accused
refused to accept service, saying that he was no longer acting. The Crown got ex parte orders to
extend time and get substitutional service. A judge set these orders aside, as the afﬁdavits used
did not satisfy R.11.28: no statement of why service was impractical, or of what method would
work. The service was incurably bad because in the meantime the orders had been set aside. The
civil appeal Rules (about the Court of Appeal) do not apply (though the Criminal Appeal Rules
do).¹
Service of an appeal on the agent of a party would sufﬁce, but the fact that in the tribunal
below the Director advanced the complainant's arguments, does not make the Director the
complainant's agent. The director is not like counsel.²
Time was not extended where the applicant said he had been away for some time and no one
checked for mail or deliveries at the address for service which he had several times given.³

14-253
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.86

Related Provisions
1.5(4) (non-compliant materials) ; 13.5 and 14.2(3) (extending time) ; 14.17(2) and 14.18(2)

(documents unavailable at time of filing) ; 14.25(4) (varying format of factum) ; 14.31 (powers
of case management officer); 14.65 (restoring appeals) ; 14.73 (procedural powers)
There is an extended discussion of amending a notice of appeal, with much citation of
authority.¹
One Justice of Appeal alone can give permission to amend a notice of appeal.²
Not every omission of a mandatory topic or recital in a notice of appeal is fatal.³
Even a notice
of appeal misstating the court and the judge may be curable, where the other details were correct
and the party on the other side could not have been misled.⁴
Maybe the Registrar should err on the side of acceptance, in case of doubt about a document
tendered for ﬁling. In any event, the Registrar's having ﬁled it has little weight, if later the document’s
validity is disputed.⁵
An appellant can amend his notice of appeal by jettisoning part of what is under appeal, or
abandoning some of the relief initially claimed.⁶
Where a new precedent suggests a fundamental
change in the law and an appeal is pending but not yet heard, the appellant should amend the
grounds in his notice of criminal appeal to raise this point of law, which probably could not have
been foreseen at trial.⁷
But a civil notice of appeal does not need grounds of appeal.
An appellant was not allowed to amend the notice of appeal greatly to expand the appeal,
either to challenge a court decision 5½ years old and never appealed before, nor to raise a very
vague “constitutional challenge” not even naming which statute was attacked, and with no real
notice to either Attorney General.⁸
The appellant cannot expand the scope of the appeal by amendment. If the notice identiﬁes
certain parts of the judgment as being appealed, that implies that the rest are not; an amendment
cannot later add other parts. An application to appeal late cannot masquerade as an application
to amend an existing appeal. The only exception is later appealing the later costs order ﬂowing out
of the substantive decision already appealed.⁹
But an appellant who does not expand the parts of
the judgment which he is appealing can amend his appeal to add more relief not claimed in the
original notice of appeal.¹⁰

Dec 10) (¶ 10).
3 Easy Loan v. Base Mtge. , supra (¶’s 38-41).
4 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 76, Part M.3. But one cannot amend to cover a second separate order: Gramaglia
v. Alta. Govt. Serv. Min.2006 ABCA 61, leave den (SCC 1 Jun ’06).

JA); cf. dicta in Easy Loan v. Base Mtge. , supra.

appealed from,

(f) [Repealed Alta. Reg.61/23]

Practice Direction — Electronic Filing, and
(i) include an electronic bookmark to
(i) the cover page, listing the name of the document, the name
of the party ﬁling the document, the appeal number and a
brief style of cause,
(ii) each heading and subheading of the document as listed in
the Table of Contents, and
(iii) the ﬁrst page of each tab, exhibit or attachment, listing a
description of the tab, exhibit or attachment.
(2) The Appeal Record, factums, Extracts of Key Evidence and Books of
Authorities must have a cover page in Form AP 5 that include the name of the
Court of Appeal, the location of the ofﬁce of the Registrar of the Court of Appeal
and the appeal number assigned by the Registrar. [Alta. Reg.41/14;61/23]

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.88(1)

Subdivision 4
Costs of Appeals
Cost awards
14.88(1) Unless otherwise ordered, the successful party in an appeal or an
application is entitled to a costs award against the unsuccessful party.
(2) The provisions of Part 10 [Lawyer’s Charges, Recoverable Costs of Litigation,
and Sanctions], Division 2 [Recoverable Costs of Litigation] and Schedule C
apply to appeals.
(3) Unless otherwise ordered, the scale of costs in an appeal shall be the same
as the scale that applies to the order or judgment appealed from.
[Alta. Reg.41/14]
Information Note
Reasons for judgment of the Court of Appeal will not make any specific direction about costs
unless an exception is to be made to the presumption in rule 14.88(1) that the successful
party is entitled to costs of the appeal. Any request for a specific direction as to costs must
be made within two months of the pronouncement of the decision.

Related Provisions
10.50 (costs against lawyer) ; 14.59 (formal offers) ; 14.66 (discontinuance) ; 14.67 (security for

costs); 14.90(1) (no costs for non-compliant steps) ; 14.90(2) (costs of struck or abandoned
appeals)
A. Scale of Costs
1. Generally
(a) References
See also the notes to R.10.29 and Schedule C.
(b) Costs in Both Courts
The Court of Appeal can consider and award costs both in the Court of Appeal and the Court
of King's Bench, especially if the Court of Appeal has completely reversed the decision in the
Court of King's Bench.¹
Where a decision of the Court of King's Bench has been entirely reversed

1 Humphreys v. Trebilcock (#2) 2017 ABCA 232, [2017] 11 WWR 382, leave den (SCC 14 Dec ’17) ; 864503
Alta. v. Genco Place Props.2019 ABCA 248, 94 Alta LR(6th) 298 (Jun 13) (¶ 8). But it maybe otherwise if
the Court of Appeal sends the matter back to the trial court to assess damages: IFP Technologies (Can. ) v.
EnCana Midstream & Mkt. (#2) 2017 ABCA 269, [2018] 1 WWR 116 (Aug 16) (¶ 8).

14-256
R.14.88(3) PART 14: APPEALS
on appeal, the basis for the award of costs at trial disappears, and the Court of Appeal can revisit
the matter of trial costs, even if there was no error as to costs themselves by the trial judge.¹
A mere request in the appellant's factum to allow the appeal "with costs" sufﬁces,²
though it is
preferable to say that in listing relief. But even if costs in the Court of King's Bench have not yet
been decided, it may be better for the Court of Appeal to decide only costs of the appeal, and
let the trial judge or case management judge in King's Bench familiar with the nuances of the
proceedings in King's Bench, decide the costs in King's Bench.³
When allowing an appeal, the Court of Appeal can give the appellant costs of the matter
appealed from and for which the King's Bench judge had given the appellant no costs, even after
judgment is entered. Rule 14.88 is no barrier, as it only has to do with scale of costs, not who gets
the costs.⁴
(c) Balancing Factors
A costs award is not a precise scientiﬁc calculation, but rather a balance of a number of
considerations.⁵
The amount of costs need not be set by Schedule C or its columns; some of the factors to
assess are reviewed.⁶
(d) Scale or Column
(i) Same Column as at Trial
The usual practice of the Court of Appeal is to follow R.14.88(3) and give costs on the same
scale as at trial. The Court of Appeal should depart from that only for some deﬁnite reason.⁷
That
means a multiple of Column 5, if that is what the trial judge awarded (or if that is the column for the
amount the appeal court later varied the trial award to). If the Court of Appeal says nothing about
costs, then the King's Bench scale, even if it is solicitor-client costs, applies.⁸
There are difﬁculties
in calculating the column of costs on an appeal where there has not yet been any assessment of
damages at trial.⁹
Appeal costs are based on the King's Bench costs’ column, not on the amount
in issue in the appeal.¹⁰

14-257
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.88(3)
So if the trial judge gave costs between the amounts in columns x and y, the Court of Appeal
may give appellate costs, between those same two columns.¹
Rule 14.88(3) on using the costs column below does not mean automatic doubling of costs,
where there was trial-level misconduct leading to that, but no such bad enough conduct in the
Court of Appeal. If the potential value of the estate litigated over is large, the column 5 of the trial
court may also be used in the Court of Appeal. But if there is a separate costs appeal, the amount
of the costs awarded at trial sets the appropriate column in the Court of Appeal for that appeal
(here col.1).²
Ordinarily the column of costs is set by the amount of the ﬁnal judgment (as amended on
appeal), ignoring interest running after the cCurt of Appeal judgment.³
On an interlocutory appeal,
the general amount in issue in the suit can be looked at to set the column.⁴
If the appeal is
interlocutory, one does not guess who will win the main suit, and costs may be based on the
amount claimed, under the general costs Rule in that regard.⁵
Where there were lump sum costs
awarded in the Court of King's Bench, a lump sum for costs was also granted in the Court of
Appeal.⁶
In a dispute about costs of an appeal struck for failure to comply with the Rules (not ﬁling
the appeal record, here), one cannot use the presumption of the same scale of costs as in the
court below; the judge below just gave a lump-sum costs award. Usually Schedule C is used for
appeals.⁷
(ii) Court of Appeal Chooses Different Column
How much the winning party has spent is not the only relevant issue. How much of that the
losing party should pay is also important. Harsh, long, complex litigation is fairly common in family
law matters. In such suits, often more time and money is spent by one or both sides, than is worth
it for the sums and topics in issue. So the winner has the automatic right to some substantial
fraction of his or her total expenses in the suit, and the proper costs may not be a fraction of actual
expenses, but some level in Schedule C, or Schedule C with some increase.⁸
Costs are discretionary, so the Court of Appeal can go above the column applicable to the
amount of the trial judgment, if the losing appellant’s appeal required the Court of Appeal to pick
through thousands of pages of written evidence extracts and transcripts of trial testimony, and
where the amounts claimed in the appellant’s factum were large and unsubstantiated.⁹

14-258
R.14.88(3) PART 14: APPEALS
In determining which level or column of costs should be awarded on appeals, the Court of
Appeal may also consider the following:
(a) The complexity of the appeals;¹
(b) The efﬁciencies achieved by having multiple appeals argued together;²
(c) Whether any offers were made, even if those offers do not trigger double costs;
(d) Abuses of process or other misconduct, including attempting to introduce fresh evidence
on appeal without permission;³
or failing to disclose bank accounts;⁴
(e) importance of the issues on appeal.⁵
If no amount is in issue (e. g. on judicial review) the default mode is column 1, which the court
may or may not raise just because the facts are complex, even if larger amounts are indirectly at
stake.⁶
But whether a matter is fast-tracked or not, is a substantive decision of importance and
entitled to $2, 500 costs. Subsidiary questions (such as extending the number of pages in the
factum and the time for oral arguments) could have been decided by the case management ofﬁcer
and those aspects should not affect the costs.⁷
(iii) Amount Indirectly in Issue
On appeal from a tribunal, one should be careful about concluding that there is no monetary
amount. Where large monetary penalties were at stake, as well as professional reputations, and
the matter was complex, Column 5 was used.⁸
Even though no amount was directly in issue in an
appeal from the utilities regulator, where indirectly large amounts were, and the matter was very
complex and the judgments lengthy, and each party offered separate different arguments, costs
were ﬁxed on Column 5.⁹
In a planning matter where taxes or charges in question were over $1
million and issues were complex, column 4 was used.¹⁰
(iv) Miscellaneous
Semble, R.14.88 does not bar the Court of Appeal from setting different columns for different
co-defendants, e. g. , where accusations are made against some and not others. Large lump-sum

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.88(3)
costs in King's Bench for misconduct do not justify higher Court of Appeal costs than usual, if the
losing party ran the appeal properly.¹
After the columns and amounts in Schedule C have changed by a Rule amendment, one does
not simply carry over the same column number used for trial costs, if the new columns refer to
different amounts. One uses the new column appropriate to the same amounts as applied to the
column previously selected in the lower court.²
(e) Inﬂation
As Schedule C was over 20 years old, it was proper for the Court to multiply the amounts in the
column which would otherwise apply.³
But ﬁnally a new Schedule C was enacted early in 2020,
albeit one calculated and drafted some years before.
(f) Overlap With Trial Work
One cannot simply say that all the research had been done for trial and so the labour for the
appeal would be slight. Even if someone had been able from memory to write a factum, a great
deal of checking would be necessary, even if no new issues or arguments came up. And to get
ready for oral hearing of an appeal, counsel have to become thoroughly familiar with the record.
Counsel cannot simply ignore appellate arguments by the other side, even if it thinks them to be
unfounded.⁴
(g) Miscellaneous
Applications for speciﬁc directions as to costs must be made within three months of the decision
under R.14.88.⁵
The presumption there can be varied within that time, even if the judgment has
been entered.⁶
On extending the time limit, R.14.88 is somewhat unusual.⁷
Almost a year after the
Court of Appeal gave its reasons, one counsel asked to argue costs. In the meantime, one of the
judges on the original panel had retired. The remaining two held that it was too late to entertain
the application.⁸
There were too many requests in Calgary to rehear decided appeals, and an ill-founded
request attracted costs of at least $14, 000.00.⁹
$750 party-party costs for even a medium-length one-Justice application for permission to
appeal is too low. A lump sum is better for the fee. Ordinarily it could be a little more, but for special
circumstances here, $1, 300 was chosen.¹⁰

R.14.88(3) PART 14: APPEALS
Where a contract calls for costs on a solicitor-client basis, those are often recoverable on
appeal, despite
• the trial judge’s costs award on a different basis (and R.  14.88(3) ’s presumption), 
especially if the Court of Appeal reversed the trial judge;
• onepartysayingtotheotherthathehadanexcusefornotperforming;or
• a breach of contract,  even accepted repudiation,  as many contractual clauses are
expressly about events after breach.¹
2. Extra Work
Where timing required 2 separate appeals (on merits and on costs), but the parties each ﬁled
a common factum for the 2 appeals, the successful appellant got an extra fee for a second notice
of appeal, and disbursements for some separate steps, but otherwise no second set of costs.²
The
factum fee was doubled where the court had called for and allowed a good deal of extra written
argument after oral argument.³
An appellant claimed alternately against two separate parties who required separate lawyers,
so when the appeal failed, each got a separate set of costs.⁴
Where there were four separate
appellants, each with different arguments and full length factums, the respondents were entitled to
collect in their costs four different items for preparation of factum.⁵
Where 2 closely-related appeals
were heard together and all consented to let each party argue both appeals fully, the losing parties
were jointly and severally all liable for the costs of both appeals.⁶
Where the respondents moved
to strike parts of the appellant’s factums, though there was no separate hearing, the respondents
had to ﬁle separate materials and succeeded, so they got 50% of the usual costs of a contested
application added onto their other costs. Those would be payable by each of the appellants and
each of the respondents would get that item of costs added.⁷
But where two appeals are run all as one and succeed, the winner should get only one set of
costs, not a separate set against each respondent who lost.⁸
That happens frequently. A fortiori

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.88(3)

parallel (interlocutory) appeal, where a case management ofﬁcer directed that it be brought.¹
If the appeal required leave to appeal, and it was granted but costs of that motion were not
then decided, a later panel can give costs of the leave application.²

B. Who Gets Costs of Appeals³
1. Results of Appeal
The Alberta Court of Appeal normally gives costs of an appeal to the winner of the appeal,⁴
especially if the trial judge’s errors were mostly induced by the losing party’s counsel.⁵
And even
more so if the appeal had some basic jurisdictional ﬂaw pointed out.⁶
There has to be some real
principled rational reason not to give costs to the winner.⁷
Special topics such as custody are not exempt from that general winner-costs rule.⁸
The party winning the appeal⁹
gets full costs of the appeal if he wins fully (e. g. the judgment
against it is set aside), even if some of that party’s arguments are rejected.¹⁰
Costs to the winner
may occasionally lead to assessing costs issue by issue,¹¹
but the courts are usually reluctant to
divide costs that way.¹²
Especially if the respondent neither narrowed nor enhanced its position.¹³

14-262
R.14.88(3) PART 14: APPEALS
A small recovery may be grounds for no costs.¹
A split result may lead to no fee costs, and
only a sharing of the disbursement for the appeal records,²
or a modest costs award.³
Costs
should be awarded to the winner of an appeal of an interlocutory application where the issues
are independent and separate from the ultimate question of the lawsuit.⁴
However, where the
appellant gets permission to appeal and then loses the appeal, the two proceedings are regarded
as integral, the leave permission being ancillary to the appeal, so the losing appellant gets no
offsetting costs of the permission application in its favor.⁵
Often a cross-appeal involves much the same issues as the main appeal, and then the Court
of Appeal may treat it all as one appeal, with no separate costs for the cross-appeal.⁶
It is very rare that the winning party, even the government, will be required to pay the costs
of the losing party.⁷
But where a child-protection (welfare) case goes to the Court of Appeal as
a test case, and the Director wants the test case, and the other parties would ﬁnd the costs of
appeal signiﬁcant, the Court of Appeal may order the Director to pay their actual legal fees and
disbursements (or those which Legal Aid advanced for them).⁸
In deciding who gets costs of interlocutory steps in an appeal, the court should rely on the face
of orders and Notices of Motion to decide who won such steps; and if it is not clear, no one may
get them. If they were necessary housekeeping for the appeal, the winning appellant may get a
modest fee for them.⁹
On Court of King's Bench Costs where the Court of Appeal reversed the King's Bench
judgment, see subpart A.1(b), supra.
On what are not grounds to deprive the winner of costs, see subpart 3 below.
2. Misconduct
The court may make an exception to the usual rule that the winner gets costs, and reduce
or eliminate the costs payable to the winner, where the winner was guilty of some neglect or

R.14.88(3) PART 14: APPEALS
But appellate costs after a properly-conducted appeal should not be increased because of
misconduct in the court appealed from.¹
Repeated re-litigation by parties without lawyers led to a lump sum costs award.²
Unrepresented
litigants who made long unfocussed and largely irrelevant arguments, with unfounded allegations
of misconduct, and lost, had to pay fairly heavy lump-sum costs.³
One party’s factum was
intemperate, and made factual allegations based on evidence not in the appeal record and if
anywhere, unilaterally put into a book of authorities. Though that party was nominally a respondent
and had supported the order below, it had now swung around and on appeal supported the
appellant. That party became liable along with the appellant for the costs of the failed appeal.⁴
That the losing appellant’s arguments in the Court of Appeal largely duplicated those made
in the Court of King's Bench, is no ground to make him pay solicitor-client costs, if there were
arguable points in issue.⁵
Solicitor-client costs should not be awarded against someone not a
party to a question which calls for such costs?⁶
Usually someone who moves for contempt should get at least enhanced costs from the
contemnor, even if the contemnor wins some other issues on appeal. Solicitor-client costs in the
trial court should stand, and R.14.88(3) presumes that Court of Appeal costs should be on the
same basis.⁷
See also R.10.53n. C.
3. Irrelevant Considerations
The losing party’s lack of funds is not a ground to shield him or her from paying costs.⁸
Hardship
is not a ground to change the usual rule.⁹
Such costs are usually ordered even if the case is difﬁcult or controversial and the law is
unsettled;¹⁰
or the Court of Appeal has some uncertainty as to whether the binding authority is
just;¹¹
or a new trial is ordered.¹²
The presumption that the winner gets costs of an appeal does
not end because the point before the court is novel.¹³
The losing litigant may argue that he was

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.88(3)
just trying to settle the law to help the public, but that argument against costs rarely succeeds in
Alberta, especially when some individual ﬁnancial interest (such as business competition with the
opposite party) exists.¹
In a ﬁght between private persons or businesses, signiﬁcant legal issues
are not unusual, and are no reason to deprive the winner of his or her costs.²
It is also irrelevant to costs of an appeal that the winning party had been guilty of some delay
in the suit.³
Out-of-town counsel may not get their travel costs as disbursements.⁴
Modest travel costs for
Edmonton counsel going to Calgary may be allowed.⁵
4. Implied (Default) Mode
See also subpart A.1(g), supra.
That the winner of the appeal gets costs of the appeal in any event is implied when the Court
of Appeal pronounces judgment, unless the panel says the contrary.⁶
If a party objects to applying
that presumption that costs follow the event, he or she should argue that in his or her factum and
remind the court of that during oral argument. The loser may not get leave to re-argue costs if that
is not done.⁷
Because of the presumption in R.14.88, often the Court of Appeal makes no speciﬁc direction
about costs, any request for a more speciﬁc direction should be made to the court within two
months.⁸
Case law says that that two-month period can be extended by the Court.⁹
Eight months
is too long to wait to ask to change the presumption that the winner gets costs of the appeal.¹⁰
Even if leave is being sought in the Supreme Court of Canada.¹¹
One cannot raise that topic with
the assessment ofﬁcer, nor on appeal from that ofﬁcer.¹²
Because of the presumption, if nothing is said when an appeal or application is decided by
a panel or one justice of appeal, the winning party can put into the formal order or judgment a
clause making the presumption express.¹³
Any time limit for giving costs awards (incorporated by
R.14.88(2) ) applies to someone seeking a different costs award, not to a party relying upon the
presumption.¹⁴

14-266
R.14.88(3) PART 14: APPEALS
This presumption that the winner gets costs, also applies to interlocutory applications decided
by one justice of appeal who says nothing about costs.¹
And in such circumstances, it is proper for
the parties to wait for the conclusion of the appeal and of related appeals, and then argue all the
costs in the Court of Appeal at one time.²
On an interlocutory appeal when the Court of Appeal says that costs follow the event, that
means that the winner of the appeal gets them permanently. It does not mean that they depend in
any way on the later outcome of the lawsuit.³
5. Interveners and Tribunals
Only rarely should the Attorney General pay costs where he or she intervened in an appeal
under statutory power and in the public interest, and where the person seeking costs challenged
a statute’s constitutionality.⁴
Where constitutional litigation is almost inevitable, public interest
sometimes lets the court soften the costs burden for the loser.⁵
An intervener ﬁled a long afﬁdavit, much of it argument, and so thought it got around the usual
page-limit for written argument, and most was on issues on which leave was not granted, so that
intervener had to pay some costs.⁶
The statutory bar against costs against individual members of a Subdivision and Development
Appeal Board does not bar costs against the Board itself, if it submits arguments on the merits.⁷
Where litigation began in an administrative tribunal, the costs cannot include those hearings, but
can take into account the history below.⁸
If no other party appears to oppose an administrative appeal and the tribunal whose decision
is in question speaks to the standard of review and comments on the legal issues, but takes
no position on the merits of the argument, that does not make it liable for costs if the appeal
succeeds. The general rule is that the tribunal neither receives nor pays costs.⁹
But if the tribunal
goes further, when another party also opposes the appeal, here is a risk of costs against the
intervening tribunal.¹⁰
6. Extra Parties or Work
See Part A.2 above.

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.88(3)
7. Level of Costs
See Part A, above.
8. Miscellaneous
Rule 10.30(1) allows costs be awarded or set after judgment has been entered, thus modifying
the functus officio rule.¹
The two-month limit in the Information Note to R.14.88 on getting costs
directions after a substantive decision, can be extended under R.13.5(2).²
Rule 13.5 may not
apply, as R.14.88 itself does not name a time limit?³
The Court of Appeal will not give a party his or her costs of the appeal out of the estate, even
though that party may later get probate, if he or she did not seek that in the court appealed from.⁴
A party respondent has to have his counsel gown and attend. He did not start the appeal,
cannot discontinue it, and unexpected things can occur in court, even if the matter now seems
moot. So full costs were given to a party attending by counsel up to the event said to make this
matter moot for him. After that, he got only a fraction of the usual costs.⁵
Appellate costs are also presumed to be paid to the winner forthwith.⁶
Costs of a stay application
are especially suited to prompt decisions and costs payable at once.⁷
On some costs given for an unnecessary appeal because the case management ofﬁcer
directed that it be brought, see the Anglin case.⁸
Where a reference to ﬁx damages is ordered on appeal, the costs may be conditional on
whether the damages exceed the amount which was paid into court (offered) by the defendant.⁹
Normally, no one could alter the costs award in an entered formal court of appeal judgment,
even if it turned out that (unknown to the panel) a formal offer had been bettered. But it could be
reconsidered in very unusual circumstances: consent (or request) of both parties, and some error
in wording the order in chambers seemed likely, leading to conﬂicting orders.¹⁰
Where a letter of credit was put up as security pending appeal to release seizure, the winner
may get as costs the expense of the letter of credit.¹¹
One cannot appeal direct to the Court of Appeal from a decision of a King's Bench assessment
ofﬁcer; one must ﬁrst appeal to a justice of the Court of King's Bench.¹²
If trial costs by agreement are put into trust to be paid out in accordance with the Court of
Appeal decision, the party losing cannot force a delay in payment pending attempts to appeal to
the Supreme Court of Canada.¹³

R.14.89(1) PART 14: APPEALS
The history of appellate costs in the Northwest Territories, and why Alberta’s Schedule C was
used, is sketched in the Fullowka case.¹
Subdivision 5
Fees on Appeal
Fees and allowances
14.89(1) In every appeal there must be paid to the Registrar or other appropriate
person, at the time of ﬁling, the fees speciﬁed in Schedule B, unless the
Registrar waives the fee, in whole or in part, in accordance with guidelines, if
any, established or adopted by the Minister of Justice for persons unable to pay
fees.
(2) Part 13 [Technical Rules], Division 5 [Payment of Fees and Allowances, and
Waivers of Fees] applies to appeals. [Alta. Reg.41/14;216/22]

Related Provisions
13.32 (fees)

Subdivision 6
Sanctions
Sanctions
14.90(1) In addition to the sanctions set out in Part 10 [Lawyer’s Charges,
Recoverable Costs of Litigation, and Sanctions], Division 4 [Sanctions],
(a) unless otherwise ordered, a party is not entitled to assess costs or
recover disbursements in respect of a procedural step in which the
party has
(i) failed to comply with a deadline set out in this Part,
(ii) ﬁled a document that fails to comply in a substantial respect
with the requirements of these rules, or
(iii) ﬁled a document that is carelessly or inadequately prepared
or that contains illegible material or text;
(b) in the case of any non-compliance with a rule or a direction or order,
a single appeal judge or a panel of the Court of Appeal may strike
from the record any document, including a notice of appeal or cross
appeal, or provide directions for the management of the appeal.
(2) Where an appeal has been struck by operation of these rules or the provisions
of any order or because of the failure of any party to appear when required, or
has been deemed to have been struck or abandoned, the respondent is entitled
to a costs award for having responded to the appeal.

PART 14: APPEALS R.14.90(3)

respondent, rules
Related Provisions
10.49 (penalty for non-compliance) ; 10.52 (contempt) ; 14.32(3) and 14.33(4) (failure to

appear); 14.32(3) (b) and 14.49 (no oral argument on default) ; 14.62 (delay) ; 14.64 (missing
deadlines); 14.74 (striking appeals) ; 14.88 (costs)
A successful respondent got no costs of the appeal because she ﬁled her factum late, applying
R.14.90(1) (a) (i).¹
The Supreme Court of Canada’s individual judges have power to give bail pending appeal to
the Supreme Court of Canada from contempt, even civil contempt.²
An appeal was not ground for
release from jail for contempt pending appeal, where there still was a serious ﬂight risk.³
On very partisan and sloppy practices by a tribunal taking part in an appeal from its decision,
see the Optimax case.⁴
Where an unappealed order found the plaintiff medically incompetent so she must have a
litigation representative, a later notice of appeal by the plaintiff in person with no counsel or
litigation representative, does not comply with a Rule or direction or order, under R.14.90(1) (b).
That Rule lets a justice strike the notice of appeal. Any further document could be ﬁled only after the
Court of King's Bench appointed a particular individual as the plaintiff’s litigation representative.⁵
Someone convicted of civil contempt and sentenced to jail was appealing both conviction
and penalty. Pending appeal, he was given a stay under R.14.90(3). The appellant would suffer
irreparable harm if jailed for a month or so and then his appeal succeeded. The appeal would be
heard quickly.⁶
Filing (sometimes late) unnecessary largely irrelevant (and even unauthorized or forbidden)
very prolix documents led to denial of costs to the parties doing so.⁷
The Court of Appeal’s adoption of electronic ﬁling does not relieve parties of their duties to
read and follow the relevant Rules of Court and Court of Appeal Practice Directions.⁸

R.14.91(1) PART 14: APPEALS
Subdivision 7
The Registrar
Duties of a Registrar
14.91(1) A Registrar must perform the duties required by these rules and the
Court of Appeal, including, subject to any direction of the Court,
(a) establishing and maintaining a court ﬁle for each appeal,
(b) establishing and maintaining lists of appeals, including the Civil
Appeal Hearing List,
(c) receiving and ﬁling all appeal materials, having control and custody
of them, and distributing them to the judges as required,
(d) attending in court as required and keeping a record of all proceedings
before the Court and its judges, including
(i) keeping records of particulars of the appeals heard and the
judges in attendance,
(ii) identifying the appearing parties and their counsel,
(iii) keeping records of particulars of the appeal heard and the
result of the appeal, and
(iv) noting the time occupied in hearing,
(e) keeping proper accounts of money and property received or
dispersed,
(f) settling and signing judgments and orders in accordance with these
rules, and
(g) at the conclusion of an appeal, returning to the court appealed from
any records of that court.
(2) In the absence of a Registrar, a Deputy Registrar or another person appointed
by the Court may act in place of the Registrar. [Alta. Reg.41/14;61/23]

rules, Unscheduled Civil Appeals List
Related Provisions
13.44 (duties of clerk) ; 14.2(4) (a) (“clerk” includes Registrar) ; 14.8(5), (6) (place of filing;

14.85 (filing of documents)
The 2023 amendment removes any mention of an Unscheduled Civil Appeals List, as the case
management ofﬁcers now handle such cases individually.
Self-represented parties or counsel on an appeal matter can electronically access online
documents (ﬁled by anyone) and information about their own case. Non-parties wanting to see
such things must request that from the Registry.

14-271
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.92
Authority of the Registrar
14.92 The Registrar may, with or without consulting with a case management
ofﬁcer or a judge,
(a) where any document presented for ﬁling is irregular, not readily
legible or otherwise carelessly or inadequately prepared,
(i) accept the document for ﬁling, with or without advising the
party presenting the document of the deﬁciency,
(ii) accept the document for ﬁling and note the deﬁciency on the
face of the document,
(iii) accept the document for ﬁling on terms, directions or
undertakings to be agreed to by the ﬁling party, or
(iv) in the case of a signiﬁcant deﬁciency that prevents the
Registrar from ﬁling the document, or that is likely to prejudice
a party or interfere with the disposition of the appeal, reject
the document;
(b) refuse to accept a document for ﬁling, or to perform any other ofﬁcial
act, where the instructions to the Registrar are not clear;
(c) endorse the court record to show a document as having been ﬁled
on the date when the document was ﬁrst tendered for ﬁling;
(d) subject to any conditions that the Registrar may specify, require
personal attendance in an ofﬁce of the Registrar of the Court of
Appeal by a party ﬁling any appeal materials, or accept documents
for ﬁling by mail;
(e) bring to the attention of the Court for summary determination any
appeal that the Registrar determines is frivolous or vexatious or
signiﬁcantly irregular, or that can otherwise be determined on a
summary basis. [Alta. Reg.41/14;61/23]

Related Provisions
13.40 (delegation) ; 13.41 (authority of clerk) ; 14.2(4) (a) (“clerk” includes Registrar)

A. Control of Record
Ordinarily all documents ﬁled in the Court of Appeal must be ﬁled electronically, not in hard
copy. (In exceptional circumstances, a case management ofﬁcer may allow ﬁling hard copies. )
The Rules of Court and the Court’s Consolidated Practice Direction (Part F) were amended
accordingly. Often a copy of the document must be served on other parties, and it is the ﬁled
version which is to be served. Concepts like multiple copies, or backwards-facing pages, are gone
now. But colored covers of documents remain. Page numbering must start with the cover as page
1. There are formatting instructions in the Court of Appeal Management Systems (v.2, p.2-10),
and in the Consolidated Practice Direction.

14-272
R.14.92 PART 14: APPEALS
Someone not used to this system would be well advised to get advice or help, or arrange to
submit a draft or test version, to see if it is acceptable, all well before any ﬁling deadline. Again
the two case management ofﬁcers would be a great help in such situations. (Electronic ﬁling of
transcripts of oral evidence has existed for a number of years, and should produce no novelty. )
Part of the 2023 amendment to the Rule removes the need to put the date of ﬁling on a
document; it merely goes into “the court record”. If a document is compliant and received
electronically by 4: 30 p. m. on a business day, the Registry will mark it as filed on that date. The
Registry tells a party filing, electronically, when the document is received (uploaded) and when it
is filed. If a proper document is marked with a later filing date, the Case Management officer can
see if that is suitable for correction by the Registry. If a document is served, will the person served
know when it was filed? It must be filed before service.
The appeal record must state who prepared it.¹
On costs for preparing an appeal record in-
house, see Breakey case.²
Pending an application for leave to appeal to the Supreme Court of Canada, a Supreme Court
Justice can order release of one of the trial exhibits for forensic testing.³
A Registrar’s refusal to accept a document for ﬁling is not subject to appeal to a judge?⁴
The
Registrar can refuse to accept appeals that are ﬁled without having the necessary permission to
appeal.⁵
Where an appeal is dismissed for failure to ﬁle an appeal record, a respondent who incurs
expenses may be able to recover costs.⁶
The Court of Appeal complained of Appeal Records slovenly in parts, with poor photocopying.⁷
In practice a number of Alberta factums have ﬂaws in form, but the Registrar does not report
this to the Court, and other parties often fail to raise it on the subject of costs. The Court’s Legal
Counsel often mention the ﬂaws to the justices hearing the appeal.
B. Checklist
Parties are asked to review and rely upon the Mandatory Requirements and Check Return
Forms and the Information Sheets, which are on the Court of Appeal’s website under “Registry
(Filing Information) ”.⁸
A few ofﬁcial forms for appeals are forms AP1 to AP6 (in vol.2 of this book). Civil appellate
forms are no longer found after the Consolidated Practice Directions of the Court of Appeal.

14-273
PART 14: APPEALS R.14.92
C. Striking an Appeal
A Registrar referred a matter to the Court of Appeal under R.14.92(e). An appeal in a suit well
under $25, 000 with no leave, is “signiﬁcantly irregular” under that Rule, even if the appellant now
calls it a claim of $60, 000, especially with no evidence of that. Doubly so if a previous vexatious-
litigant order required leave for the appellant to ﬁle any more papers in court, and had not sought
such permission. The appeal was struck.¹
It is unwise to decide an appeal summarily, even by a panel of three judges, especially deciding
summarily one issue of a pending appeal.²
The Registrar referred to a panel of the Court of Appeal an appeal from a statutory tribunal,
where the legislation did not appear to allow an appeal in those circumstances.³
